Universal Structures in ℂ \mathbb{C} -Linear Enumerative Invariant Theories This paper is a contribution to the Special Issue on Enumerative and Gauge-Theoretic Invariants in honor of Lothar Göttsche on the occasion of his 60th birthday. The full collection is available at https://www.emis.de/journals/SIGMA/Gottsche.html
Jacob GROSS a, Dominic JOYCE a and Yuuji TANAKA b

Abstract
An enumerative invariant theory in algebraic geometry, differential geometry, or representation theory, is the study of invariants which ‘count’ τ \tau -(semi)stable objects E E with fixed topological invariants ⟦ E ⟧ = α \llbracket E\rrbracket=\alpha in some geometric problem, by means of a virtual class [ ℳ α ss ( τ ) ] virt [{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{{\rm ss}}(\tau)]_{{\rm virt}} in some homology theory for the moduli spaces ℳ α st ( τ ) ⊆ ℳ α ss ( τ ) {\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{{\rm st}}(\tau)\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{{\rm ss}}(\tau) of τ \tau -(semi)stable objects. Examples include Mochizuki’s invariants counting coherent sheaves on surfaces, Donaldson–Thomas type invariants counting coherent sheaves on Calabi–Yau 3- and 4-folds and Fano 3-folds, and Donaldson invariants of 4-manifolds. We make conjectures on new universal structures common to many enumerative invariant theories. Any such theory has two moduli spaces ℳ {\mathbin{\cal M}} , ℳ pl {\mathbin{\cal M}}^{{\rm pl}} , where the second author (see https://people.maths.ox.ac.uk/~joyce/hall.pdf ) gives H ∗ ​ ( ℳ ) H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}) the structure of a graded vertex algebra , and H ∗ ( ℳ pl ) H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{{\rm pl}}\big) a graded Lie algebra , closely related to H ∗ ​ ( ℳ ) H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}) . The virtual classes [ ℳ α ss ( τ ) ] virt [{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{{\rm ss}}(\tau)]_{{\rm virt}} take values in H ∗ ( ℳ pl ) H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{{\rm pl}}\big) . In most such theories, defining [ ℳ α ss ( τ ) ] virt [{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{{\rm ss}}(\tau)]_{{\rm virt}} when ℳ α st ( τ ) ≠ ℳ α ss ( τ ) {\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{{\rm st}}(\tau)\neq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{{\rm ss}}(\tau) (in gauge theory, when the moduli space contains reducibles) is a difficult problem. We conjecture that there is a natural way to define invariants [ ℳ α ss ( τ ) ] inv [{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{{\rm ss}}(\tau)]_{\rm inv} in homology over ℚ {\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}} , with [ ℳ α ss ( τ ) ] inv = [ ℳ α ss ( τ ) ] virt [{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{{\rm ss}}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}=[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{{\rm ss}}(\tau)]_{{\rm virt}} when ℳ α st ( τ ) = ℳ α ss ( τ ) {\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{{\rm st}}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{{\rm ss}}(\tau) , and that these invariants satisfy a universal wall-crossing formula under change of stability condition τ \tau , written using the Lie bracket on H ∗ ( ℳ pl ) H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{{\rm pl}}\big) . We prove our conjectures for moduli spaces of representations of quivers without oriented cycles. Versions of our conjectures in algebraic geometry using Behrend–Fantechi virtual classes are proved in the sequel [ arXiv:2111.04694 ].



keywords
invariant; stability condition; vertex algebra; wall crossing formula; quiver

In honour of Lothar Göttsche’s 60
th
birthday

1
Introduction

For us, an
enumerative invariant theory
in algebraic or differential geometry or representation theory is the study of invariants
I_{\alpha}(\tau)
which ‘count’
\tau
-semistable objects
E
with fixed topological invariants
\llbracket E\rrbracket=\alpha
in some geometric problem, usually by means of a virtual class
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
in some homology theory for the moduli space
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
of
\tau
-semistable objects, with
I_{\alpha}(\tau)=\int_{[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}}\mu_{\alpha}
for some natural cohomology class
\mu_{\alpha}
. Often the invariants
I_{\alpha}(\tau)
have a deformation-invariance property.

We say the enumerative invariant theory is
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-
linear
if the objects
E
to be counted live in a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear additive category
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. (The algebro-geometric version of our theory should extend to
{\mathbin{\mathbb{K}}}
-linear additive categories, for
{\mathbin{\mathbb{K}}}
an algebraically closed field.) Here are some examples of such
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear theories:

(i)

Invariants counting Gieseker semistable coherent sheaves on complex projective surfaces, as in Mochizuki
[
79
]
.

(ii)

Donaldson–Thomas invariants counting semistable coherent sheaves on Calabi–Yau or Fano 3-folds, as in Thomas
[
100
]
and Joyce–Song
[
60
]
.

(iii)

Donaldson–Thomas type invariants counting semistable coherent sheaves on Calabi–Yau 4-folds, as in Borisov–Joyce
[
11
]
and Oh–Thomas
[
87
]
.

(iv)

Donaldson invariants counting anti-self-dual
{\mathbin{\rm U}}(n)
- or
\mathop{\rm SU}(n)
-connections on compact oriented 4-manifolds, as in Donaldson–Kronheimer
[
22
,
23
,
69
]
.

(v)

Invariants counting semistable representations of quivers, or quivers with relations, and similar representation-theoretic problems.

A non-example is Gromov–Witten invariants counting Deligne–Mumford stable
J
-holomorphic curves
j\colon\Sigma\rightarrow X
, since these do not form a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear category.

We discuss some new universal mathematical structures that we expect to underlie most of the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear enumerative invariant theories above. We explain our conjectural picture in detail in Section
4
. Here is a brief partial sketch, for simplicity in the algebraic geometry case with
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
for a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme
X
, as in (i), (ii) above.

(a)

There are two ways to form a moduli stack of objects in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
: the usual moduli stack
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
, in which a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-point
[E]\in{\mathbin{\cal M}}
corresponding to an object
E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
has isotropy group
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\cal M}}([E])=\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)
, and the ‘projective linear’ moduli stack
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
, in which
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}}([E])=\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)/({\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}\cdot{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{E})
. There is a morphism
{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
which is a
[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
-fibration over nonzero objects.

The second author
[
58
]
explains how to give
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}})
the structure of a
graded vertex algebra
, and
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}\big)
a
graded Lie algebra
(both with nonstandard gradings). Here by a well-known construction in vertex algebra theory
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}})/D(H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}))
is a Lie algebra, and
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}\big)\cong H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}})/D(H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}))
, giving the Lie algebra structure.

Note that
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}})
and
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}\big)
, with their vertex algebra/Lie algebra structures, can often be written down quite explicitly (see
[
42
]
).

(b)

Let
\tau
be a suitable stability condition on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, and write
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)
for the moduli schemes of
\tau
-stable and
\tau
-semistable objects in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
with Chern class
\alpha
. Then the coarse moduli scheme
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)
is proper. In the cases we are interested in, either
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)
is smooth, or has a natural perfect obstruction theory in the sense of
[
6
]
. Also
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)\subset{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
is an open substack.

Hence if
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)
(that is, if there are no strictly
\tau
-semistable objects in class
\alpha
) we have a virtual class
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
in the homology
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau),{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})
or
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau),{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}})
, and we may regard
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
as lying in the Lie algebra
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}})
from (a). We consider
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
for all
\alpha
to be the family of invariants we want to study.

(c)

If
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)\neq{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)
, the question of defining virtual classes
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
or
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
is a well known, mostly unsolved problem. In algebraic geometry, the issue is that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)
is not proper, and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)
does not have an obstruction theory, so we cannot use
[
6
]
. In differential geometry, the problem is reducible connections giving singularities in moduli spaces.

We conjecture that there are natural invariants
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\in H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}\big)
for all
\alpha
, with
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}=[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
in (b) when
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)
, which satisfy a package of properties including (d), (e) below. Here we must work in homology over
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
, not
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
, if
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)\neq{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)
.

(d)

Let
\tau
,
\tilde{\tau}
be two suitable stability conditions on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, for instance, Gieseker stability on
\operatorname{coh}(X)
with respect to two polarizations
L,\tilde{L}\rightarrow X
.

We conjecture that the invariants
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
,
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})]_{\rm inv}
are related by the universal wall-crossing formula taken from the second author
[
53
]
:

\displaystyle[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})]_{\rm inv}=\sum_{\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha}

\displaystyle\tilde{U}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\bigl[\bigl[\dots\bigl[[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)]_{\rm inv},

\displaystyle[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha_{2}}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\bigr],\dots\bigr],[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\bigr],

(1.1)

where the
\tilde{U}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
are a combinatorial coefficient system with only finitely many non-zero terms (see
[
53
, Definition 4.4]
,
[
60
, Section 3.3]
), and (
1.1
) uses the Lie bracket
[\,,\,]
on
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}\big)
from (a).

(e)

We do not currently have a direct definition of
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
in (c) when
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)\neq{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)
. However, as for Donaldson–Thomas invariants in
[
60
, Section 5.4]
and Mochizuki’s use of ‘
L
-Bradlow pairs’ in
[
79
, Section 7.3]
, there is an indirect way to define
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
using the wall-crossing formula (
1.1
) in an auxiliary category
{\mathbin{\cal B}}
of ‘pairs’
V\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(-N)\rightarrow E
in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
.

Our theory is inspired by two main sources. The second author has a general theory of motivic invariants in algebraic geometry
[
48
,
49
,
50
,
51
,
52
,
53
]
, including a wall crossing formula
[
53
, Theorems 5.2 and 5.4]
of the form (
1.1
) in a Lie algebra of ‘stack functions’, which was applied to Donaldson–Thomas theory in
[
60
]
. This does not apply to the enumerative invariants above, which are not motivic. But his recent work on vertex algebra and Lie algebra structures on homology of moduli spaces
[
58
]
provides the tools we need to extend it to enumerative invariants.

We hope that in the future our theory will lead to a better understanding of deep properties of enumerative invariants. In particular, the connection between vertex algebras and invariants is relatively unexplored. For example, can we explain modular properties and other structural features of generating functions of invariants in terms of the vertex algebras appearing in (a)?

So far we have described only conjectures. The main results of this paper, stated in Section
5
and proved in Section
6
, are to prove our conjectures in (c)–(e) above when
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
is the abelian category of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-representations of a quiver
Q
without oriented cycles. In a sequel
[
59
]
, the second author will prove the conjectures in other cases in algebraic geometry using Behrend–Fantechi virtual classes
[
6
]
, including
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\text{\rm mod-${\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q/I$}
for
(Q,I)
a quiver with relations, and
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a smooth projective complex curve, surface, or Fano 3-fold.

We define our invariants in
ordinary homology

H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}\big)
. To form virtual classes in ordinary homology, one needs
proper
moduli schemes, as in (b) above. One might hope that there should be a version of our theory in
Borel–Moore homology

H_{*}^{\rm BM}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}\big)
, that would not require properness.

However, this does not work, as pushforwards are only defined in Borel–Moore homology for proper morphisms, but we need pushforwards along non-proper morphisms at several crucial points, in particular to define the vertex algebra on
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}})
and Lie algebra on
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}\big)
in (a). Also if
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)
we need to push
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
forward along the not necessarily proper inclusion
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)\hookrightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}_{\alpha}
to regard
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
as an element of
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big)
.

2
Background on vertex algebras and Lie algebras

In this section, we review some material from the second author
[
58
]
. Throughout this section
R
is a commutative
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-algebra, for instance
R={\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}},{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
or
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
.

2.1
Vertex algebras and Lie algebras

For background on vertex algebras, we recommend Frenkel–Ben-Zvi
[
28
]
, Kac
[
62
]
, Lepowsky–Li
[
73
]
, and the second author
[
58
]
. As we work over a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-
algebra

R
, there are a few simplifications to the general theory. Here is one of several equivalent definitions of vertex algebra.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
V_{*}=\bigoplus_{n\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}}V_{n}
be a graded
R
-module. Write
V_{*}((z)):=V_{*}[[z]]\big[z^{-1}\big]
for the
R
-module of Laurent series in a formal variable
z
. The
R
-modules
V_{*}[[z]],\allowbreak V_{*}((z))
are made
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded by declaring
\deg z=-2
.

A
field
on
V_{*}
is an
R
-module homomorphism
V_{*}\rightarrow V_{*}((z))
. The set of all fields on
V_{*}
is denoted
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(V_{*})
and is considered as a graded
R
-module by declaring
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(V_{*})_{n}
to be the set of degree
n
fields
V\rightarrow V((z))
for
n\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

A
graded vertex algebra

\big(V_{*},{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}},{\rm e}^{zD},Y\big)
over
R
is a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
R
-module
V_{*}
with an identity element
{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}}\in V_{0}
, a grading-preserving operator
{\rm e}^{zD}\colon V\rightarrow V[[z]]
with
{\rm e}^{zD}v=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\frac{1}{n!}D^{n}(v)\,z^{n}
for
D\colon V_{*}\rightarrow V_{*+2}
the
translation operator
, and a grading-preserving
state-field correspondence

Y\colon V_{*}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal F}}(V_{*})_{*}
written
Y(u,z)v=\sum_{n\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}}u_{n}(v)z^{-n-1}
, where
u_{n}
maps
V_{*}\rightarrow V_{*+a-2n-2}
for
u\in V_{a}
, satisfying:

(i)

Y({\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}},z)v=v
for all
v\in V
.

(ii)

Y(v,z){\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}}={\rm e}^{zD}v
for all
v\in V
.

(iii)

For all
u\in V_{a}
and
v\in V_{b}
, there exists
N\gg 0
such for all
w\in V_{*}

(z_{1}-z_{2})^{N}\bigl(Y(u,z_{1})Y(v,z_{2})w-(-1)^{ab}Y(v,z_{2})Y(u,z_{1})w\bigr)=0\quad\text{in $V_{*}\big[\big[z_{1}^{\pm 1},z_{2}^{\pm 1}\big]\big]$}.

Part (iii) is called the
weak commutativity property
.

Let
\big(V_{*},{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}},{\rm e}^{zD},Y\big)
and
\big(V^{\prime}_{*},{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}}^{\prime},{\rm e}^{zD^{\prime}},Y^{\prime}\big)
be graded vertex algebras over
R
. A
morphism

\phi\colon\big(V_{*},{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}},{\rm e}^{zD},Y\big)\rightarrow\big(V^{\prime}_{*},{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}}^{\prime},{\rm e}^{zD^{\prime}},Y^{\prime}\big)
is an
R
-module morphism
\phi\colon V_{*}\rightarrow V_{*}^{\prime}
which preserves all the structures. That is,
\phi
maps
V_{n}\rightarrow V_{n}^{\prime}
, and
\phi({\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}})={\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}}^{\prime}
, and
\phi\circ D=D^{\prime}\circ\phi
, and
\phi\circ Y=Y^{\prime}\circ(\phi\otimes\phi)
. Such morphisms make graded vertex algebras over
R
into a category
\mathop{\bf VertAlg}\nolimits_{R}^{\bf gr}
.

Vertex algebras are very complicated objects, and the above brief definition probably communicates little real understanding of them – we refer readers to
[
28
,
58
,
62
,
73
]
for more. In this paper, the main property of (graded) vertex algebras we use is that they have a functor to (graded) Lie algebras.

Definition 2.2
.

A
graded Lie algebra
over
R
is a pair
(V_{*},[\,,\,])
, where
V_{*}=\bigoplus_{a\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}}V_{a}
is a graded
R
-module, and
[\,,\,]\colon V_{*}\times V_{*}\rightarrow V_{*}
is an
R
-bilinear map called the
Lie bracket
, which is graded (that is,
[\,,\,]
maps
V_{a}\times V_{b}\rightarrow V_{a+b}
for all
a,b\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
), such that for all
a,b,c\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
u\in V_{a}
,
v\in V_{b}
and
w\in V_{c}
we have

[v,u]=(-1)^{ab+1}[u,v],\qquad(-1)^{ca}[[u,v],w]+(-1)^{ab}[[v,w],u]+(-1)^{bc}[[w,u],v]=0.

Let
(V_{*},[\,,\,])
,
(V^{\prime}_{*},[\,,\,])
be graded Lie algebras over
R
. A
morphism

\phi\colon(V_{*},[\,,\,])\rightarrow(V^{\prime}_{*},[\,,\,])
is an
R
-module morphism
\phi\colon V_{*}\rightarrow V_{*}^{\prime}
which preserves all the structures. That is,
\phi
maps
V_{n}\rightarrow V_{n}^{\prime}
and
\phi\bigl([u,v]\bigr)=\bigl[\phi(u),\phi(v)\bigr]
. Such morphisms make graded Lie algebras over
R
into a category
\mathop{\bf LieAlg}\nolimits_{R}^{\bf gr}
.

The next proposition is due to Borcherds
[
10
, Section 4]
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
\big(V_{*},{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}},{\rm e}^{zD},Y\big)
be a graded vertex algebra over
R
. We may construct a graded Lie algebra
\big(\check{V}_{*},[\,,\,]\big)
over
R
as follows. Noting the shift in grading, define a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
R
-module
\check{V}_{*}
by

\check{V}_{n}=V_{n+2}/D(V_{n})\qquad\text{for $n\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}$},

so that
\check{V}_{*}=V_{*+2}/D(V_{*})
. If
u\in V_{a+2}
and
v\in V_{b+2}
, the Lie bracket on
\check{V}_{*}
is

\bigl[u+D(V_{a}),v+D(V_{b})\bigr]=u_{0}(v)+D(V_{a+b})\in\check{V}_{a+b}.

A morphism
\phi\colon\big(V_{*},{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}},{\rm e}^{zD},Y\big)\!\rightarrow\!\big(V^{\prime}_{*},{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}}^{\prime},{\rm e}^{zD^{\prime}},Y^{\prime}\big)
induces a morphism
\check{\phi}\colon(V_{*},[\,,\,])\!\rightarrow\!(V^{\prime}_{*},[\,,\,])
by
\check{\phi}\bigl(u+D(V_{*})\bigr)=\phi(u)+D^{\prime}(V^{\prime}_{*})
. Mapping
\big(V_{*},{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}},{\rm e}^{zD},Y\big)\allowbreak\mapsto(\check{V}_{*},[\,,\,])
and
\phi\mapsto\check{\phi}
defines a functor
\mathop{\bf VertAlg}\nolimits_{R}^{\bf gr}\rightarrow\mathop{\bf LieAlg}\nolimits_{R}^{\bf gr}
.

2.2
Stacks, and their homology groups

In Section
2.3
we will explain that if
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is a suitable
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear abelian category, or
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
is a suitable
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear triangulated category, and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
is the moduli stack of objects in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
or
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
, and we choose a little extra data, then
[
58
]
makes the homology
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
into a graded vertex algebra. First we give some brief background on stacks
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
and their homology groups
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
.

Stacks are a class of spaces in algebraic geometry. In this paper, two types of algebro-geometric stacks are relevant:
Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stacks
, as in Gómez
[
33
]
, Laumon and Moret-Bailly
[
72
]
and Olsson
[
89
]
, which form a 2-category
\mathop{\bf Art}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
, and
higher
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stacks
, as in Toën and Vezzosi
[
102
,
103
,
105
,
106
]
, which form an
\infty
-category
\mathop{\bf HSta}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
containing
\mathop{\bf Art}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\subset\mathop{\bf HSta}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
as a full discrete 2-subcategory.

The general rule is that for any algebro-geometric
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear abelian or exact category
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
appearing in this paper, such as
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
or
{\mathbin{\cal A}}={\rm vect}(X)
for
X
a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme, the moduli stack
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
of objects in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is an Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack, and for any algebro-geometric
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear triangulated category
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
, such as
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}=D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
, the moduli stack
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
is a higher
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack.

If
S
is an Artin or higher
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack, we write
S({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
for the set of 2-isomorphism classes
[x]
of 1-morphisms
x\colon\mathop{\rm Spec}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\rightarrow S
. Elements of
S({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
are called
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-
points
, or
geometric points
, of
S
. If
\phi\colon S\rightarrow T
is a 1-morphism then composition with
\phi
induces a map of sets
\phi_{*}\colon S({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})\rightarrow T({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
.

If
S
is an Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack, each
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-point
x\in S({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
has an
isotropy group

\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{S}(x)
, an algebraic
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-group. We say that
S

has affine geometric stabilizers
if
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{S}(x)
is an affine algebraic
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-group for all
x\in S({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
.

An important class of Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stacks are
quotient stacks

[S/G]
, where
S
is a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme and
G
is an algebraic
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-group acting on
S
. When
{\mathbin{\cal M}}=[S/G]
, the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-points are
G({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
-orbits
xG({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
for
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-points
x\in S({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
, and the isotropy groups are
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{[S/G]}(xG({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}))=\mathop{\rm Stab}\nolimits_{G({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})}(x)
.

As in Simpson
[
95
]
and Blanc
[
8
, Section 3.1]
, any Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack or higher
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
has a
topological realization

{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm top}
, which is a topological space (in fact, a CW-complex) natural up to homotopy equivalence. Topological realization gives a functor
(-)^{\rm top}\colon\mathop{\rm Ho}(\mathop{\bf HSta}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})\rightarrow{\mathop{\bf Top}\nolimits}^{\bf ho}
from the homotopy category
\mathop{\rm Ho}(\mathop{\bf HSta}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
to the category
{\mathop{\bf Top}\nolimits}^{\bf ho}
of topological spaces with morphisms homotopy classes of continuous maps.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
be an Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack, or higher
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack, and
R
be a commutative
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-algebra, such as
R={\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}},{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
or
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
. We define the
homology
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})=H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}},R)
of
M
with coefficients in

R
to be
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})=H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm top},R)
, the usual homology of the topological space
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm top}
. Similarly we define the cohomology
H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})=H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}},R)=H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm top},R)
. These are sometimes called the
Betti (co)homology
, to distinguish them from other (co)homology theories of stacks. We usually omit the coefficient
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-algebra
R
from our notation
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
,
H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
.

The following properties of
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
,
H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
will be important later:

(a)

Let
S
be a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme, and
S^{\rm an}
the underlying complex analytic space. Then
H_{*}(S)\cong H_{*}(S^{\rm an})
and
H^{*}(S)\cong H^{*}(S^{\rm an})
.

(b)

If
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
is a quotient stack
[S/G]
, we have a homotopy equivalence

{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm top}\simeq(S^{\rm an}\times EG^{\rm an})/G^{\rm an},

where
EG^{\rm an}\rightarrow BG^{\rm an}
is a classifying space for the complex analytic topological group
G^{\rm an}=G({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
. If
S
is contractible (e.g., if
S
is a point
*
or an affine space
{\mathbin{\mathbb{A}}}^{n}
) this implies that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm top}\simeq BG^{\rm an}
.

(c)

For a disjoint union
{\mathbin{\cal M}}=\coprod_{i\in I}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{i}
we have
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\cong\bigoplus_{i\in I}H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{i})
and
H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\cong\prod_{i\in I}H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{i})
.

(d)

H_{*}(-)
is covariantly functorial, and
H^{*}(-)
is contravariantly functorial, under morphisms of stacks, in the obvious way.

(e)

Cap and cup products
\cap
,
\cup
are defined and have the usual properties, e.g., if
f\colon S\rightarrow T
is a morphism of stacks and
\alpha\in H_{*}(S)
,
\beta\in H^{*}(T)
then

H_{*}(f)(\alpha\cap H^{*}(f)(\beta))=H_{*}(f)(\alpha)\cap\beta.

(f)

The Künneth theorem gives isomorphisms
H_{*}(S\times T)\cong H_{*}(S)\otimes_{R}H_{*}(T)
and
H^{*}(S\times T)\cong H^{*}(S)\otimes_{R}H^{*}(T)
, as
R
is a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-algebra.

(g)

Let
{\mathbin{\cal E}}^{\bullet}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
be a perfect complex (e.g., a vector bundle). Then
{\mathbin{\cal E}}^{\bullet}
corresponds to a morphism
\phi_{{\mathbin{\cal E}}^{\bullet}}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Perf}_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
, where
\mathop{\rm Perf}_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
is a higher stack which classifies perfect complexes, as in Toën and Vezzosi
[
106
, Definition 1.3.7.5]
. The topological realization of
\mathop{\rm Perf}_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
is
B{\mathbin{\rm U}}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
, where
B{\mathbin{\rm U}}=\varinjlim_{n\rightarrow\infty}B{\mathbin{\rm U}}(n)
is the stable unitary classifying space, so that
B{\mathbin{\rm U}}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
is the classifying space for topological complex K-theory
K^{0}(-)
, as in May
[
76
, Sections 23–24]
. Thus
\phi_{{\mathbin{\cal E}}^{\bullet}}^{\rm top}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm top}\rightarrow B{\mathbin{\rm U}}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
defines a K-theory class
[{\mathbin{\cal E}}^{\bullet}]\in K^{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm top})
. Hence we may define the
Chern classes

c_{i}({\mathbin{\cal E}}^{\bullet})=c_{i}([{\mathbin{\cal E}}^{\bullet}])
in
H^{2i}({\mathbin{\cal M}})=H^{2i}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm top})
. These have the usual properties of Chern classes, e.g.,
c_{k}({\mathbin{\cal E}}^{\bullet}\oplus{\mathbin{\cal F}}^{\bullet})=\sum_{i+j=k}c_{i}({\mathbin{\cal E}}^{\bullet})\cup c_{j}({\mathbin{\cal F}}^{\bullet})
.

As
B{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}\simeq{\mathbin{\mathbb{CP}}}^{\infty}
, using (b) above and
{\mathbin{\mathbb{CP}}}^{n}\hookrightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{CP}}}^{\infty}
we have an isomorphism

\displaystyle H^{*}([*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}])\cong R[[z]]\quad\text{as $R$-algebras, with $\deg z=2$, so that}

\displaystyle H^{2n}([*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}])=\langle z^{n}\rangle_{R},\quad\text{normalized so that}\quad\int_{{\mathbin{\mathbb{CP}}}^{n}}z^{n}=1.

(2.1)

Our conjectures in Section
4
involve fairly general Artin and higher stacks. However, our main results in Sections
5
–
6
involve only moduli stacks
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
of abelian categories of quiver representations
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. These are of a very simple kind: we have
{\mathbin{\cal M}}=\coprod_{\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}
, where
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}
is a quotient stack
[V_{\boldsymbol{d}}/\Pi_{v\in Q_{0}}\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits(\boldsymbol{d}(v),{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})]
for
V_{\boldsymbol{d}}
a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space. Then we can compute
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
,
H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
using (b), (c) above. So Sections
5
–
6
do not need a detailed knowledge of stacks.

There is also a theory of
topological stacks
due to Metzler
[
77
]
and Noohi
[
85
,
86
]
, which we can use to write the differential-geometric version of our conjectural picture in Section
4
. In this paper, by ‘topological stacks’ we mean
hoparacompact topological stacks
in the sense of Noohi
[
86
, Section 8.3]
, which form a 2-category
{\mathop{\bf TopSta}\nolimits}
. These are a generalization of topological spaces, which as in
[
86
]
have a well-behaved homotopy theory. As for the algebraic case, there is a topological realization functor
(-)^{\rm top}\colon\mathop{\rm Ho}({\mathop{\bf TopSta}\nolimits})\rightarrow{\mathop{\bf Top}\nolimits}^{\bf ho}
. If
{\mathbin{\cal B}}
is a topological stack we define
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal B}})=H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal B}}^{\rm top},R)
and
H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal B}})=H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal B}}^{\rm top},R)
, as above.

We will be primarily interested in the following case (see
[
61
]
for more details). Let
X
be a compact manifold,
P\rightarrow X
a principal
{\mathbin{\rm U}}(n)
-bundle,
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{P}
the infinite-dimensional affine space of all connections
\nabla_{P}
on
P
, and
{{\cal G}}_{P}=\mathop{\rm Aut}(P)
the infinite-dimensional Lie group of gauge transformations of
P
. Then
{{\cal G}}_{P}
acts continuously on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{P}
, and we define
{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{P}={\mathbin{\cal A}}_{P}/{{\cal G}}_{P}
to be the quotient topological stack. Since
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{P}
is contractible, we have

H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal B}}_{P}):=H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal B}}_{P}^{\rm top},R\big)=H_{*}(({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{P}\times E{{\cal G}}_{P})/{{\cal G}}_{P},R)\cong H_{*}(B{{\cal G}}_{P},R).

2.3
A geometric construction of vertex algebras

In
[
58
]
, amongst other things, the second author defines new graded vertex algebra structures on the homology
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
of moduli stacks
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
of objects in suitable abelian categories
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
or triangulated categories
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
. We explain the construction in a special case. The next assumption sets out the data we need. As in Section
2.2
, throughout
R
is a fixed
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-algebra, and
H_{*}(-)=H_{*}(-,R)
and
H^{*}(-)=H^{*}(-,R)
denote (co)homology over
R
.

Assumption 2.4
.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
be a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear abelian or exact category coming from algebraic geometry or representation theory, e.g., we could take
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
or
{\rm vect}(X)
for
X
a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme, or
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
the category of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-representations of a quiver
Q
. Assume:

(a)

We can form a natural moduli stack
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
of objects in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, an Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack, locally of finite type. Then
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-points of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
are isomorphism classes
[E]
of objects
E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, and the isotropy groups are
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\cal M}}([E])=\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)
.

(b)

There is a natural morphism of Artin stacks
\Phi\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
which on
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-points acts by
\Phi_{*}\colon([E],[F])\mapsto[E\oplus F]
, for all objects
E,F\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, and on isotropy groups acts by
\Phi_{*}\colon\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}}([E],[F])\cong\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)\times\mathop{\rm Aut}(F)\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\cal M}}([E\oplus F])\cong\mathop{\rm Aut}(E\oplus F)
by
(\lambda,\mu)\mapsto\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda&0\\
0&\mu\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
for
\lambda\in\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)
and
\mu\in\mathop{\rm Aut}(F)
, using the obvious matrix notation for
\mathop{\rm Aut}(E\oplus F)
. That is,
\Phi
is the morphism of moduli stacks induced by direct sum in the abelian category
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. It is associative and commutative in
\mathop{\rm Ho}(\mathop{\bf Art}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
.

(c)

There is a natural morphism of Artin stacks
\Psi\colon[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
which on
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-points acts by
\Psi_{*}\colon(*,[E])\mapsto[E]
, for all objects
E
in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, and on isotropy groups acts by
\Psi_{*}\colon\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}}(*,[E])\cong{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}\times\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)\rightarrow\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\cal M}}([E])\cong\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)
by
(\lambda,\mu)\mapsto\lambda\mu=(\lambda\cdot{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{E})\circ\mu
for
\lambda\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}
and
\mu\in\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)
. Note that
\Psi
is
not
the same as the projection
\pi_{\mathbin{\cal M}}\colon[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
from the product
[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
, which acts on isotropy groups as
(\pi_{\mathbin{\cal M}})_{*}\colon(\lambda,\mu)\mapsto\mu
. We have identities in
\mathop{\rm Ho}(\mathop{\bf Art}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
:

\displaystyle\Psi\circ({\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]}\times\Phi)=\Phi\circ\bigl(\Psi\circ\Pi_{12},\Psi\circ\Pi_{13}\bigr)\colon\ [*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{2}\longrightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}},

\displaystyle\Psi\circ({\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]}\times\Psi)=\Psi\circ(\Omega\times{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{\mathbin{\cal M}})\colon\ [*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]^{2}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}\longrightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}},

where
\Pi_{ij}
projects to the
i^{\rm th}
and
j^{\rm th}
factors, and

\Omega\colon[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]^{2}\rightarrow[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
is induced by the morphism
{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}
mapping
(\lambda,\mu)\mapsto\lambda\mu
.

(d)

We are given a surjective quotient of abelian groups
K_{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}})\twoheadrightarrow K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
of the Grothendieck group
K_{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}})
of
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. We write
\llbracket E\rrbracket\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
for the class of
E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. We suppose that if
E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
with
\llbracket E\rrbracket=0
in
K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
then
E=0
.

We require that the map
{\mathbin{\cal M}}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})\rightarrow K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
mapping
E\mapsto\llbracket E\rrbracket
should be locally constant. This gives a decomposition
{\mathbin{\cal M}}=\coprod_{\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}
of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
into open and closed
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-substacks
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\subset{\mathbin{\cal M}}
of objects in class
\alpha
, where
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{0}=\{[0]\}
. We write
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\neq 0}={\mathbin{\cal M}}\setminus{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{0}
. We write
\Phi_{\alpha,\beta}=\Phi|_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha+\beta}
and
\Psi_{\alpha}=\Psi|_{[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}}\colon[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}
.

(e)

We are given a symmetric biadditive form
\chi\colon K({\mathbin{\cal A}})\times K({\mathbin{\cal A}})\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

(f)

We are given signs
\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}\in\{\pm 1\}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
, such that for all
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
we have

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}\cdot\epsilon_{\beta,\alpha}=(-1)^{\chi(\alpha,\beta)+\chi(\alpha,\alpha)\chi(\beta,\beta)},

(2.2)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}\cdot\epsilon_{\alpha+\beta,\gamma}=\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta+\gamma}\cdot\epsilon_{\beta,\gamma},

(2.3)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\alpha,0}=\epsilon_{0,\alpha}=1.

(2.4)

(g)

We are given a perfect complex
\Theta^{\bullet}
on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
, such that the restriction
\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}^{\bullet}:=\Theta^{\bullet}|_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}}
to
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}
has rank
\chi(\alpha,\beta)
for all
\alpha,\beta\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
, and there are isomorphisms of perfect complexes

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{*}(\Theta^{\bullet})\cong(\Theta^{\bullet})^{\vee}[2n],

(2.5)

\displaystyle(\Phi\times{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{\mathbin{\cal M}})^{*}(\Theta^{\bullet})\cong\Pi_{13}^{*}(\Theta^{\bullet})\oplus\Pi_{23}^{*}(\Theta^{\bullet}),

(2.6)

\displaystyle({\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times\Phi)^{*}(\Theta^{\bullet})\cong\Pi_{12}^{*}(\Theta^{\bullet})\oplus\Pi_{13}^{*}(\Theta^{\bullet}),

(2.7)

\displaystyle(\Psi\times{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{\mathbin{\cal M}})^{*}(\Theta^{\bullet})\cong\Pi_{1}^{*}(L_{[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]})\otimes\Pi_{23}^{*}(\Theta^{\bullet}),

(2.8)

\displaystyle(\Pi_{2},\Psi\circ\Pi_{13})^{*}(\Theta^{\bullet})\cong\Pi_{1}^{*}(L_{[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]}^{*})\otimes\Pi_{23}^{*}(\Theta^{\bullet}).

(2.9)

Here (
2.5
) is on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
, where
\sigma_{\mathbin{\cal M}}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
exchanges the factors, and
n\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
. Equations (
2.6
)–(
2.7
) are on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
, and (
2.8
)–(
2.9
) on
[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
. We write
\Pi_{i}
for the projection to the
i^{\rm th}
factor, and
\Pi_{ij}
for the projection to the product of the
i^{\rm th}
and
j^{\rm th}
factors. We write
L_{[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]}\rightarrow[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
for the line bundle corresponding to the weight 1 representation of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}={\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\setminus\{0\}
on
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
.

Although Assumption
2.4
(a)–(g) look like a lot of rather arbitrary data, as explained in
[
58
,
59
]
and Section
4
, there are natural choices for all this data in many large classes of interesting examples.

Definition 2.5
.

Suppose Assumption
2.4
holds. Given all this data, we define a graded vertex algebra structure on the homology
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
from Section
2.2
. The inclusion of the zero object
0\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
gives a morphism
[0]\colon*\hookrightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
inducing
R\cong H_{*}(*)\rightarrow H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
, and we define
{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}}\in H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
to be the image of
1\in R
under this map. Taking homology of
\Psi
gives a map

\textstyle{H_{*}([*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}])\!\otimes_{R}\!H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxtimes}
\textstyle{H_{*}([*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]\!\times\!{\mathbin{\cal M}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H_{*}(\Psi)}
\textstyle{H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}).}

As
H^{*}([*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}])\cong\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}(H_{*}([*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]),R)
, this is equivalent to a map

\textstyle{H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\hat{\otimes}_{R}H^{*}([*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm(\ref{wc2eq1})}}
\textstyle{H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})[[z]],}

using equation (
2.1
), and we denote the composition
{\rm e}^{zD}
.

The decomposition
{\mathbin{\cal M}}=\coprod_{\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}
induces an identification

H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})}H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}).

(2.10)

For
u\in H_{a}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha})\subset H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
and
v\in H_{b}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta})\subset H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
, define

\displaystyle Y(u,z)v=Y(z)(u\otimes v)=\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}(-1)^{a\chi(\beta,\beta)}\sum\nolimits_{i\geqslant 0}z^{\chi(\alpha,\beta)-i}

\displaystyle\hphantom{Y(u,z)v=Y(z)(u\otimes v)= }{}\times H_{*}(\Phi)\circ\big({\rm e}^{zD}\otimes{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}\big)((u\boxtimes v)\cap c_{i}(\Theta^{\bullet})).

(2.11)

Using (
2.10
), for
n\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
we write

\hat{H}_{n}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha})=H_{n-\chi(\alpha,\alpha)}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}),\qquad\hat{H}_{n}({\mathbin{\cal M}})=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})}\hat{H}_{n}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}).

(2.12)

That is,
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
is
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
, but with grading shifted by
-\chi(\alpha,\alpha)
in the component
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha})\subset H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
. The second author
[
58
]
proves:

Theorem 2.6
.

In Definition (
2.5
),
\big(\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}),{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}},{\rm e}^{zD},Y\big)
is a graded vertex algebra over
R
.

Many examples of vertex algebras can be found in Frenkel–Ben-Zvi
[
28
]
, Kac
[
62
]
, and Lepowsky–Li
[
73
]
. They are generally complicated to write down, as all nontrivial (i.e., non-holomorphic) vertex algebras are infinite-dimensional. When we can compute the vertex algebras arising from Theorem
2.6
explicitly, they are usually minor modifications of (
super-
)
lattice vertex algebras

[
28
, Section 5.4]
,
[
62
, Section 5.4]
. See Theorem
4.8
below for a class of examples for
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
in which we can describe
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
and its vertex algebra structure explicitly.

Vertex algebras from quiver categories
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
or
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=D^{b}\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
can also be written down explicitly
[
58
]
. That for
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=D^{b}\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
is the lattice vertex algebra used to construct the Kac–Moody Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
associated to the underlying Dynkin diagram (undirected graph) of
Q
, as in Kac
[
63
, Sections 1 and 5]
, and
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
gives the vertex subalgebra yielding the positive part
\mathfrak{n}_{+}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

2.4
Lie algebras from the vertex algebras of Section
2.3

In the situation of Section
2.3
, Proposition
2.3
makes
\hat{H}_{*+2}({\mathbin{\cal M}})/D\big(\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\big)
into a graded Lie algebra. We can interpret this as the shifted homology
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
of a modification
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
, which we now describe.

Definition 2.7
.

Continue in the situation of Assumption
2.4
and Definition
2.5
. Then
[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
is a group stack, and
\Psi\colon[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
is an action of
[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{0}\amalg{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\neq 0}
, which is trivial on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{0}=\{[0]\}
, and free on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\neq 0}
. As explained in
[
58
]
, we may take the quotient of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
by
\Psi
to get a stack
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
, which we call the
projective linear moduli stack
, with projection
\Pi^{\rm pl}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
, in a 2-co-Cartesian square in
\mathop{\bf Art}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
:

\textstyle{[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\mathbin{\cal M}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbin{\cal M}}}
\scriptstyle{\Pi^{\rm pl}}
\textstyle{{\mathbin{\cal M}}}
\scriptstyle{\Pi^{\rm pl}}
\textstyle{{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}.\!}

The construction of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
as
{\mathbin{\cal M}}/[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
is known in the literature as
rigidification
, as in Abramovich–Olsson–Vistoli
[
1
]
and Romagny
[
92
]
, and is written
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}={\mathbin{\cal M}}\!\!\fatslash\,{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}
in
[
1
,
92
]
. It is used, for example, to rigidify the Picard stack
\mathop{\rm Pic}(X)
of line bundles on a projective scheme
X
to get the Picard scheme
\mathop{\rm Pic}(X)\!\!\fatslash\,{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}
. A typical example is that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
contains a component
[*/\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits(n,{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})]
, and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
contains a corresponding component
[*/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits(n,{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})]
, where
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits(n,{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})=\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits(n,{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})/({\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}\cdot{\mathop{\rm Id}\nolimits}_{n})
. That is, the passage
{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
converts general linear isotropy groups
\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits(n,{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
to projective linear isotropy groups
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits(n,{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
, which is why we call
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
‘projective linear’.

We regard
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
as the moduli stack of all objects in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
‘up to projective linear isomorphisms’. Since
\Pi^{\rm pl}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
is a
[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
-bundle it is an isomorphism on
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-points. Thus,
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-points
x\in{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
correspond naturally to isomorphism classes
[E]
of objects
E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, as for
{\mathbin{\cal M}}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
, and we will write points of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
as
[E]
, and then
\Pi^{\rm pl}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
maps
[E]\mapsto[E]
.

The isotropy groups of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
satisfy
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}}([E])\cong\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\cal M}}([E])/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}
for
E\neq 0
, where the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}
-subgroup of
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\cal M}}([E])
is determined by the action of
\Psi
on isotropy groups. Thus by Assumption
2.4
(c) we see that

\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}}([E])\cong\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)/({\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}\cdot{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{E}).

(2.13)

The action of
\Pi^{\rm pl}
on isotropy groups is given by the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\cal M}}([E])}
\scriptstyle{\Pi^{\rm pl}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}}([E])}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{{\rm(\ref{wc2eq14})}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon\,\longmapsto\,\epsilon{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)/({\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}\cdot{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{E}).\!\!}

The splitting
{\mathbin{\cal M}}=\coprod_{\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}
descends to
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}=\coprod_{\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
, with
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\!\!\fatslash\,{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}
for
\alpha\neq 0
and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{0}^{\rm pl}={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{0}=*
. Thus as for (
2.10
) we have

H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})}H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big).

(2.14)

In a similar way to (
2.12
), using (
2.14
), for
n\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
we write

\check{H}_{n}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big)=H_{n+2-\chi(\alpha,\alpha)}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big),\qquad\check{H}_{n}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})}\check{H}_{n}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big).

(2.15)

That is,
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
is
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
, but with grading shifted by
2-\chi(\alpha,\alpha)
in the component
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big)\subset H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
.

Remark 2.8
.

We now have
two different versions

{\mathbin{\cal M}}
and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
of the moduli stack of objects in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. Most of the literature on moduli stacks focusses on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
. However, for enumerative invariants,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}

is often more useful
.

To see why, observe that one often forms enumerative invariants by forming
moduli schemes

{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
of
\tau
-(semi)stable objects in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
in class
\alpha
in
K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
, for
\tau
a suitable stability condition. In good cases
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)
has a perfect obstruction theory, and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
is proper. If
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
, then by Behrend and Fantechi
[
6
]
we have a virtual class
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
in
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau))
.

If
E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is
\tau
-stable then
\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)={\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}
, and thus
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\cal M}}([E])={\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}
and
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}}([E])=\{1\}
. Now
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-schemes may be regarded as examples of Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stacks with trivial isotropy groups. Then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
is an
open substack
, so we can regard
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
as lying in
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
. But in general there is no natural morphism
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
, so we cannot map
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
to
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
.

The next theorem, proved in
[
58
]
, gives a geometric interpretation of the graded Lie algebra
\hat{H}_{*+2}({\mathbin{\cal M}})/D\big(\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\big)
.

Theorem 2.9
.

Work in the situation of Assumption
2.4
and Definitions
2.5
and
2.7
, and consider the graded Lie algebra
\hat{H}_{*+2}({\mathbin{\cal M}})/D\big(\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\big)
constructed by combining Proposition
2.3
and Theorem
2.6
. Then
\Pi^{\rm pl}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
gives a morphism
H_{*}\big(\Pi^{\rm pl}\big)\colon H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\allowbreak\rightarrow H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
. It is surjective, with kernel
D(H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}))
. This induces an isomorphism

H_{*}\big(\Pi^{\rm pl}\big)_{*}\colon\ H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})/D(H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}))\longrightarrow H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big).

Comparing
(
2.12
)
and
(
2.15
)
, we see this is an isomorphism for all
n\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
:

\hat{H}_{n+2}({\mathbin{\cal M}})/D\big(\hat{H}_{n}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\big)\longrightarrow\check{H}_{n}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big).

(2.16)

Thus there is a unique Lie bracket
[\,,\,]
on
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
making it into a graded Lie algebra, such that
(
2.16
)
is a Lie algebra isomorphism. Hence
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
is a Lie algebra.

Remark 2.10
.

(a)

The fact that (
2.16
) is an isomorphism depends on the assumptions that
R
is a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-algebra, that we are working with an abelian or exact category
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
rather than a triangulated category
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
, and on another assumption we have suppressed that holds in all the cases we will discuss, which imply that the
[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
-fibration
\Pi^{\rm pl}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
is ‘rationally trivial’ in the sense of
[
58
]
.

In the more general cases discussed in
[
58
]
– in particular, if either
R
is not a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-algebra, or we work with triangulated categories
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
such as
D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
– then equation (
2.16
) need not be an isomorphism, although under mild conditions it is an isomorphism when
n=0
, which is what matters in this paper.

Proving a conjecture in
[
58
]
, Upmeier
[
107
]
gives a direct construction of the graded Lie bracket
[\,,\,]
on
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
, using a new notion of characteristic class of perfect complexes over
[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
-fibrations
\Pi\colon S\rightarrow T
of stacks
S
,
T
, called the
projective Euler class
, such that (
2.16
) is a morphism of graded Lie algebras in every case, whether or not (
2.16
) is an isomorphism.

(b)

As in
[
58
]
, in cases in which we can compute
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
explicitly,
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
is often (a minor modification of) a Kac–Moody Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
as in Kac
[
63
, Sections 1 and 5]
(when
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is a derived category
D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
or
D^{b}\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
), or its positive part
\mathfrak{n}_{+}
(when
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
). For example, if
Q
is a quiver whose underlying graph is an ADE Dynkin diagram and
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=D^{b}\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
then
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)={\mathfrak{g}}
is the corresponding finite-dimensional ADE Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}(n+1,{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
,
{\mathfrak{so}}(2n,{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
,
\mathfrak{e}_{6}
,
\mathfrak{e}_{7}
,
\mathfrak{e}_{8}
, and
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
gives its positive part
\mathfrak{n}_{+}
.

Vertex algebras were originally introduced in mathematics by Borcherds
[
10
]
to better understand the construction of Kac–Moody-type Lie algebras.

2.5
Morphisms of the vertex and Lie algebras of Sections
2.3
–
2.4

We now construct morphisms between the graded vertex algebras and Lie algebras of Sections
2.3
–
2.4
, that will be used in the proofs of our main results in Section
6
.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
\Phi
,
\Psi
,
K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
,
\chi
,
\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}
,
\Theta^{\bullet}
and

{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime}
,
\Phi^{\prime}
,
\allowbreak\Psi^{\prime}
,
\allowbreak K({\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime})
,
\allowbreak\chi^{\prime}
,
\allowbreak\epsilon^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}
,
\Theta^{\prime\bullet}
be two sets of data satisfying Assumption
2.4
. Write

\big(\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}),\allowbreak{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}},\allowbreak{\rm e}^{zD},\allowbreak Y\big)
and
\big(\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime}),{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}}^{\prime},{\rm e}^{zD^{\prime}},Y^{\prime}\big)
for the corresponding graded vertex algebras from Theorem
2.6
. Suppose:

(a)

We are given a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear exact functor
\Sigma\colon{\mathbin{\cal A}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}
. This should induce a morphism
\sigma\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime}
of moduli stacks, which acts on
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-points as
\sigma([E])=[\Sigma(E)]
. The induced morphism
\Sigma_{*}\colon K_{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}})\rightarrow K_{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime})
descends to
\Sigma_{*}\colon K({\mathbin{\cal A}})\rightarrow K({\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime})
, with
\Sigma_{*}(\llbracket E\rrbracket)=\llbracket\Sigma(E)\rrbracket
. In
\mathop{\rm Ho}(\mathop{\bf Art}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
we have

\Phi^{\prime}\circ(\sigma\times\sigma)=\sigma\circ\Phi,\qquad\Psi^{\prime}\circ({\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]}\times\sigma)=\sigma\circ\Psi.

(b)

We are given a biadditive morphism
\xi\colon K({\mathbin{\cal A}})\times K({\mathbin{\cal A}})\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

(c)

We are given a vector bundle
F\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
of mixed rank, such that
F_{\alpha,\beta}:=F|_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}}
has rank
\xi(\alpha,\beta)
for all
\alpha,\beta\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
. We also write
G\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
for the vector bundle
\Delta_{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{*}(F^{*})
, where
\Delta_{\mathbin{\cal M}}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
is the diagonal morphism. Then
G_{\alpha}:=G|_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}}
has rank
\xi(\alpha,\alpha)
.

All this data should satisfy:

(i)

\chi^{\prime}(\Sigma_{*}(\alpha),\Sigma_{*}(\beta))=\chi(\alpha,\beta)+\xi(\alpha,\beta)+\xi(\beta,\alpha)
for all
\alpha,\beta\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
.

(ii)

\epsilon^{\prime}_{\Sigma_{*}(\alpha),\Sigma_{*}(\beta)}=(-1)^{\xi(\alpha,\beta)}\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
.

(iii)

As for (
2.6
)–(
2.9
), there are isomorphisms of vector bundles

\displaystyle(\Phi\times{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{\mathbin{\cal M}})^{*}(F)\cong\Pi_{13}^{*}(F)\oplus\Pi_{23}^{*}(F),

(2.17)

\displaystyle({\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times\Phi)^{*}(F)\cong\Pi_{12}^{*}(F)\oplus\Pi_{13}^{*}(F),

(2.18)

\displaystyle(\Psi\times{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{\mathbin{\cal M}})^{*}(F)\cong\Pi_{1}^{*}(L_{[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]})\otimes\Pi_{23}^{*}(F),

(2.19)

\displaystyle(\Pi_{2},\Psi\circ\Pi_{13})^{*}(F)\cong\Pi_{1}^{*}(L_{[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]}^{*})\otimes\Pi_{23}^{*}(F).

(2.20)

(iv)

In
K_{0}(\mathop{\rm Perf}({\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}))
we have

(\sigma\times\sigma)^{*}([\Theta^{\prime\bullet}])=[\Theta^{\bullet}]+[F]+[\sigma_{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{*}(F^{*})],

where
\sigma_{\mathbin{\cal M}}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
exchanges the factors. (This implies (i).)

(v)

There is a vector bundle
G^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
with
G\cong\big(\Pi^{\rm pl}\big)^{*}\big(G^{\rm pl}\big)
.

Write
c_{\rm top}(G)\in H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
for the top Chern class
c_{\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits G}(G)
of
G
. Note that as
\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits G
depends on the component
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\subset{\mathbin{\cal M}}
as in (c), we have
c_{\rm top}(G)|_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}}=c_{\xi(\alpha,\alpha)}(G_{\alpha})
. Define an
R
-module morphism

\Omega\colon\ H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\longrightarrow H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime})\qquad\text{by}\quad\Omega(u)=H_{*}(\sigma)\bigl(u\cap c_{\rm top}(G)\bigr).

(2.21)

Here if
u\in H_{a}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha})
then
\Omega(u)\in H_{a-2\xi(\alpha,\alpha)}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime}_{\Sigma_{*}(\alpha)})
. Combining this with (
2.12
) and (i), we see that
\Omega\colon\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\rightarrow\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime})
is grading-preserving. The next theorem is proved in
[
58
]
:

Theorem 2.12
.

In Definition
2.11
,
\Omega\colon\big(\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}),{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}},{\rm e}^{zD},Y\big)\rightarrow\big(\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime}),\allowbreak{\mathbin{\mathbbm{1}}}^{\prime},\allowbreak{\rm e}^{zD^{\prime}},\allowbreak Y^{\prime}\big)
is a morphism of graded vertex algebras.

Remark 2.13
.

To prove Theorem
2.12
, it is essential that
G\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
should be a
vector bundle
, not just a perfect complex. This ensures that
\xi(\alpha,\alpha)=\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits G_{\alpha}\geqslant 0
and
c_{i}(G_{\alpha})=0
for
i>\xi(\alpha,\alpha)
, which are used in the proof.

One consequence is that we cannot make the analogues of Definition
2.11
and Theorem
2.12
work when we replace
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}
by
triangulated categories

{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
,
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}^{\prime}
, such as
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}=D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
, as allowed in
[
58
]
, because we cannot satisfy the conditions of Definition
2.11
with nonzero vector bundles
F
,
G
in the triangulated case – for example, unless
\xi\equiv 0
we would have
\xi(\alpha,\beta)<0
for some
\alpha
,
\beta
with
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha},{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}\neq\varnothing
, and then Definition
2.11
(c) gives
\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits F_{\alpha,\beta}<0
, a contradiction.

Definition 2.14
.

Work in the situation of Definition
2.11
. Then
\sigma\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime}
descends to a morphism
\sigma^{\rm pl}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}
with
\sigma^{\rm pl}\circ\Pi^{\rm pl}=\Pi^{\prime{\rm pl}}\circ\sigma
in
\mathop{\rm Ho}(\mathop{\bf Art}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
. As for (
2.21
), define an
R
-module morphism

\Omega^{\rm pl}\colon\ H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\longrightarrow H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}\big)\qquad\text{by}\quad\Omega^{\rm pl}(u)=H_{*}\big(\sigma^{\rm pl}\big)\big(u\cap c_{\rm top}\big(G^{\rm pl}\big)\big).

(2.22)

By (
2.15
) we see that
\Omega^{\rm pl}\colon\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\longrightarrow\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}\big)
is grading preserving. From
\sigma^{\rm pl}\circ\Pi^{\rm pl}=\Pi^{\prime{\rm pl}}\circ\sigma
,
G\cong\big(\Pi^{\rm pl}\big)^{*}\big(G^{\rm pl}\big)
, and (
2.21
)–(
2.22
) we see that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})}
\scriptstyle{\Omega|_{H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})}}
\scriptstyle{H_{*}(\Pi^{\rm pl})}
\textstyle{H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{H_{*}(\tilde{\Pi}^{\rm pl})}
\textstyle{H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\rm pl}}
\textstyle{H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}\big).\!}

Hence Theorems
2.6
,
2.9
, and
2.12
imply:

Corollary 2.15
.

In Definition
2.14
,
\Omega^{\rm pl}\colon\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\rightarrow\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}\big)
is a morphism of the graded Lie algebras in Theorem
2.9
.

3
Background on stability conditions, wall-crossing formulae,

and pair invariants

Next we explain parts of the second author’s series
[
48
,
49
,
50
,
51
,
52
,
53
]
, focussing in particular on Ringel–Hall algebras and Lie algebras, motivic invariants, and their wall-crossing formulae. Almost all of this section will not be used later, but appears only for motivation, because of the comparison between Theorem
3.15
and Conjecture
4.2
. So we omit details in places.

3.1
Constructible functions and stack functions

The theory of constructible functions on Artin stacks was developed by the second author
[
49
]
. We use the notation on stacks from Section
2.2
. All stacks in Section
3
are assumed to have affine geometric stabilizers.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
X
,
Y
be Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stacks. We call
C\subseteq X({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})

constructible
if
C=\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
, where
\{X_{i}\colon i\in I\}
is a finite collection of finite type Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-substacks
X_{i}
of
X
. Let
R
be a commutative
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-algebra. A function
f\colon X({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})\rightarrow R
is called
constructible
if
f(X({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}))
is finite and
f^{-1}(c)
is a constructible set in
X({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
for each
c\in f(X({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}))\setminus\{0\}
. Write
\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(X)
for the
R
-module of
R
-valued constructible functions on
X
. If
C\subseteq X({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
is constructible we write
\delta_{C}\in\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(X)
for its characteristic function. If
\phi\colon X\rightarrow Y
is a representable 1-morphism then
[
49
, Section 4.3]
defines the
R
-linear
pushforward

\phi_{*}\colon\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(X)\rightarrow\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(Y)
, using Euler characteristics. If
\theta\colon X\rightarrow Y
is a finite type 1-morphism then
[
49
, Section 5.2]
defines the
R
-linear
pullback

\theta^{*}\colon\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(Y)\rightarrow\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(X)
.

Here
[
49
, Theorems 5.4, 5.6 and Definition 5.5]
are some properties of
these.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
W
,
X
,
Y
,
Z
be Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stacks, and
\beta\colon X\rightarrow Y
,
\gamma\colon Y\rightarrow Z
be
1
-morphisms. Then

\displaystyle(\gamma\circ\beta)_{*}=\gamma_{*}\circ\beta_{*}\colon\ \mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(X)\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(Z),

(3.1)

\displaystyle(\gamma\circ\beta)^{*}=\beta^{*}\circ\gamma^{*}\colon\ \mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(Z)\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(X),

(3.2)

supposing
\beta,\gamma
representable in
(
3.1
)
, and of finite type
in
(
3.2
)
. If

(3.3)

The second author
[
52
]
also introduced ‘stack functions’, a universal generalization of constructible functions on stacks. To each Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack
X
we assign an
R
-module
\mathop{\rm SF}(X)
of ‘stack functions’, which is generated by morphisms
f\colon S\rightarrow X
with
S
a finite type Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack and
f
a representable 1-morphism, subject to some relations we will not give. They have the same package of properties as constructible functions above:

(i)

A constructible set
C\subset X({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
has a ‘characteristic function’
\bar{\delta}_{C}\in\mathop{\rm SF}(X)
.

(ii)

There are
R
-linear
pushforwards

\phi_{*}\colon\mathop{\rm SF}(X)\rightarrow\mathop{\rm SF}(Y)
by representable 1-morphisms
\phi\colon X\rightarrow Y
.

(iii)

There are
R
-linear
pullbacks

\theta^{*}\colon\mathop{\rm SF}(Y)\rightarrow\mathop{\rm SF}(X)
by finite type 1-morphisms
\theta\colon X\rightarrow Y
.

(iv)

The analogues of (
3.1
)–(
3.3
) hold. There are natural transformations
\mathop{\rm SF}(X)\rightarrow\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(X)
, defined using fibrewise Euler characteristics, which commute with pushforwards and pullbacks, and map
\bar{\delta}_{C}\mapsto\delta_{C}
in (i).

As in
[
48
,
49
,
50
,
51
,
52
,
53
]
, stack functions are useful for studying
motivic invariants
.

3.2
Ringel–Hall algebras and Lie algebras

Following
[
50
]
, we now explain how to define the
Ringel–Hall algebra
of a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear abelian category, using either constructible functions or stack functions. The next assumption sets out the data we need. It is similar to Assumption
2.4
, and often both hold at once.

Assumption 3.3
.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
be a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear abelian category coming from algebraic geometry or representation theory, e.g., we could take
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme, or
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
the category of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-representations of a quiver
Q
. Assume:

(a)

We can form a natural moduli stack
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
of objects
E
in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, an Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack, locally of finite type. Then
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-points of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
are isomorphism classes
[E]
of objects
E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, and the isotropy groups are
\mathop{\rm Iso}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\cal M}}([E])=\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)
.

(b)

We can form a natural moduli stack
\mathop{\mathfrak{Exact}}
of short exact sequences
E_{\bullet}=(0\rightarrow E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}\rightarrow E_{3}\rightarrow 0)
in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, an Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack, locally of finite type. There are 1-morphisms
\pi_{i}\colon\mathop{\mathfrak{Exact}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
for
i=1,2,3
acting by
\pi_{i}\colon E_{\bullet}\mapsto E_{i}
on
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-points. Here
\pi_{2}\colon\mathop{\mathfrak{Exact}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
is representable, and
(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})\colon\mathop{\mathfrak{Exact}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
is finite type.

(c)

We are given a surjective quotient
K_{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}})\twoheadrightarrow K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
of the Grothendieck group
K_{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}})
of
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. We write
\llbracket E\rrbracket\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
for the class of
E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. We require that the map
{\mathbin{\cal M}}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})\rightarrow K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
mapping
E\mapsto\llbracket E\rrbracket
should be locally constant. This gives a decomposition
{\mathbin{\cal M}}=\coprod_{\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}
of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
into open and closed
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-substacks
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\subset{\mathbin{\cal M}}
of objects in class
\alpha
. We also suppose that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{0}=\{[0]\}
, that is,
0\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is the only object in class
0\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
.

Define the
positive cone

C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\subset K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
by
C({\mathbin{\cal A}})=\bigl\{\llbracket E\rrbracket\colon 0\neq E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}\bigr\}
.

(d)

For all
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
there is a 2-commutative diagram in
\mathop{\bf Art}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
with all squares 2-Cartesian:

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.8472pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.8472pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 468.65317pt\raise-59.7708pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 131.0906pt\raise-73.1944pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})}$}}}\kern 332.92711pt\raise-74.68745pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 445.24785pt\raise-79.6944pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 445.24785pt\raise-79.6944pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-1.9861pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha+\beta+\gamma}.\!}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\end{gathered}

Here
\mathop{\mathfrak{Exact}}_{\alpha,\beta}
is the moduli stack of short exact sequences
0\rightarrow E_{\alpha}\rightarrow E_{\alpha+\beta}\rightarrow E_{\beta}\rightarrow 0
in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, where
\llbracket E_{\delta}\rrbracket=\delta
, and
{\mathbin{\cal N}\kern 0.39993pt}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
is the moduli stack of flags
E_{\alpha}\subset E_{\alpha+\beta}\subset E_{\alpha+\beta+\gamma}
of subobjects in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. In the language of
[
48
]
,
{\mathbin{\cal N}\kern 0.39993pt}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
is a moduli stack of
configurations
in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
.

Assumption
3.3
holds in large classes of interesting examples, as in
[
48
,
50
,
51
,
53
]
.

Definition 3.4
.

Suppose Assumption
3.3
holds. Following
[
50
, Section 4]
, define an
R
-bilinear product
*\colon\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})\times\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})\rightarrow\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
by

\displaystyle f*g=(\pi_{2})_{*}\circ(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})^{*}(f\boxtimes g),

using
\pi_{2}\colon\mathop{\mathfrak{Exact}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})\colon\mathop{\mathfrak{Exact}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
and the notation of Section
3.1
. Considering the commutative diagram from (
3.3
) and (
(d)
)

\textstyle{\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\!\times\!{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}\!\times\!{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\gamma})}
\scriptstyle{({\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}}\times(\pi_{1},\pi_{3}))^{*}}
\scriptstyle((\pi_{1},\pi_{3})\times{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\gamma}})^{*}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathfrak{Exact}}_{\alpha,\beta}\!\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\gamma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\Pi_{(\alpha,\beta),\gamma})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{(\pi_{2}\times{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\gamma}})_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha+\beta}\!\times\!{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\gamma})}
\scriptstyle{(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\!\times\!\mathop{\mathfrak{Exact}}_{\beta,\gamma})}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{\alpha,(\beta,\gamma)}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{({\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}}\times\pi_{2})_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal N}\kern 0.39993pt}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\Pi_{(\alpha+\beta,\gamma)})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{(\Pi_{(\alpha,\beta+\gamma)})_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathfrak{Exact}}_{\alpha+\beta,\gamma})}
\scriptstyle{(\pi_{2})_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\!\times\!{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta+\gamma})}
\scriptstyle{(\pi_{1},\pi_{3})^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathfrak{Exact}}_{\alpha,\beta+\gamma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\pi_{2})_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha+\beta+\gamma}),\!}

we find that
(f_{\alpha}*f_{\beta})*f_{\gamma}=f_{\alpha}*(f_{\beta}*f_{\gamma})
for
f_{\alpha},f_{\beta},f_{\gamma}\in\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
. Hence
\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
is an
associative
R
-algebra
, with product
*
and identity
\delta_{[0]}
, where
\delta_{[0]}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})\rightarrow R
is given by
\delta_{[0]}([E])=1
if
E=0
and
\delta_{[0]}([E])=0
otherwise.

The same definition makes the stack functions
\mathop{\rm SF}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
into an associative
R
-algebra. We call
\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
,
\mathop{\rm SF}({\mathbin{\cal M}})

Ringel–Hall algebras
, as the construction originated with the work of Ringel on Hall algebras
[
91
]
. Observe that as
*
is associative,
\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
,
\mathop{\rm SF}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
are also
Lie algebras
over
R
, with Lie bracket

[f,g]=f*g-g*f.

(3.10)

An object
E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is called
indecomposable
if we cannot write
E\cong E_{1}\oplus E_{2}
for
E_{1},E_{2}\not\cong 0
in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. A constructible function
f\in\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
is called
supported on indecomposables
if
f([E])\neq 0
for
[E]\in{\mathbin{\cal M}}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
implies that
E
is indecomposable. Write
\mathop{\rm CF^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})\subset\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
for the
R
-submodule of
f\in\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
supported on indecomposables. Then
[
50
, Theorem 4.9]
says that
\mathop{\rm CF^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
is closed under the Lie bracket (
3.10
), and so is a
Lie subalgebra
of
\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
. Note that
\mathop{\rm CF^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
is generally not closed under
*
.

In
[
50
, Section 5]
, the second author defines an
R
-subalgebra
\mathop{\rm SF_{al}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})\subset\mathop{\rm SF}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
of stack functions
with algebra stabilizers
, and a Lie subalgebra
\mathop{\rm SF_{al}^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})\subset\mathop{\rm SF_{al}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
of stack functions
supported on virtual indecomposables
, which is an analogue of the Lie subalgebra
\mathop{\rm CF^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})\subset\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
.

3.3
(Weak) stability conditions on abelian categories

Next we summarize some material from
[
51
]
. See also Rudakov
[
93
]
.

Definition 3.5
.

Suppose Assumption
3.3
holds. Let
(T,\leq)
be a totally ordered set and
\tau\colon C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\rightarrow T
be a map. We call
(\tau,T,\leq)
a
weak stability condition
on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
if for all
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
with
\beta=\alpha+\gamma
, either

\tau(\alpha)\leq\tau(\beta)\leq\tau(\gamma)
, or
\tau(\alpha)\geq\tau(\beta)\geq\tau(\gamma)
.

We call
(\tau,T,\leq)
a
stability condition
if for all such
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\gamma
, either

\tau(\alpha)<\tau(\beta)<\tau(\gamma)
, or
\tau(\alpha)>\tau(\beta)>\tau(\gamma)
, or
\tau(\alpha)=\tau(\beta)=\tau(\gamma)
.

Let
(\tau,T,\leq)
be a weak stability condition. An object
E
of
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is called

(i)

\tau
-stable
if
\tau([E^{\prime}])<\tau([E/E^{\prime}])
for all subobjects
E^{\prime}\subset E
with
E^{\prime}\neq 0,E
.

(ii)

\tau
-semistable
if
\tau([E^{\prime}])\!\leq\!\tau([E/E^{\prime}])
for all
E^{\prime}\subset E
with
E^{\prime}\neq 0,E
.

(iii)

\tau
-unstable
if it is not
\tau
-semistable.

(iv)

strictly
\tau
-semistable
if it is
\tau
-semistable but not
\tau
-stable.

If
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
,
\big(\tilde{\tau},\tilde{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
are weak stability conditions on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, we say that
\big(\tilde{\tau},\tilde{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)

dominates

(\tau,T,\leq)
if
\tau(\alpha)\leq\tau(\beta)
implies
\tilde{\tau}(\alpha)\leq\tilde{\tau}(\beta)
for all
\alpha,\beta\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
.

We call a weak stability condition
(\tau,T,\leq)
on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}

permissible
if

(a)

{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is
\tau
-
artinian
, that is, there are no infinite chains
\cdots\subsetneq E_{3}\subsetneq E_{2}\subsetneq E_{1}
of subobjects in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
with
\tau([E_{n+1}])\leq\tau([E_{n}/E_{n+1}])
for all
n=1,2,\dots
.

(b)

For each
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
, write
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)=\bigl\{[E]\in{\mathbin{\cal M}}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})\colon E
is
