\tau
-semistable,
\llbracket E\rrbracket=\alpha\bigr\}
. Then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)
is a constructible set, as in Definition
3.1
.

We also write
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)=\bigl\{[E]\in{\mathbin{\cal M}}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})\colon E
is
\tau
-stable,
\llbracket E\rrbracket=\alpha\bigr\}
.

Remark 3.6
.

(a)

As in
[
51
, Section 4.1]
, there are some theorems which hold for stability conditions but are false for weak stability conditions. However, we will not use any of these in this paper, so we will work with weak stability conditions.

(b)

In the examples we will be interested in,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\alpha}(\tau)
and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)
will actually be
finite type open substacks
in
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
, but for the theory of
[
51
,
53
]
it is sufficient to suppose only that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)
is a constructible set.

Example 3.7
.

For any
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
satisfying Assumption
3.3
, we may take
T=*
to be the point,
\leqslant\penalty
the unique order on
*
, and
\tau_{\rm tr}\colon C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\rightarrow*
to be the projection. Then
(\tau_{\rm tr},*,\leqslant\penalty)
is a stability condition on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, the
trivial stability condition
. Every
E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is
\tau_{\rm tr}
-semistable, so
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau_{\rm tr})={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
for
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
. Thus
(\tau_{\rm tr},*,\leqslant\penalty)
is permissible if and only if
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}
is of finite type for all
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
. The trivial stability condition dominates all weak stability conditions on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
.

The next two examples are taken from
[
51
, Sections 4.3–4.4]
.

Example 3.8
.

As explained in more detail in Section
5
, let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},h,t)
be a quiver, and
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
be the abelian category of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-representations of
Q
. Write objects of
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
as
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})=((V_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}},(\rho_{e})_{e\in Q_{1}})
, where
V_{v}
is a finite-dimensional
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space and
\rho_{e}\colon V_{t(e)}\rightarrow V_{h(e)}
a linear map. Define the
dimension vector
of
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
to be
\boldsymbol{d}={\mathbin{\bf dim}\kern 1.00006pt}(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\subset{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}}
, where
\boldsymbol{d}(v)=\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}V_{v}
for
v\in Q_{0}
. Define
K({\mathbin{\cal A}})={\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}}
to be the lattice of dimension vectors, with
\llbracket(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})\rrbracket={\mathbin{\bf dim}\kern 1.00006pt}(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
. Then the positive cone is
C({\mathbin{\cal A}})={\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
.

Choose real numbers
\mu_{v}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
for all
v\in Q_{0}
. Define a map
\mu\colon C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
by

\mu(\boldsymbol{d})=\frac{\sum_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)\mu_{v}}{\sum_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)}.

Then
[
51
, Example 4.14]
shows that
(\mu,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}},\leqslant\penalty)
is a permissible stability condition on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, called
slope stability
. We call
\mu
a
slope function
.

Example 3.9
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme of dimension
m
, and
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
the
abelian category of coherent sheaves on
X
. Let
K({\mathbin{\cal A}})=K^{\rm num}(\operatorname{coh}(X))
be the numerical Grothendieck group. Define
G
to be the set of monic rational polynomials in
t
of degree at most
m
:

G=\bigl\{p(t)=t^{d}+a_{d-1}t^{d-1}+\cdots+a_{0}\colon d=0,1,\dots,m,\,a_{0},\dots,a_{d-1}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}\bigr\}.

Define a total order ‘
\leqslant\penalty
’ on
G
by
p\leqslant\penalty p^{\prime}
for
p,p^{\prime}\in G
if
either

(a)

\deg p>\deg p^{\prime}
, or

(b)

\deg p=\deg p^{\prime}
and
p(t)\leqslant\penalty p^{\prime}(t)
for all
t\gg 0
.

We write
p<q
if
p\leqslant\penalty q
and
p\neq q
. Note that
\deg p>\deg p^{\prime}

in (a) implies that
p(t)>p^{\prime}(t)
for all
t\gg 0
, which is the
opposite to
p(t)\leqslant\penalty p^{\prime}(t)
for
t\gg 0
in (b).

Fix a very ample line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(1)
on
X
. For
E\in\operatorname{coh}(X)
, the
Hilbert polynomial

P_{E}
is the unique polynomial in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}[t]
such that
P_{E}(n)=\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits H^{0}(E(n))
for all
n\gg 0
. Equivalently,
P_{E}(n)=\bar{\chi}\bigl([{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(-n)],[E]\bigr)
for all
n\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
. Thus,
P_{E}
depends only on the class
\alpha\in K^{\rm num}(\operatorname{coh}(X))
of
E
, and we may write
P_{\alpha}
instead of
P_{E}
. Define
\tau\colon C(\operatorname{coh}(X))\rightarrow G
by
\tau(\alpha)=P_{\alpha}/r_{\alpha}
, where
P_{\alpha}
is the Hilbert polynomial of
\alpha
, and
r_{\alpha}
is the
leading coefficient of
P_{\alpha}
, which must be positive. Then as in

[
51
, Example 4.16]
,
(\tau,G,\leqslant\penalty)
is a permissible stability condition on

\operatorname{coh}(X)
, called
Gieseker stability
. Gieseker stability
is studied in
[
46
, Section 1.2]
.

3.4
Constructible and stack functions
\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{\alpha}(\tau)}
,
\boldsymbol{\bar{\epsilon}_{\alpha}(\tau)}

Definition 3.10
.

Suppose Assumption
3.3
holds, and let
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
be a permissible weak stability condition on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. Then for each
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
we have a constructible set
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\subset{\mathbin{\cal M}}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
, so as in Section
3.1
we have
\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}\in\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
and
\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}\in\mathop{\rm SF}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
, where in fact
\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}\in\mathop{\rm SF_{al}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
. Following
[
51
, Definitions 7.6 and 8.1]
, define elements
\epsilon^{\alpha}(\tau)\in\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
and
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha}(\tau)\in\mathop{\rm SF_{al}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
for
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
by

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\alpha}(\tau)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha,\,\tau(\alpha_{i})=\tau(\alpha),\text{ all
$i$}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}*\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}*\cdots*\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)},

(3.11)

\displaystyle\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha}(\tau)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha,\,\tau(\alpha_{i})=\tau(\alpha),\text{ all
$i$}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}*\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}*\cdots*\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)},

(3.12)

where
*
is the Ringel–Hall multiplication, and there are only finitely many nonzero terms in (
3.11
)–(
3.12
) by
[
51
, Definition 7.6]
. Then
[
51
, Theorems 7.7 and 8.2]
prove

\displaystyle\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha,\,\tau(\alpha_{i})=\tau(\alpha),\text{ all
$i$}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n!}\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)*\epsilon^{\alpha_{2}}(\tau)*\cdots*\epsilon^{\alpha_{n}}(\tau),

(3.13)

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha,\,\tau(\alpha_{i})=\tau(\alpha),\text{ all
$i$}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n!}\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)*\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha_{2}}(\tau)*\cdots*\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha_{n}}(\tau),

(3.14)

with only finitely many nonzero terms in (
3.13
)–(
3.14
). Formally, for each
t\in T
we may rewrite (
3.11
) and (
3.13
) as

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\tau(\alpha)=t}\epsilon^{\alpha}(\tau)=\log\Biggl[1+\sum_{\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\tau(\alpha)=t}\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}\Biggr],

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\tau(\alpha)=t}\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}=\exp\Biggl[\sum_{\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\tau(\alpha)=t}\epsilon^{\alpha}(\tau)\Biggr].

Then
[
51
, Theorems 7.8 and 8.7]
prove:

Theorem 3.11
.

In Definition
3.10
,
\epsilon^{\alpha}(\tau)
lies in the Lie subalgebra
\mathop{\rm CF^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})\!\subset\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
from Section
3.2
, and
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha}(\tau)
lies in the Lie subalgebra
\mathop{\rm SF_{al}^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})\subset\mathop{\rm SF_{al}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
.

Here is a way to think about the point of all this:

•

Knowing the
\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}
,
\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}
for
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
is equivalent to knowing the subsets of
\tau
-semistable objects
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\subset{\mathbin{\cal M}}({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
.

•

By (
3.11
)–(
3.14
), knowing the
\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}
,
\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}
for all
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
is equivalent to knowing the
\epsilon^{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha}(\tau)
for all
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
.

•

The
\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}
,
\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}
tend to satisfy identities in the algebras
(\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}}),*)
and
(\mathop{\rm SF_{al}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}}),*)
, but the
\epsilon^{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha}(\tau)
satisfy corresponding identities in the
Lie algebras

(\mathop{\rm CF^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}}),[\,,\,])
and
(\mathop{\rm SF_{al}^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}}),[\,,\,])
.

•

Thus, working with the
\epsilon^{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha}(\tau)
may be useful if we want to use a construction which works for Lie algebras, but not for associative algebras.

This occurs in Donaldson–Thomas theory of Calabi–Yau 3-folds
[
60
, Section 5.3]
, where there is a Lie algebra morphism from
\mathop{\rm SF_{al}^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
which does not extend to
\mathop{\rm SF_{al}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
. It also occurred in Section
2.3
, when we had a Lie algebra structure on
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
, but no corresponding associative algebra.

3.5
Wall-crossing under change of stability condition

In
[
53
]
the second author proved transformation laws for the

\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}
,
\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}
and
\epsilon^{\alpha}(\tau)
,
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha}(\tau)
under change of stability condition. These involve combinatorial coefficients

S(*;\tau,\tilde{\tau})\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
U(*;\tau,\tilde{\tau})\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
defined in

[
53
, Section 4.1]
. Following
[
60
, Section 3.3]
, we have changed some notation
from
[
53
]
.

Definition 3.12
.

Suppose Assumption
3.3
holds, and let

(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
,
\big(\tilde{\tau},\tilde{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
be weak stability conditions on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
.

Let
n\geqslant 1
and
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
. If for all

i=1,\dots,n-1
we have either

(a)

\tau(\alpha_{i})\leqslant\penalty\tau(\alpha_{i+1})
and

\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i})>\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{i+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n})
, or

(b)

\tau(\alpha_{i})>\tau(\alpha_{i+1})
and
\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{i})\leqslant\penalty\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{i+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n})
,

then define
S(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})=(-1)^{r}
,
where
r
is the number
of
i=1,\dots,n-1
satisfying (a). Otherwise define

S(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})=0
. Now
define

\displaystyle U(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})=

(3.15)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phantom{wiggle}\\
1\leqslant\penalty l\leqslant\penalty m\leqslant\penalty n,\,0=a_{0}<a_{1}<\cdots<a_{m}=n,\,0=b_{0}<b_{1}<\cdots<b_{l}=m\colon\\
\text{Define $\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{m}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})$ by
$\beta_{i}=\alpha_{a_{i-1}+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{a_{i}}$}.\\
\text{Define $\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{l}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})$ by
$\gamma_{i}=\beta_{b_{i-1}+1}+\cdots+\beta_{b_{i}}$}.\\
\text{We require $\tau(\beta_{i})=\tau(\alpha_{j})$, $i=1,\dots,m$,
$a_{i-1}<j\leqslant\penalty a_{i}$},\\
\text{and $\tilde{\tau}(\gamma_{i})=\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n})$,
$i=1,\dots,l$}\end{subarray}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}\begin{aligned} \frac{(-1)^{l-1}}{l}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{l}S(\beta_{b_{i-1}+1},\beta_{b_{i-1}+2},\dots,\beta_{b_{i}};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{1}{(a_{i}-a_{i-1})!}&.\end{aligned}

Here are some properties of the coefficients
U(-)
. Equations (
3.16
)–(
3.17
) come from
[
53
, Theorem 4.8]
, and (
3.18
) follows from (
3.15
) and
[
53
, equation (60)]
.

Theorem 3.13
.

Let Assumption
3.3
hold
, and

(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
,
\big(\hat{\tau},\hat{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
,
\big(\tilde{\tau},\tilde{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
be weak stability conditions on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. Then for all
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
we have

\displaystyle U(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tau)=\begin{cases}1,&n=1,\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(3.16)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,\,a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\colon m=1,\dots,n,\\
0=a_{0}<a_{1}<\cdots<a_{m}=n,\\
\text{set $\beta_{k}=\alpha_{a_{k-1}+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{a_{k}}$},\\
k=1,\dots,m\end{subarray}}\,\begin{aligned} &U(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{m};\hat{\tau},\tilde{\tau})\prod_{k=1}^{m}U(\alpha_{a_{k-1}+1},\alpha_{a_{k-1}+2},\dots,\alpha_{a_{k}};\tau,\hat{\tau})\\
&\qquad=U(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau}).\end{aligned}\end{aligned}

(3.17)

If also
\big(\tilde{\tau},\tilde{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
dominates
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
, as in Definition
3.5
, then

U(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})=U(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tilde{\tau},\tau)=0\qquad\text{unless $\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{n})$}.

(3.18)

Then in
[
53
, Theorem 5.2]
the second author derives wall-crossing formulae under change of stability condition from
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
to
\big(\tilde{\tau},\tilde{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
:

Theorem 3.14
.

Let Assumption
3.3
hold, and
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
,

\big(\tilde{\tau},\tilde{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
be permissible weak stability conditions on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. Suppose also that there exists a permissible weak stability condition
\big(\hat{\tau},\hat{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
which dominates both
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
,
\big(\tilde{\tau},\tilde{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
in the sense of Definition
3.5
. Then for all
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
we have

\displaystyle\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\tau})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha\end{subarray}}S(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}*\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}*\cdots*\delta_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)},

(3.19)

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\tau})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha\end{subarray}}S(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}*\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}*\cdots*\bar{\delta}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)},

(3.20)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha\end{subarray}}U(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)*\epsilon^{\alpha_{2}}(\tau)*\cdots*\epsilon^{\alpha_{n}}(\tau),

(3.21)

\displaystyle\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha\end{subarray}}U(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha_{1}}(\tau)*\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha_{2}}(\tau)*\cdots*\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha_{n}}(\tau),

(3.22)

where there are only finitely many nonzero terms in
(
3.19
)–(
3.22
).

Here the third weak stability condition
\big(\hat{\tau},\hat{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
does not enter (
3.19
)–(
3.22
), but is used to prove that there are only finitely many nonzero terms. For the case of quivers in Sections
5
–
6
, we may take
\big(\hat{\tau},\hat{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
to be the trivial stability condition
(\tau_{\rm tr},*,\leqslant\penalty)
in Example
3.7
, which dominates any
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
,
\big(\tilde{\tau},\tilde{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
.

Theorem
3.13
implies that using (
3.21
)–(
3.22
) to transform from
\epsilon^{*}(\tau)
,
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{*}(\tau)
to
\epsilon^{*}(\hat{\tau})
,
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{*}(\hat{\tau})
, and then to transform from
\epsilon^{*}(\hat{\tau})
,
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{*}(\hat{\tau})
to
\epsilon^{*}(\tilde{\tau})
,
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{*}(\tilde{\tau})
, is equivalent to transforming from
\epsilon^{*}(\tau)
,
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{*}(\tau)
to
\epsilon^{*}(\tilde{\tau})
,
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{*}(\tilde{\tau})
, as you would expect.

The next result is proved in
[
53
, Theorem 5.4]
. We have no explicit definition for
\tilde{U}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\allowbreak\tau,\tilde{\tau})
, we only show that (
3.22
) can be rewritten in the form (
3.23
).

Theorem 3.15
.

In Theorem
3.14
, equations (
3.21
)–(
3.22
) may be rewritten as equations in the Lie algebras
\mathop{\rm CF^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
,
\mathop{\rm SF_{al}^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
using the Lie brackets
[\,,\,]
, rather than as equations in
\mathop{\rm CF}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
,
\mathop{\rm SF_{al}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
using the Ringel–Hall product
*
. That is, we
may rewrite (
3.22
), and similarly (
3.21
), in the form

\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha}(\tilde{\tau})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha\end{subarray}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!}\tilde{U}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\,\cdot[[\cdots[[\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha_{1}}(\tau),\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha_{2}}(\tau)],\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha_{3}}(\tau)],\dots],\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha_{n}}(\tau)],

(3.23)

for some system of combinatorial coefficients
\tilde{U}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
, with only finitely many nonzero terms, such that if we expand
[f,g]=f*g-g*f
then
(
3.23
)
becomes
(
3.22
)
.

Alternatively, we may interpret
(
3.21
)–(
3.22
)
as holding in the universal enveloping algebras
U(\mathop{\rm CF^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}}))
,
U(\mathop{\rm SF_{al}^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}}))
.

It will be very important later that (
3.21
)–(
3.23
) are
universal wall-crossing formulae in a Lie algebra
. So we can make sense of the same wall-crossing formulae in the Lie algebras
\check{H}_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
of Section
2.4
.

These results are applied in
[
53
, Sections 6.4–6.5]
and
[
60
]
in the following way. Suppose we can define a Lie algebra morphism
\Psi\colon\mathop{\rm SF_{al}^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})\rightarrow L_{K({\mathbin{\cal A}})}
, where
L_{K({\mathbin{\cal A}})}
is an explicit Lie algebra, often of the form
\langle\lambda^{\alpha}\colon\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})\rangle_{R}
for some commutative ring
R
, with Lie bracket
[\lambda^{\alpha},\lambda^{\beta}]=c_{\alpha,\beta}\lambda^{\alpha+\beta}
for coefficients
c_{\alpha,\beta}
in
R
. Then we may define motivic invariants
J^{\alpha}(\tau)\in R
by
\Psi(\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\alpha}(\tau))=J^{\alpha}(\tau)\lambda^{\alpha}
. Applying
\Psi
to (
3.22
), interpreted using Theorem
3.15
, then gives a wall-crossing formula for the invariants
J^{\alpha}(\tau)
. This is used in
[
60
, Theorem 3.14]
to prove a wall-crossing formula for Donaldson–Thomas invariants of Calabi–Yau 3-folds.

4
A conjectural picture of universal structures

for enumerative invariant theories

4.1
General statement of the conjecture

We first describe our conjectural picture, in a way that tries to unify several rather different contexts in algebraic geometry and differential geometry. Sections
4.3
–
4.6
will explain these contexts. Our initial statement will be imprecise, with details added in Sections
4.3
–
4.6
. We write it as Assumption
4.1
followed by Conjecture
4.2
. The assumption covers material which is basically already known, and we can provide explicit definitions and proofs for (much of them in
[
58
]
).

Assumption 4.1
.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
be a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear additive category. Some examples we have in mind are, in algebraic geometry,
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
or
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme; in representation theory,
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
or
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=D^{b}\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
for
Q
a quiver without oriented cycles; and in differential geometry,
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
could be the category of pairs
(E,\nabla_{E})
for
E\rightarrow X
a unitary complex vector bundle on a fixed compact manifold
X
, and
\nabla_{E}
a connection on
E
. Assume:

(a)

We can form two natural moduli spaces
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
of objects
E
in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, where the usual moduli space
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
parametrizes such
E
up to isomorphism, and the ‘projective linear’ moduli space
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
parametrizes
E
up to isomorphisms modulo
{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}\cdot{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{E}
. There is a morphism
\Pi\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
.

Here
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
may be Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stacks, or higher
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stacks, or derived
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stacks, or topological stacks, or topological spaces up to homotopy equivalence, depending on the context.

There is a morphism
\Phi\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
mapping
([E],[F])\mapsto[E\oplus F]
.

(b)

We are given a surjective quotient
K_{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}})\twoheadrightarrow K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
of the Grothendieck group
K_{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}})
of
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. We write
\llbracket E\rrbracket\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
for the class of
E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. There should be splittings
{\mathbin{\cal M}}=\coprod_{\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}=\coprod_{\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
such that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
parametrize objects in class
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
. We write
\Phi_{\alpha,\beta}=\Phi|_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha+\beta}
.

(c)

We are given a symmetric biadditive form
\chi\colon K({\mathbin{\cal A}})\times K({\mathbin{\cal A}})\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

(d)

We are given some natural additional geometric structures
{\mathbin{\cal G}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal G}}^{\rm pl}
on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
\allowbreak{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
. In the case of abelian categories in algebraic geometry, for
{\mathbin{\cal G}}
we mean the data
\Psi
,
\Theta^{\bullet}
in Assumption
2.4
.

Using
{\mathbin{\cal G}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal G}}^{\rm pl}
, there are notions of
real virtual dimension
of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
, with
\mathop{\rm vdim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}=-\chi(\alpha,\alpha)
and
\mathop{\rm vdim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}=2-\chi(\alpha,\alpha)
for
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
.

(e)

There is a notion of
orientation
on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
, defined using the geometric structures
{{\cal G}}
,
{{\cal G}}^{\rm pl}
. In some contexts the complex geometry induces a natural orientation, just as a complex manifold has a natural orientation considered as a real manifold, so issues of orientations can largely be ignored. In other contexts there is no natural choice. The morphism
\Pi\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
identifies orientations on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
.

We suppose that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
is orientable (this can often be proved), and that an orientation has been chosen for
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
, the natural one if this is defined. We write
o_{\alpha}
,
o_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
for the orientations on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
.

(f)

The morphism
\Phi\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
has a natural relative orientation, so orientations on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
pull back under
\Phi
to orientations on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
. Using this, there are signs
\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}\in\{\pm 1\}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
with
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha},{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}\neq\varnothing
such that
o_{\alpha}\boxtimes o_{\beta}=\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}\cdot\Phi^{*}(o_{\alpha+\beta})
. These
\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}
satisfy (
2.2
)–(
2.4
).

(g)

Define
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
to be
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
with grading shifted as in (
2.12
). Then, as described in
[
58
]
, and in Section
2.3
for abelian categories
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
in algebraic geometry, using the data
{\mathbin{\cal G}}
and signs
\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}
in (d),(f) we can make
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
into a
graded vertex algebra over

R
.

(h)

Define
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
to be
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
with grading shifted as in (
2.15
). Then, as described in
[
58
]
, and in Section
2.4
for abelian categories
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
in algebraic geometry, using the data
{\mathbin{\cal G}}^{\rm pl}
and signs
\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}
in (d), (f) we can make
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
into a
graded Lie algebra over

R
. Thus
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
is a Lie algebra.

Note that in many situations one can compute
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
and
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
very explicitly (often
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
is a lattice vertex algebra), and they are not difficult to work with in examples.

(i)

There is a notion of
stability condition

\tau
on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, which induces notions of when objects
E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
are
\tau
-
stable
or
\tau
-
semistable
. For example:

\bullet

When
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is an abelian category in algebraic geometry, we take
\tau
to be a permissible weak stability condition as in Section
3.3
.

\bullet

When
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is a triangulated category such as
D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
or
D^{b}\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, we may take
\tau
to be a suitable Bridgeland stability condition.

\bullet

When
X
is a compact oriented 4-manifold with
b^{2}_{+}(X)=1
, and we wish to study Donaldson theory on
X
, and
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is the category of unitary complex vector bundles
E\rightarrow X
with connections
\nabla_{E}
, a stability condition is a Riemannian metric
g
on
X
, which induces a splitting
H^{2}_{\rm dR}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})={\mathbin{\cal H}}^{2}_{+}\oplus{\mathbin{\cal H}}^{2}_{-}
of the de Rham 2-cohomology into harmonic self-dual and anti-self-dual forms, with
{\mathbin{\cal H}}^{2}_{+}=\langle\omega_{+}\rangle
. Then
(E,\nabla)
is
\tau
-
semistable
if
F_{\nabla_{E}}^{+}=ic\cdot{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{E}\otimes\omega_{+}
for
c\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
. We call
(E,\nabla)

\tau
-
stable
if it is
\tau
-semistable and irreducible.

(j)

If
\tau
is a stability condition on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, we form moduli spaces
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
of
\tau
-(semi)stable objects
E\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
in each class
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
.

Here
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
is compact (or has a compact ‘coarse moduli space’), and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)
has the structure of a ‘virtual manifold’ (for example,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)
may be a smooth
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme, or a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme with perfect obstruction theory
[
6
]
, or a smooth manifold, or a derived smooth manifold
[
54
,
55
,
56
,
57
]
). The orientation on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
in (e) induces an orientation on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)
.

Thus, if
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
(that is, if there are no strictly
\tau
-semistables in class
\alpha
) then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
is a compact, oriented virtual manifold of virtual dimension
\mathop{\rm vdim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)=2-\chi(\alpha,\alpha)
, so it has
virtual class

[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
in homology
H_{2-\chi(\alpha,\alpha)}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big)
, which is
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big)
by (
2.15
).

In fact
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
may be defined in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-homology
H_{2-\chi(\alpha,\alpha)}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})
.

As in Sections
4.3
–
4.6
and
[
58
,
59
]
, there are many interesting enumerative invariant theories in which we can define all the data of Assumption
4.1
.

The following conjecture should not be regarded as a precise statement, nor are we claiming that it should hold in every theory for which some version of Assumption
4.1
holds. It is intended as an outline of a general structure we expect to see in many enumerative invariant theories, which may need modification in particular cases. We make some more exact conjectures in Sections
4.3
–
4.6
.

Conjecture 4.2
.

Suppose Assumption
4.1
holds. Then:

(i)

For all
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
we may define invariants
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\in\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big)
. If
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
then Assumption
4.1
(j) gives a virtual class
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
in
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big)
, and then
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\allowbreak=[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
. It is crucial that
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big)=H_{2-\chi(\alpha,\alpha)}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big)
is homology over a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-
algebra

R
, as
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
may exist in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-homology but not in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-homology.

(ii)

Let
\tau
,
\tilde{\tau}
be stability conditions on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
as in Assumption
4.1
(i). It may be necessary to impose a condition on
\tau
,
\tilde{\tau}
to ensure that (
4.1
)–(
4.2
) below have only finitely many nonzero terms – see the notion of ‘globally finite change of stability condition’ in
[
53
, Definition 5.1]
. Roughly, this says that
\tau
,
\tilde{\tau}
are sufficiently close in the space of stability conditions on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
.

Then for all
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
(or in a ‘positive cone’
C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\subset K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
), the analogue of (
3.23
) holds in the Lie algebra
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
from Assumption
4.1
(h):

\begin{gathered}{}[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\tau})]_{\rm inv}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha\end{subarray}}\begin{aligned} \tilde{U}(\alpha_{1},&\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\bigl[\bigl[\cdots\bigl[[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv},\\
&[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\bigr],\dots\bigr],[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\bigr].\end{aligned}\end{gathered}

(4.1)

Equivalently, as in Theorem
3.15
, the analogue of (
3.22
) holds in the universal enveloping algebra
\bigl(U\big(\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\big),*\bigr)
:

\begin{gathered}{}[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\tau})]_{\rm inv}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha\end{subarray}}\begin{aligned} U(\alpha_{1},&\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}*\\
&[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{2}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}*\cdots*[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}.\end{aligned}\end{gathered}

(4.2)

We call (
4.1
)–(
4.2
)
wall-crossing formulae
for the invariants
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
.

We have only defined the
U(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})
,
\tilde{U}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})
in Section
3.5
in the abelian category case, in which the ‘positive cone’
C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
makes sense. There should be a way to extend the theory to triangulated categories such as
D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X),D^{b}\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. Then we no longer have a good definition of ‘positive cone’
C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\subset K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
, so it is more difficult both to define the
U(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})
, and to ensure that (
4.1
)–(
4.2
) have only finitely many nonzero terms. This extension was discussed in
[
53
, Problem 7.1]
.

(iii)

We currently have no
direct
definition of the invariants
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
in (i) when
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)\neq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
, starting from the moduli space
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
and the geometric structures
{{\cal G}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
,
o_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
upon it. However, in many cases there is an
indirect
definition, by making use of the wall-crossing formulae (
4.1
)–(
4.2
) in an auxiliary category
{\mathbin{\cal B}}
. We call this the
method of pair invariants
, and it is used in
[
60
, Sections 5.4 and 13.1]
to define Donaldson–Thomas invariants of Calabi–Yau 3-folds. (These are used by Tanaka–Thomas
[
97
]
to study Vafa–Witten invariants. See also Mochizuki’s use of ‘
L
-Bradlow pairs’ in
[
79
, Section 7.3]
.) When it applies, it shows that classes
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
satisfying (i),(ii) must be unique. We explain it in the next definition.

Definition 4.3
.

Let Assumption
4.1
and Conjecture
4.2
(i), (ii) hold for
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. For clarity, suppose also that
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear abelian category in algebraic geometry as in Assumption
3.1
, and that ‘stability conditions’
\tau
in Assumption
4.1
(i) mean permissible weak stability conditions
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
in Section
3.3
.

To use the
method of pair invariants
, we should construct a second abelian category
{\mathbin{\cal B}}
satisfying Assumption
4.1
and Conjecture
4.2
(i), (ii), such that:

(i)

There is a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear inclusion
i\colon{\mathbin{\cal A}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbin{\cal B}}
as a full abelian subcategory.

(ii)

There is a distinguished object
I\in{\mathbin{\cal B}}
, with
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\cal B}}(I,I)={\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
. Every object
B\in{\mathbin{\cal B}}
fits into an exact sequence in
{\mathbin{\cal B}}
, unique up to isomorphism:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V\otimes_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

(4.3)

where
A\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
and
V
is a finite-dimensional
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space, so that
V\otimes_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}I\cong{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0ptI\oplus I\oplus\cdots\oplus I}\limits^{{\ulcorner\hskip 8.19447pt\text{$n$ copies }\hskip 8.19447pt\urcorner}}}}
if
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}V=n
.

(iii)

There is an identification
K({\mathbin{\cal B}})=K({\mathbin{\cal A}})\oplus{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
with
\llbracket B\rrbracket=\bigl(\llbracket A\rrbracket,\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}V\bigr)
in (
4.3
). Then
C({\mathbin{\cal B}})=\bigl((C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\amalg\{0\})\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}\bigr)\setminus\{(0,0)\}
.

(iv)

Writing
\chi
,
\tilde{\chi}
for
\chi
in Assumption
4.1
(c) for
{\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathbin{\cal B}}
, we have

\chi(\alpha,\beta)=\tilde{\chi}((\alpha,0),(\beta,0))
for
\alpha,\beta\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
and
\tilde{\chi}((0,m),(0,n))=2mn
for
m,n\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

(v)

Write
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
for the moduli spaces for
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, and
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}
,
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}^{\rm pl}
for the moduli spaces for
{\mathbin{\cal B}}
. Then the inclusion
i\colon{\mathbin{\cal A}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbin{\cal B}}
in (i) induces morphisms
i\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}
,
i^{\rm pl}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}^{\rm pl}
, which are isomorphisms
i\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,0)}
and
i^{\rm pl}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,0)}^{\rm pl}
for
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
. These isomorphisms identify the extra data
{{\cal G}}_{\alpha}
,
{{\cal G}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
and
\tilde{{\cal G}}{}_{(\alpha,0)}
,
\tilde{{\cal G}}{}_{(\alpha,0)}^{\rm pl}
in Assumption
4.1
(d) for
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal B}}
, and the orientations
o_{\alpha}
,
o_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
and
\tilde{o}{}_{(\alpha,0)}
,
\tilde{o}{}_{(\alpha,0)}^{\rm pl}
in Assumption
4.1
(e). The signs
\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}
,
\tilde{\epsilon}_{\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta}}
in Assumption
4.1
(f) satisfy
\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}=\tilde{\epsilon}_{(\alpha,0),(\beta,0)}
.

(vi)

i_{*}\colon\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\rightarrow\hat{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}\big)
and
i_{*}^{\rm pl}\colon\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\rightarrow\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}^{\rm pl}\big)
should be morphisms of the graded vertex and Lie algebras in Assumption
4.1
(g), (h).

For example, as in
[
60
, Section 5.4]
, if
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme, we may define
{\mathbin{\cal B}}
to be the category of morphisms
\phi\colon V\otimes_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(n)\rightarrow A
for
n\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
fixed, and
V
a finite-dimensional
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space, and
A\in\operatorname{coh}(X)
.

Now suppose
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
is a permissible weak stability condition on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. Define permissible weak stability conditions
(\tau_{+},T_{+},\leqslant\penalty)
and
(\tau_{-},T_{-},\leqslant\penalty)
on
{\mathbin{\cal B}}
, such that
T_{+}=(T\times\{0,1\})\amalg\{+\infty\}
and
T_{-}=(T\times\{-1,0\})\amalg\{-\infty\}
, where:

•

The order
\leqslant\penalty
on
T_{+}
is given by
(t_{1},n_{1})\leqslant\penalty(t_{2},n_{2})
for
(t_{1},n_{1})
,
(t_{2},n_{2})
in
T\times\{0,1\}
if either
t_{1}<t_{2}
in
T
, or
t_{1}=t_{2}
and
n_{1}\leqslant\penalty n_{2}
, and
(t,n)<+\infty
for all
(t,n)\in T\times\{0,1\}
.

•

The order
\leqslant\penalty
on
T_{-}
is given by
(t_{1},n_{1})\leqslant\penalty(t_{2},n_{2})
for
(t_{1},n_{1})
,
(t_{2},n_{2})
in
T\times\{-1,0\}
if either
t_{1}<t_{2}
in
T
, or
t_{1}=t_{2}
and
n_{1}\leqslant\penalty n_{2}
, and
-\infty<(t,n)
for all
(t,n)\in T\times\{-1,0\}
.

•

If
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
then
\tau_{\pm}(\alpha,0)=(\tau(\alpha),0)
and
\tau_{\pm}(\alpha,n)=(\tau(\alpha),\pm 1)
for
n>0
.

•

If
n>0
then
\tau_{\pm}((0,n))=\pm\infty\in T_{\pm}
.

We should then be able to easily prove that:

(a)

If
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
then
i^{\rm pl}
identifies
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
with
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,0)}^{\rm ss}(\tau_{\pm})
. Hence
i_{*}^{\rm pl}
maps
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\mapsto\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,0)}^{\rm ss}(\tau_{\pm})\big]_{\rm inv}
.

(b)

{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm st}(\tau_{\pm})={\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm pl}=\{[I]\}
is a point, and
\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm ss}(\tau_{\pm})\big]_{\rm inv}=1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 5.7361pt\widetilde{\kern-5.7361pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.27773pt}\kern 3.27773pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm pl})}
in
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm pl}\big)=H_{0}\big({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm pl}\big)=R
.

(c)

If
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
then
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,1)}^{\rm ss}(\tau_{-})=\varnothing
, as if
B\in{\mathbin{\cal B}}
with
\llbracket B\rrbracket=(\alpha,1)
then (
4.3
) gives a subobject
i(A)\subset B
with
B/i(A)\cong I
, and
\tau_{-}(i(A))>-\infty=\tau_{-}(I)
, so
B
is
\tau_{-}
-unstable by Definition
3.5
. Hence
\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,1)}^{\rm ss}(\tau_{-})\big]_{\rm virt}=0
.

(d)

If
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
then
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,1)}^{\rm st}(\tau_{+})={\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,1)}^{\rm ss}(\tau_{+})
, as if
B\in{\mathbin{\cal B}}
with
\llbracket B\rrbracket=(\alpha,1)
then there can exist no subobjects
0\neq B^{\prime}\subsetneq B
with
\tau_{+}(B^{\prime})=\tau_{+}(B/B^{\prime})
. Hence the virtual class
\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,1)}^{\rm ss}(\tau_{+})\big]_{\rm virt}
is defined in Assumption
4.1
(j), without using Conjecture
4.2
.

Now consider the wall-crossing formula (
4.1
) for
{\mathbin{\cal B}}
with
(\alpha,1)
,
\allowbreak\tau_{-}
,
\allowbreak\tau_{+}
in place of
\alpha
,
\tau
,
\tilde{\tau}
, for
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
. It turns out that this may be written

\displaystyle\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,1)}^{\rm ss}(\tau_{+})\big]_{\rm virt}=

(4.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\;\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha,\\
\tau(\alpha_{i})=\tau(\alpha),\;i=1,\dots,n\end{subarray}}\!\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\cdot\bigl[\bigl[\cdots\bigl[\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm ss}(\tau_{-})\big]_{\rm inv},\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha_{1},0)}^{\rm ss}(\tau_{-})\big]_{\rm inv}\bigr],\dots\bigr],\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha_{n},0)}^{\rm ss}(\tau_{-})\big]_{\rm inv}\bigr].

This holds because using
[
60
, Proposition 13.8]
one can show that

\displaystyle U\bigl((\alpha_{1},0),\dots,(\alpha_{k-1},0),(0,1),(\alpha_{k},0),\dots,(\alpha_{n},0);\tau_{-},\tau_{+})

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\begin{cases}\dfrac{(-1)^{n-k}}{(k-1)!(n-k)!},&\text{if $\tau(\alpha_{i})=\tau(\alpha)$, $i=1,\dots,n$},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

and then equation (
4.4
) follows from (
4.2
) as in
[
60
, Proposition 13.10]
.

Using (a),(b) above, let us rewrite (
4.4
) as

\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,1)}^{\rm ss}(\tau_{+})\big]_{\rm virt}=\bigl[i_{*}^{\rm pl}\bigl([{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\bigr),1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 5.7361pt\widetilde{\kern-5.7361pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.27773pt}\kern 3.27773pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm pl})}\bigr]+\text{lower order terms},

(4.5)

where the ‘lower order terms’ are those with
n\geqslant 2
in the sum in (
4.4
). Our goal is to define
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
uniquely in the case when
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)\neq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
. We do this by induction on
\alpha
in some order in
C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
compatible with addition, for instance by induction on
\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits\alpha=1,2,\dots
if this is defined. Then by induction we can suppose the ‘lower order terms’ are uniquely defined. The left-hand side of (
4.5
) is determined by (d) above. Hence in the inductive step,
\bigl[i_{*}^{\rm pl}\bigl([{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\bigr),1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 5.7361pt\widetilde{\kern-5.7361pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.27773pt}\kern 3.27773pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm pl})}\bigr]
is uniquely defined. If we have chosen
{\mathbin{\cal B}}
such that
\big[{-},1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 5.7361pt\widetilde{\kern-5.7361pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.27773pt}\kern 3.27773pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm pl})}\big]
is injective, then as
i_{*}^{\rm pl}
is an isomorphism, this shows
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
is uniquely determined.

There is an alternative version of the method of pair invariants in which we replace (
4.3
) by the exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V\otimes_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

and exchange
(\tau_{+},T_{+},\leqslant\penalty)
and
(\tau_{-},T_{-},\leqslant\penalty)
in the argument above.

Remark 4.4
.

In defining enumerative invariants in algebraic or differential geometry, there are usually three main difficulties:

(a)

transversality
, whether we can make the moduli spaces smooth.

(b)

compactness
, whether the moduli spaces are compact, or can be compactified by including singular solutions.

(c)

strictly semistable
or
reducible
points in the moduli spaces, which cause problems with the definition of virtual classes when
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)\neq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
.

An important aspect of Conjecture
4.2
is that it offers a new, universal, systematic approach to problem (c).

With a proper understanding of obstruction theories
[
6
]
, derived algebraic geometry
[
102
,
103
,
104
,
105
,
106
]
, and derived differential geometry
[
54
,
55
,
56
,
57
]
, part (a) is really not a problem (though people still make a fuss about it): virtual classes are well defined and well behaved even when moduli spaces are not transverse.

For (b), in algebraic geometry we usually get compactness for free by considering moduli spaces of the right kind of objects (for example, torsion-free sheaves rather than vector bundles). In differential geometry, compactifying moduli spaces by singular solutions usually involves difficult analytic issues. We have nothing new to say about this, we will just assume it works.

A common approach to problem (c) is to avoid it, by only considering classes
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
for which
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
, or by restricting to
\alpha
for which there are very few strictly
\tau
-semistable points in
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
, which can be understood and dealt with ‘by hand’. Some examples of this: Thomas
[
100
]
originally defined Donaldson–Thomas invariants of Calabi–Yau 3-folds only when
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
. In Donaldson theory of 4-manifolds
[
23
]
, almost all work focusses on
\mathop{\rm SU}(2)
- or
\mathop{\rm SO}(3)
-instantons, rather than
G
-instantons for other Lie groups
G
, and on simply-connected 4-manifolds. This is because reducibles for
\mathop{\rm SU}(2)
- or
\mathop{\rm SO}(3)
-instantons come from line bundles, and are easily understood. Similar comments apply to Casson invariants of 3-manifolds
[
2
,
98
]
, in which restricting to
\mathop{\rm SU}(2)
and to homology 3-spheres is used to control reducibles.

However, if we wish to study wall-crossing for enumerative invariants, we cannot restrict to
\alpha
with
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
, as the wall-crossing may involve other moduli spaces
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
with
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}^{\rm st}(\tau)\neq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
. Conjecture
4.2
aims to solve problem (c) of defining invariants in the presence of strictly semistables/reducibles, and understanding their wall-crossing, simultaneously, and Conjecture
4.2
(iii) uses the wall-crossing formula to define the invariants.

Remark 4.5

(on orientations)
.

In contexts in which we are free to choose the orientation
o_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
in Assumption
4.1
(e), we can change the sign of each invariant
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
arbitrarily by changing the sign of
o_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
.

This may seem to contradict the identities (
4.1
), (
4.2
), (
4.4
) which mix
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
for different
\alpha
. However, changing the
o_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}
changes the
\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}
in Assumption
4.1
(f), and the Lie bracket
[\,,\,]
on
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
and product
*
on
U\big(\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\big)
used in (
4.1
), (
4.2
), (
4.4
) depend on the
\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}
. The combined effect of the sign changes on
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
and
[\,,\,],*
cancels out.

As in
[
58
]
, we can set the theory up in an orientation-independent way, by replacing
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
by homology
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl},O^{\rm pl}\big)
twisted by a principal
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}_{2}
-bundle
O^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
of orientations on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
, which is assumed to be trivializable (though not canonically trivial) in Assumption
4.1
(e). Then
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv},[\,,\,],*
exist in and on
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl},O^{\rm pl}\big)
canonically, without having to choose orientations.

4.2
Rewriting the wall-crossing formula in the style of Kontsevich–Soibelman

The second author developed the wall-crossing story for motivic invariants in Section
3
and
[
48
,
49
,
50
,
51
,
52
,
53
]
in 2003–2005, and applied it to Donaldson–Thomas invariants of Calabi–Yau 3-folds in 2008
[
60
]
. Independently, Kontsevich and Soibelman
[
66
, Section 1.4]
in 2008 wrote down their own wall-crossing formula for motivic Donaldson–Thomas invariants, which is equivalent to a special case of (
3.19
)–(
3.20
) in a suitable associative algebra. Kontsevich and Soibelman’s version has proved more popular with subsequent authors, possibly because the coefficients
S(-)
,
U(-)
in (
3.19
)–(
3.22
) are not easy to understand and compute.

We now explain how to rewrite our conjectured wall-crossing formulae (
4.1
)–(
4.2
) in the style of Kontsevich–Soibelman
[
66
, Section 1.4]
. We work in the universal enveloping algebra
U\big(\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\big)
, an associative
R
-algebra with product
*
. As
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\subset U\big(\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\big)
, the invariants
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
in Conjecture
4.2
(i) lie in
U\big(\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\big)
, as we used in equation (
4.2
).

In the situation of Assumption
4.1
and Conjecture
4.2
(i), in an analogue of (
3.13
)–(
3.14
), define elements
\delta^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)\in U\big(\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\big)
for
\alpha\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
by

\delta^{\rm ss}_{\alpha}(\tau)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha,\,\tau(\alpha_{i})=\tau(\alpha),\text{ all
$i$}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n!}[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}*\cdots*[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}.

(4.6)

As for (
3.11
)–(
3.12
), this equation can be inverted to give

[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}=\alpha,\;\tau(\alpha_{i})=\tau(\alpha),\text{ all
$i$}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}\delta_{\alpha_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)*\cdots*\delta_{\alpha_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\tau).

Let
(\tau,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}},\leqslant\penalty)
and
(\tilde{\tau},{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}},\leqslant\penalty)
be permissible slope stability conditions on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
in the sense of Section
3.3
. (See Example
3.8
and Section
5.2
on slope stability.) Suppose that
a<b
in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
are such that the quasi-abelian subcategories
{\mathcal{A}}_{[a,b]},\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{[a,b]}\subset{\mathbin{\cal A}}
generated by
\tau
- and
\tilde{\tau}
-semistable objects
E,\tilde{E}\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
with
\tau(E),\tilde{\tau}(\tilde{E})\in[a,b]
satisfy
{\mathcal{A}}_{[a,b]}=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{[a,b]}
. Then the Kontsevich–Soibelman style analogue of (
4.2
) is

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
a\leqslant\penalty\tau(\alpha_{1})<\tau(\alpha_{2})<\cdots<\tau(\alpha_{n})\leqslant\penalty b\end{subarray}}\delta_{\alpha_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)*\cdots*\delta_{\alpha_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\colon\\
a\leqslant\penalty\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{1})<\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{2})<\cdots<\tilde{\tau}(\alpha_{n})\leqslant\penalty b\end{subarray}}\delta_{\alpha_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\tau})*\cdots*\delta_{\alpha_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\tau}),

when this makes sense (we do not claim that it always does). As the sums in (
4.2
) are infinite, they should be interpreted as lying in a completion
\bar{U}\big(\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\big)
of
U\big(\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\big)
with respect to a suitable ideal. Morally speaking, both sides of (
4.2
) count all objects in
{\mathcal{A}}_{[a,b]}=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{[a,b]}
.

Remark 4.6
.

As in Zhu
[
109
]
, the graded vertex algebra
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
has a
Zhu algebra

A\big(\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\big)=\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})/I
, an associative
R
-algebra, with a natural morphism
U\big(\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\big)\rightarrow A\big(\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\big)
. It might be interesting to interpret (
4.2
) and (
4.6
)–(
4.2
) as equations in
A\big(\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\big)
or its completion
\bar{A}\big(\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\big)
.

4.3
The conjecture in algebraic geometry and representation theory

We now give more details on the ideas of Section
4.1
for
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear enumerative invariant theories coming from algebraic geometry and representation theory. But we exclude Donaldson–Thomas type invariants of Calabi–Yau 4-folds
[
11
,
19
,
87
]
, which will be discussed in Section
4.4
. In the sequel
[
59
, Sections 6–8]
we prove Conjecture
4.2
for most of the situations discussed in this section.

4.3.1
General discussion for abelian categories

We first discuss the case of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear abelian categories
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, such as
\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme, or compactly-supported coherent sheaves
\operatorname{coh}_{\rm cs}(X)
for
X
smooth and quasi-projective, or
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
for
Q
a quiver, or
mod-
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q/I
for
(Q,I)
a quiver with relations.

Then we have
Ext groups

\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{k}(E,F)
for
E,F\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
and
k=0,1,\dots
, with
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{0}(E,F)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\cal A}}(E,F)
, which are finite-dimensional
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector spaces. We say that the category
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
has
dimension

\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits{\mathbin{\cal A}}=m
if
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{k}(E,F)=0
for all
E
,
F
and
k>m
, and
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{m}(E,F)\neq 0
for some
E
,
F
. For example, if
X
is a smooth (quasi-)projective
m
-fold then
\operatorname{coh}(X)
(or
\operatorname{coh}_{\rm cs}(X)
) has dimension
m
, and if
Q
is a quiver then
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
has dimension 1 (or 0 if
Q
has no edges).

The
Euler form
of
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is the biadditive map
\chi_{\mathbin{\cal A}}\colon K_{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}})\times K_{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}})\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
with

\chi_{\mathbin{\cal A}}([E],[F])=\sum_{k=0}^{\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits{\mathbin{\cal A}}}(-1)^{k}\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{k}(E,F).

It need not be symmetric. The
numerical Grothendieck group
is
K^{\rm num}({\mathbin{\cal A}})=K_{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}})/\mathop{\rm Ker}\chi_{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. It is usually a good choice for
K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
in Assumption
4.1
(b).

In all these cases Assumption
2.4
applies, and the vertex algebra
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
and Lie algebra
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
in Assumption
4.1
(g), (h) are constructed as in Sections
2.3
–
2.4
. There is a natural perfect complex
\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet}
on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
called the
Ext complex
, whose cohomology at a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-point
([E],[F])\in({\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}})({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})
is
H^{k}(\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet}|_{([E],[F])})=\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{k}(E,F)
, with
\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits\bigl(\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet}|_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}}\bigr)=\chi_{\mathbin{\cal A}}(\alpha,\beta)
for
\alpha,\beta\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
.

As in
[
58
]
, in Assumption
2.4
(g) we define
\Theta^{\bullet}=(\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet})^{\vee}\oplus\sigma_{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{*}(\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet})[2n]
for some
n\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
, where
\sigma_{\mathbin{\cal M}}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
swaps the factors. Then (
2.5
)–(
2.9
) hold. We set
\chi(\alpha,\beta)=\chi_{\mathbin{\cal A}}(\alpha,\beta)+\chi_{\mathbin{\cal A}}(\beta,\alpha)
, so that
\chi
is symmetric with
\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits\bigl(\Theta^{\bullet}|_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\beta}}\bigr)=\chi(\alpha,\beta)
. In all these cases, there are canonical orientations on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
in Assumption
4.1
(e) coming from the complex geometry. For these orientations, the signs in Assumption
4.1
(f) are
\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}=(-1)^{\chi_{\mathbin{\cal A}}(\alpha,\beta)}
.

All the material of Assumption
4.1
(a)–(i) works for abelian categories
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
of any dimension
m\geqslant 0
, e.g., when
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a smooth projective
m
-fold. However, in Assumption
4.1
(j), the formation of virtual classes
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
depends critically on
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits{\mathbin{\cal A}}
:

(i)

If
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits{\mathbin{\cal A}}=1
then moduli spaces
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)
are smooth
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-schemes. If
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
is a compact complex manifold, and thus has a fundamental class
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm fund}
in homology. This case includes
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a projective curve, and
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
for
Q
a quiver.

(ii)

If
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits{\mathbin{\cal A}}=2
then moduli spaces
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)
have perfect obstruction theories in the sense of Behrend–Fantechi
[
6
]
. If
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
is proper, and thus has a virtual class
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
by
[
6
]
. This case includes
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a projective surface, and
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\text{\rm mod-${\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q/I$}
for
(Q,I)
a quiver with relations (though see Remark
4.7
).

In general if
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits{\mathbin{\cal A}}\geqslant 3
there is no way to define virtual classes
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
, and enumerative invariants do not exist. However, there are three special cases in which
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
can be defined by a mathematical trick:

(iii)

If
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a Calabi–Yau 3-fold, as in Donaldson–Thomas
[
24
]
, Thomas
[
100
]
and Joyce–Song
[
60
]
. (Essentially the same idea is used for Vafa–Witten invariants of surfaces in Tanaka–Thomas
[
96
,
97
]
.)

(iv)

If
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a Fano 3-fold, as in Thomas
[
100
]
.

(v)

If
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a Calabi–Yau 4-fold, as in Borisov–Joyce
[
11
]
, Oh–Thomas
[
87
]
and Cao–Leung
[
19
]
.

We will discuss (iii), (iv) in Section
4.3.6
, and (v) in Section
4.4
.

Remark 4.7
.

A well behaved abelian category
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
has an inclusion
A\hookrightarrow D^{b}{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, and then
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{k}(E,F)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{D^{b}{\mathbin{\cal A}}}(E,F[k])
for
E,F\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
and
k\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

For abelian categories
mod-
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q/I
of representations of a quiver with relations
(Q,I)
, this definition of Ext groups may have
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{k}(E,F)\neq 0
for infinitely many
k
, and
\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet}
is not perfect, so the theory above does not work. However, there is a way to fix this. One can define a triangulated category
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
, similar to
D^{b}{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, with an inclusion
{\mathbin{\cal A}}\hookrightarrow{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
as the heart of a t-structure, such that
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\cal T}\kern 0.29439pt}}^{k}(E,F)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\cal T}\kern 0.29439pt}}(E,F[k])
has the properties we need, and then we replace
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{k}(E,F)
above by
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\cal T}\kern 0.29439pt}}^{k}(E,F)
. See
[
60
, Remark 7.10]
for discussion of this.

4.3.2
General discussion for triangulated categories

Next we discuss our programme for triangulated categories, such as derived categories
D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
and
D^{b}\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. The moduli spaces
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
of objects in such categories are higher stacks or derived stacks in the sense of
[
102
,
103
,
104
,
105
,
106
]
.

One surprising fact is that the homology
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}),H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
is usually simpler and easier to compute than for abelian categories. For abelian categories, the direct sum
\oplus
makes
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
into a commutative monoid in stacks, and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm top}
into an H-space. In triangulated categories, the shift operator
[1]
acts as an inverse for
\oplus
up to homotopy, making
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
into (roughly) an abelian group in stacks, and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm top}
into a grouplike H-space, which are nicer than general H-spaces.

In the triangulated case, as in
[
58
]
it is generally no longer true that
\check{H}_{k}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\allowbreak\cong\hat{H}_{k+2}({\mathbin{\cal M}})/\allowbreak D\big(\hat{H}_{k}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\big)
, but this still holds under reasonable conditions when
k=0
, and the Lie algebra
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
is what we need for applications to enumerative invariants. Here is the main result of the first author
[
42
, Theorem 1.1]
:

Theorem 4.8
.

Suppose
X
is a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme which is either a curve, a surface, a rational
3
- or
4
-fold, a toric variety, a flag variety, or one of some other classes we will not give.

Write
K^{i}(X^{\rm an})
for
i=0,1
for the topological K-theory groups of the complex analytic topological space
X^{\rm an}
of
X
, and
K^{0}_{\rm sst}(X)
for the
0^{\rm th}
semi-topological K-theory group of
X
, in the sense of Friedlander and Walker
[
29
]
.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
be the moduli stack of objects in
D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
, a higher
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack, which exists by
[
104
]
. Then there is a canonical isomorphism of graded
R
-modules:

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\cong R\big[K^{0}_{\rm sst}(X)\big]\otimes_{R}\mathop{\rm Sym}\nolimits^{*}\bigl(K^{0}(X^{\rm an})\otimes_{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}t^{2}R\big[t^{2}\big]\bigr)\otimes_{R}\bigwedge\vphantom{\bigl(}^{*}\bigl(K^{1}(X^{\rm an})\otimes_{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}tR\big[t^{2}\big]\bigr).\!\!\!

(4.7)

Here
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
is
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}},R)
with grading shifted as in (
2.12
). The group ring
R[K^{0}_{\rm sst}(X)]:=\langle e^{\alpha}\colon\alpha\in K^{0}_{\rm sst}(X)\rangle_{R}
is graded by
\deg e^{\alpha}=-\chi(\alpha,\alpha)
. In the symmetric and exterior products
\mathop{\rm Sym}^{*}(\cdots),\bigwedge^{*}(\cdots)
, we take
K^{i}(X^{\rm an})
to have degree
0
, and
t
to be a formal variable with
\deg t=1
.

The vertex algebra structure on
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
is also equally explicit. By

[
58
]
, this gives an explicit description of the Lie algebra
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)=\hat{H}_{2}({\mathbin{\cal M}})/D\big(\hat{H}_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\big)
.

Even if we only want to study enumerative invariants for the abelian category
\operatorname{coh}(X)
, it may still be helpful to work with
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
,
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
for
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
the moduli of objects in
D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
, as these are easy to write down.

It is an important problem to extend Conjecture
4.2
to Bridgeland stability conditions
[
15
]
on triangulated categories such as
D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
and
D^{b}\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. This raises several difficult issues. There is already a significant literature for related questions on derived category versions of Donaldson–Thomas invariants of Calabi–Yau 3-folds. We make some remarks:

(a)

The authors expect the issues of defining invariants
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
counting strictly
\tau
-semistables in Conjecture
4.2
(i), and their characterization via pair invariants in Conjecture
4.2
(iii), to extend to Bridgeland stability conditions
\tau=(Z,{\mathbin{\cal P}})
on triangulated categories
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
with essentially no changes. This is because both these issues can be written in the abelian subcategory
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}_{\phi}\subset{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
of
\tau
-semistable objects with phase
\phi\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
.

(b)

The authors only expect (
4.1
)–(
4.2
) to make sense in a triangulated category
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
, and to have finitely many nonzero terms, if
\tau
,
\tilde{\tau}
are sufficiently close in the moduli space of Bridgeland stability conditions on
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
.

If
\tau=(Z,{\mathbin{\cal P}})
and
\tilde{\tau}=\big(\tilde{Z},\tilde{\mathbin{\cal P}}\big)
are close, then there should exist a unique third Bridgeland stability condition
\hat{\tau}=\big(\hat{Z},\hat{\mathbin{\cal P}}\big)
on
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
such that:

(i)

The central charges
Z
,
\tilde{Z}
,
\hat{Z}
satisfy
\mathop{\rm Re}\hat{Z}=\mathop{\rm Re}Z
and
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\hat{Z}=\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\tilde{Z}
.

(ii)

Write
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{[\phi,\phi+\pi)},\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{[\phi,\phi+\pi)},\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{[\phi,\phi+\pi)}\subset{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
for the abelian subcategories generated by
\tau
-,
\tilde{\tau}
- and
\hat{\tau}
-semistable objects with phases in
[\phi,\phi+\pi)
, for
\phi\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
. Then
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{[-\pi/2,\pi/2)}={\mathbin{\cal A}}_{[-\pi/2,\pi/2)}
and
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{[0,\pi)}=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{[0,\pi)}
.

Then wall-crossing from
\tau
to
\hat{\tau}
is equivalent to wall-crossing in the abelian category
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{[-\pi/2,\pi/2)}
, and wall-crossing from
\hat{\tau}
to
\tilde{\tau}
is equivalent to wall-crossing in
\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{[0,\pi)}
, as explained in
[
53
, Section 7]
. Hence Conjecture
4.2
(ii) for triangulated categories reduces to Conjecture
4.2
(ii) for abelian categories.

(c)

Bridgeland
[
16
]
has some interesting work on encoding Donaldson–Thomas invariants of a 3-Calabi–Yau triangulated category
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
into geometric structures (attractively called ‘Joyce structures’) on the space of Bridgeland stability conditions
\mathop{\rm Stab}\nolimits({{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt})
on
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}
. The Kontsevich–Soibelman wall-crossing formula
[
66
]
discussed in Section
4.2
is an ingredient in
[
16
, Definition 5.3]
. By replacing this by (
4.2
) and basing the definition on
U\big(\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\big)
, it may be possible to generalize
[
16
]
to enumerative invariants in other triangulated categories, for example
{{\cal T}\kern 0.39993pt}=D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a projective surface.

(d)

Work by Halpern-Leistner and coauthors (see
[
43
]
and references therein) generalizes the requirement in Assumption
4.1
(j) that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
should have a compact ‘coarse moduli space’ to triangulated categories.

4.3.3
Representations of quivers, and quivers with relations

We will discuss these at length in Sections
5
–
6
and
[
59
]
, so we say only a few words here. Sections
5
–
6
will prove Conjecture
4.2
when
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
for
Q
a quiver without oriented cycles (‘without oriented cycles’ makes
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
compact). This will be generalized in
[
59
]
to
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\text{\rm mod-${\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q/I$}
for
(Q,I)
a quiver with relations. There may be other categories of interest in representation theory that we can study using similar techniques.

4.3.4
Vector bundles and coherent sheaves on curves

Let
X
be a projective complex curve, that is, a compact Riemann surface. Then we can study moduli spaces
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{r,d}={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{(r,d)}(\mu)
of
\mu
-semistable vector bundles (for
r>0
) or coherent sheaves (for
r=0
) on
X
with rank
r
and degree
d
, where
\mu
is slope stability. This was done by Harder–Narasimhan
[
44
]
in algebraic geometry and Atiyah–Bott
[
5
]
in differential geometry, and many subsequent authors. It is common to restrict to
r,d
coprime, as in
[
5
, Section 9]
, as then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{(r,d)}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{(r,d)}(\mu)
is nonsingular, and a compact complex manifold.

There is only really one nice stability condition on
\operatorname{coh}(X)
, namely slope stability
\mu
. So wall-crossing as in Conjecture
4.2
(ii) is not interesting for curves. However, Conjecture
4.2
(i), (iii) may still have something new to say. They predict that there are classes
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{r,d}]_{\rm inv}
in
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}\big)
for all
(r,d)
, equal to the fundamental class of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{r,d}
in
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}\big)
when
r,d
are coprime, which could be computed using Definition
4.3
. For example, one could ask whether these classes have any interesting number-theoretic properties, as in
[
44
]
.

4.3.5
Coherent sheaves on surfaces

Next consider our programme when
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a projective complex surface. We take stability conditions on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
to be Gieseker stability for an ample line bundle
L\rightarrow X
. Invariants counting Gieseker semistable sheaves on
X
have been studied in depth by Mochizuki
[
79
]
, and should be understood as an algebro-geometric analogue of Donaldson invariants of 4-manifolds
[
23
]
.

When
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
, the virtual classes
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
in Assumption
4.1
(i) are defined using Behrend–Fantechi
[
6
]
perfect obstruction theories
\phi\colon{\mathbin{\cal F}}^{\bullet}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{L}}}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}}
as in
[
79
, Sections 5 and 6.1]
. If the geometric genus
p_{g}=h^{2,0}(X)
has
p_{g}>0
, and
\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits\alpha>0
, there is a trivial factor in
h^{-1}({\mathbin{\cal F}}^{\bullet})
which causes
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}=0
, see
[
79
, Proposition 6.2.2]
. As in
[
79
, Section 6.2]
, we can obtain nonzero invariants by fixing determinants, but that takes us outside the framework of Conjecture
4.2
. So here we restrict to the case
p_{g}=0
.

Conjecture 4.9
.

Conjecture
4.2
holds for
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
with
X
a projective complex surface with
p_{g}=0
, with ‘stability condition
\tau
’ meaning Gieseker stability for an ample line bundle on
X
, and other details as in Section
4.3.1
.

This is proved in the sequel
[
59
, Section 7.7]
, which also covers the case
p_{g}>0
. It builds on previous work by Mochizuki
[
79
]
and others.

4.3.6
Donaldson–Thomas theory for Calabi–Yau and Fano 3-folds

Donaldson–Thomas invariants counting semistable coherent sheaves on Calabi–Yau 3-folds were proposed by Donaldson–Thomas
[
24
]
and defined by Thomas
[
100
]
, who also gave a version for Fano 3-folds. The Calabi–Yau 3-fold version was extended in Joyce–Song
[
60
]
and Kontsevich–Soibelman
[
66
]
.

Let
X
be a Calabi–Yau or Fano 3-fold, and
\tau
be Gieseker stability on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
, and
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
with
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
. Since
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits{\mathbin{\cal A}}=3
in Section
4.3.1
, the natural obstruction theory
{\mathbin{\cal F}}^{\bullet}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{L}}}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}}
on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
is perfect in
[-2,0]
, and it is perfect in
[-1,0]
(which is necessary for the virtual class
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
from
[
6
]
to be defined) if
h^{2}\big(({\mathbin{\cal F}}^{\bullet})^{\vee}\big)=0
. For a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-point
[E]
in
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
we have

h^{2}\big(({\mathbin{\cal F}}^{\bullet})^{\vee}|_{[E]}\big)=\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{3}(E,E)\cong\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(E,E\otimes K_{X})^{*}.

(a)

If
X
is a Calabi–Yau 3-fold then
K_{X}\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
, and as
E
is stable we have
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(E,E\otimes K_{X})={\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
, so that
h^{2}\big(({\mathbin{\cal F}}^{\bullet})^{\vee}\big)={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)}
. As in
[
100
]
we may modify
{\mathbin{\cal F}}^{\bullet}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{L}}}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}}
by deleting the line bundle
h^{2}\big(({\mathbin{\cal F}}^{\bullet})^{\vee}\big)
, and then
[
6
]
gives a virtual class
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
of real dimension
-2\chi_{\mathbin{\cal A}}(\alpha,\alpha)=0
.

Donaldson–Thomas theory of Calabi–Yau 3-folds
does not fit into the set-up of Assumption

4.1

and Conjecture

4.2
, as deleting
h^{2}\big(({\mathbin{\cal F}}^{\bullet})^{\vee}\big)
means that
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}\in\check{H}_{-2}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
