, which is the wrong dimension.

In the sequel
[
59
, Section 1.5]
, the second author explains a modification of Conjecture
4.2
adapted to Donaldson–Thomas theory of Calabi–Yau 3-folds. The graded vertex algebra structure on
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
in Assumption
4.1
(g) is replaced by a
graded vertex Lie algebra
.

(b)

If
X
is a Fano 3-fold and
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\mathop{\rm supp}E>0
, that is, if
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\alpha>0
, then using
\tau
-semistability of
E
and
K_{X}
negative one can show that
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(E,E\otimes K_{X})=0
, so
{\mathbin{\cal F}}^{\bullet}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{L}}}_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}}
is perfect in
[-1,0]
, and
[
6
]
gives a virtual class
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
of real dimension
2-2\chi_{\mathbin{\cal A}}(\alpha,\alpha)
. If
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\alpha=0
this argument does not work, and
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
is undefined. The sequel
[
59
, Section 7.8]
proves Conjecture
4.2
for
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
for classes
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
with
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits\alpha>0
only.

Note that Donaldson–Thomas theory for Calabi–Yau 3-folds, and for Fano 3-folds, are significantly different, because of the difference in virtual dimension.

4.3.7
Theories of vector bundles or sheaves with extra data

There are also enumerative invariant theories in the literature, in which the objects to be counted are one or more vector bundles or coherent sheaves on a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme
X
, together with some morphisms between these sheaves. Such theories are usually special cases of the following general definition of Álvarez-Cónsul and García-Prada
[
3
]
.

Definition 4.10
.

Let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},h,t)
be a quiver, and
X
be a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme. Assign a vector bundle
E_{e}\rightarrow X
to each edge
e\in Q_{1}
. Following
[
3
]
, define a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear abelian category
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
whose objects are tuples
((V_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}},(\phi_{e})_{e\in Q_{1}})
, where
V_{v}\in\operatorname{coh}(X)
and
\phi_{e}\colon V_{t(e)}\rightarrow V_{h(e)}\otimes E_{e}
is a morphism in
\operatorname{coh}(X)
. Objects of
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
are called
twisted quiver sheaves
, or
twisted quiver bundles
if the
V_{v}
are vector bundles. If
E_{e}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}
for all
e\in Q_{1}
they are called
quiver sheaves
, or
quiver bundles
.

Álvarez-Cónsul and García-Prada
[
3
]
prove a Hitchin–Kobayashi correspondence for twisted quiver bundles, identifying solutions of a gauge theory equation with
\tau
-polystable objects in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
for a stability condition
\tau
on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
. This extends previously known Hitchin–Kobayashi correspondences. For example:

•

If
X
is a Riemann surface, and
Q=\overset{v}{\bullet}\,\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{$\circlearrowleft$}\,\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptstyle e$}
, and
E_{e}=K_{X}
, then twisted quiver bundles are
Higgs bundles
, as in Hitchin
[
45
]
.

•

If
Q=\overset{v}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e}}}\,\overset{w}{\bullet}
then
semistable pairs
as in Bradlow and Daskalopoulos
[
12
,
13
,
14
]
, García-Prada
[
31
,
32
]
, Thaddeus
[
99
]
(who studies wall-crossing under change of stability condition), and others, are examples of
\tau
-semistable quiver bundles with
V_{v}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}
.

We usually have
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits X+1
(or
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits X
for some moduli spaces, under extra conditions). To fit such categories
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
into the enumerative invariant set-up of Section
4.3
, we need
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits{\mathbin{\cal A}}\leqslant\penalty 2
for virtual classes
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
to be defined, as in Section
4.3.1
, so we take
X
to be a curve (or possibly a surface, under extra conditions). We also require the quiver
Q
to have no oriented cycles, to ensure that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
is compact. Under these conditions we expect Conjecture
4.2
to hold. Note that in contrast to Section
4.3.4
, there are usually many suitable stability conditions on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
, so wall-crossing in
Conjecture
4.2
(ii) is nontrivial.

4.3.8
Theories using equivariant homology

Several interesting enumerative invariant theories involve moduli spaces
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
with the action of an algebraic torus
T\cong{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}^{k}
, and we form virtual classes
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
in the
equivariant
homology
H_{*}^{T}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
. See for example Tanaka–Thomas
[
96
,
97
]
for the case of Vafa–Witten invariants. Then an equivariant version of Conjecture
4.2
may apply. This is discussed in the sequel
[
59
]
.

One useful property of the equivariant setting, as used in
[
96
,
97
]
for instance, is that to define virtual classes
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
in
H_{*}^{T}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
we do not need
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
to be compact, but only that the
T
-fixed locus
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)^{T}
should be compact, which is often easier to satisfy.

4.4
The conjecture for Calabi–Yau 4-fold DT4 invariants

We summarize some ideas from derived algebraic geometry
[
90
,
102
,
103
,
104
,
105
,
106
]
and Donaldson–Thomas type invariants of Calabi–Yau 4-folds
[
11
,
17
,
19
,
87
]
:

(a)

Let
X
be a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme. Then Toën and Vaquié
[
104
]
construct a
derived moduli stack

\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal M}}}
of objects in
\operatorname{coh}(X)
or in
D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
, as a locally finitely presented derived
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack in the sense of Toën and Vezzosi
[
102
,
103
,
105
,
106
]
. It has a
virtual dimension

\mathop{\rm vdim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal M}}}
, a locally constant map
\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal M}}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
. The classical truncation
{\mathbin{\cal M}}=t_{0}(\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal M}}})
is the usual moduli stack, as an Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack or higher
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack.

(b)

Pantev, Toën, Vaquié and Vezzosi
[
90
]
introduced a theory of shifted symplectic derived algebraic geometry, defining
k
-
shifted symplectic structures

\omega
on a derived stack
\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal S}}}
for
k\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
. If
X
is a Calabi–Yau
m
-fold and
\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal M}}}
is a derived moduli stack of objects in
\operatorname{coh}(X)
or
D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
then
\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal M}}}
has a
(2-m)
-shifted symplectic structure,
[
90
, Corollary 2.13]
.

(c)

If
(\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal S}}},\omega)
is a
k
-shifted symplectic derived stack for
k
even, Borisov–Joyce
[
11
, Section 2.4]
define a notion of
orientation
on
(\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal S}}},\omega)
.

(d)

Let
(\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal S}}},\omega)
be a proper, oriented
-2
-shifted symplectic derived scheme with
{\mathbin{\cal S}}=t_{0}(\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal S}}})
. Then Borisov–Joyce
[
11
, Corollary 1.2]
construct a
virtual class

[\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal S}}}]_{\rm virt}
in
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal S}},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})
using derived differential geometry
[
54
,
55
,
56
,
57
]
, of real dimension
\mathop{\rm vdim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal S}}}={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\mathop{\rm vdim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal S}}}
. Note that this is
half the expected dimension
. Oh–Thomas
[
87
]
provide an alternative definition of
[\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal S}}}]_{\rm virt}
in the style of Behrend–Fantechi
[
6
]
.

(e)

Let
X
be a Calabi–Yau 4-fold, and
(\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal M}}},\omega)
the
-2
-shifted symplectic derived moduli stack of objects in
\operatorname{coh}(X)
or
D^{b}\operatorname{coh}(X)
from (a),(b). Then Cao–Gross–Joyce
[
17
, Corollary 1.17]
prove that
(\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal M}}},\omega)
is orientable in the sense of (c). By taking a shifted symplectic quotient by
[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
, one can show that
\big(\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal M}}}^{\rm pl},\omega\big)
is also
-2
-shifted symplectic and orientable. Choose an orientation on
\big(\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal M}}}^{\rm pl},\omega\big)
.

(f)

Suppose
\alpha\in K(\operatorname{coh}(X))
with
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
, where
\tau
is Gieseker stability. Then
\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal M}}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
is a proper, oriented
-2
-shifted symplectic derived scheme, and has a virtual class
[\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal M}}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}
in
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
. Borisov–Joyce
[
11
]
propose to define Donaldson–Thomas type ‘DT4 invariants’ of
X
using these virtual classes. Cao–Leung
[
19
]
make a similar proposal using gauge theory rather than derived algebraic geometry.

We can now extend Section
4.1
to
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
for
X
a Calabi–Yau 4-fold. This works as in Section
4.3.1
, with the following important differences:

(i)

In Section
4.3.1
we took
\Theta^{\bullet}=(\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet})^{\vee}\oplus\sigma_{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{*}(\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet})[2n]
in Assumption
2.4
(g), where
\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet}
is the Ext complex on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
, and
\chi(\alpha,\beta)=\chi_{\mathbin{\cal A}}(\alpha,\beta)+\chi_{\mathbin{\cal A}}(\beta,\alpha)
in Assumption
4.1
(c). As in
[
58
]
, in the Calabi–Yau
2m
-fold case we instead set
\Theta^{\bullet}=(\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet})^{\vee}
and
\chi(\alpha,\beta)=\chi_{\mathbin{\cal A}}(\alpha,\beta)
, and use these to define the vertex and Lie algebra structures on
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
,
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
in Assumption
4.1
(g), (h) as in Sections
2.3
–
2.4
. Serre duality for Calabi–Yau 4-folds implies that (
2.5
) holds with
n=2
, and
\chi
is symmetric. Note that
\Theta^{\bullet}
and
\chi
are both (roughly speaking) half of their values in Section
4.3.1
. This is parallel to the virtual class
[\boldsymbol{{\mathbin{\cal S}}}]_{\rm virt}
in (d) above having half the expected dimension.

(ii)

In contrast to Section
4.3.1
, in the Calabi–Yau 4-fold case the complex geometry does not determine canonical orientations in Assumption
4.1
(e), but we must instead use Borisov–Joyce orientations
[
11
]
as in (c) above. As in (e), orientations exist on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
, and we must choose one.

(iii)

As in (d), we use Borisov–Joyce or Oh–Thomas virtual classes
[
11
,
87
]
instead of Behrend–Fantechi virtual classes
[
6
]
(which are undefined in this case) in Assumption
4.1
(j) when
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
.

Conjecture 4.11
.

Conjecture
4.2
holds for
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\operatorname{coh}(X)
when
X
is a Calabi–Yau 4-fold, with details in Assumption
4.1
as above.

4.5
Donaldson theory for 4-manifolds with
\boldsymbol{b^{2}_{+}=1}

Let
(X,g)
be a compact, oriented Riemannian 4-manifold, which need not be simply-connected. Hodge theory gives a natural isomorphism
H^{2}_{\rm dR}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\cong{\mathbin{\cal H}}^{2}
, where
H^{2}_{\rm dR}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
is the second de Rham cohomology group, and
{\mathbin{\cal H}}^{2}=\bigl\{\eta\in\Gamma^{\infty}\big(\Lambda^{2}T^{*}X\big)\colon{\rm d}\eta={\rm d}^{*}\eta=0\bigr\}
is the harmonic 2-forms on
X
. The splitting
\Lambda^{2}T^{*}X=\Lambda^{2}_{+}T^{*}X\oplus\Lambda^{2}_{-}T^{*}X
into self-dual and anti-self-dual 2-forms induces a splitting
{\mathbin{\cal H}}^{2}={\mathbin{\cal H}}^{2}_{+}\oplus{\mathbin{\cal H}}^{2}_{-}
. Write
H^{2}_{\rm dR}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})=H^{2}_{+}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\oplus H^{2}_{-}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
for the corresponding splitting in de Rham cohomology, and
b^{2}_{\pm}(X)=\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits H^{2}_{\pm}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
.

Donaldson theory
is the study of enumerative invariants (called
Donaldson invariants
) that ‘count’ connections with anti-self-dual curvature (called
instantons
) on vector or principal bundles
E\rightarrow X
, as in Donaldson and Kronheimer
[
23
]
. They have the amazing property that they can distinguish different smooth structures on the same topological 4-manifold. Most work on Donaldson theory takes
E\rightarrow X
to be a principal
\mathop{\rm SU}(2)
- or
\mathop{\rm SO}(3)
-bundle, but we will consider
{\mathbin{\rm U}}(m)
-bundles for
m\geqslant 0
.

We divide into three cases:

(i)

If
b^{2}_{+}(X)=0
then Donaldson invariants cannot be defined.
1
1

1

But the moduli spaces can be used to restrict the intersection form of
X
, as in
[
21
]
.

(ii)

If
b^{2}_{+}(X)=1
then Donaldson invariants can be defined. They depend on the splitting
H^{2}_{\rm dR}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})=H^{2}_{+}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\oplus H^{2}_{-}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
induced by
g
, and have wall-crossing behaviour under changes of this splitting.

(iii)

If
b^{2}_{+}(X)>1
then Donaldson invariants can be defined, and are independent of
g
and the splitting
H^{2}_{\rm dR}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})=H^{2}_{+}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\oplus H^{2}_{-}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
.

We are interested here in case (ii). We will sketch how Donaldson theory for
{\mathbin{\rm U}}(m)
-bundles on
X
when
b^{2}_{+}(X)=1
can be made, conjecturally, into a theory with the structure described in Section
4.1
. The sequel
[
59
, Section 7.7]
will study case (iii) for coherent sheaves on surfaces. We have nothing new to say about case (i).

Some papers on Donaldson theory with
b^{2}_{+}=1
are Kotschick–Morgan
[
67
,
68
]
, Ellingsrud–Göttsche
[
25
]
, Friedman–Qin
[
30
]
, Göttsche
[
34
]
, Göttsche–Zagier
[
40
]
, Moore–Witten
[
80
]
, and Göttsche–Nakajima–Yoshioka
[
37
]
. We define the moduli spaces of instantons we are interested in.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
(X,g)
be a compact, connected, oriented Riemannian 4-manifold, and
E\rightarrow X
be a
unitary vector bundle
, that is, a complex vector bundle
E\rightarrow X
with a Hermitian metric
h
on its fibres. A
unitary connection

\nabla_{E}
is a connection
\nabla_{E}
on
E
preserving
h
. Write
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{E}
for the topological space of unitary connections on
E
, with the
C^{\infty}
-topology. The group
\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)
of unitary automorphisms of
E
acts on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{E}
, and its normal subgroup
{\mathbin{\rm U}}(1)={\mathbin{\rm U}}(1)\cdot{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{E}\subset\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)
acts trivially, so that
\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)/{\mathbin{\rm U}}(1)
also acts on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{E}
. We call a connection
\nabla_{E}

irreducible
if
\mathop{\rm Stab}\nolimits_{\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)}(\nabla_{E})={\mathbin{\rm U}}(1)
, and
reducible
otherwise. Write
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{E}^{\rm irr}\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{E}
for the open subset of irreducible connections. Define topological stacks
{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}^{\rm irr}\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}
and
{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}^{{\rm irr},{\rm pl}}\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}^{\rm pl}
as in
[
77
,
85
,
86
]
by

\displaystyle{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}^{\rm irr}={\mathbin{\cal A}}_{E}^{\rm irr}/\mathop{\rm Aut}(E),\qquad

\displaystyle{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}={\mathbin{\cal A}}_{E}/\mathop{\rm Aut}(E),

\displaystyle{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}^{{\rm irr},{\rm pl}}={\mathbin{\cal A}}_{E}^{\rm irr}/(\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)/{\mathbin{\rm U}}(1)),\qquad

\displaystyle{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}^{\rm pl}={\mathbin{\cal A}}_{E}/(\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)/{\mathbin{\rm U}}(1)),

Then
{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}^{{\rm irr},{\rm pl}}
is a topological space, as
\mathop{\rm Aut}(E)/{\mathbin{\rm U}}(1)
acts freely on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{E}^{\rm irr}
.

Let
\nabla_{E}
in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{E}
have curvature
F^{\nabla_{E}}
, and split
F^{\nabla_{E}}=F^{\nabla_{E}}_{+}\oplus F^{\nabla_{E}}_{-}
for
F^{\nabla_{E}}_{\pm}
the components in
{\rm ad}(E)\otimes_{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}\Lambda^{2}_{\pm}T^{*}X
. We call
\nabla_{E}
an
instanton
if

F^{\nabla_{E}}_{+}=i\,{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{E}\otimes\omega\qquad\text{for some $\omega\in{\mathbin{\cal H}}^{2}_{+}$}.

(4.8)

Define moduli spaces of instantons
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{E}^{\rm st}\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{E}^{\rm ss}
by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{E}^{\rm st}=\bigl\{[\nabla_{E}]\in{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}^{{\rm irr},{\rm pl}}\colon\text{$\nabla_{E}$ is an instanton}\bigr\}\subset{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}^{{\rm irr},{\rm pl}},\\
&{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{E}^{\rm ss}=\bigl\{[\nabla_{E}]\in{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}^{\rm pl}\colon\text{$\nabla_{E}$ is an instanton}\bigr\}\subset{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}^{\rm pl}.\end{split}

(4.9)

If
g
is generic then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{E}^{\rm st}
is a smooth manifold of dimension

\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{E}^{\rm st}=1+b^{2}_{+}(X)-\chi(\llbracket E\rrbracket,\llbracket E\rrbracket),

(4.10)

where
\chi\colon K^{0}(X)\times K^{0}(X)\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
is the symmetric biadditive map

\displaystyle\chi(\alpha,\beta)={}

\displaystyle-\bigl(1-b_{1}(X)+b^{2}_{+}(X)\bigr)\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits\alpha\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits\beta-2\int_{X}\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{1}(\alpha)\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{1}(\beta)

\displaystyle{}+2\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits\alpha\int_{X}\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{2}(\beta)+2\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits\beta\int_{X}\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{2}(\alpha),

(4.11)

with
\mathop{\rm ch}\nolimits_{i}(-)
the Chern characters. Here in (
4.10
), the first term
1
compensates for the quotient by
{\mathbin{\rm U}}(1)
in (
4.9
), the second
b^{2}_{+}(X)
compensates for
\omega\in{\mathbin{\cal H}}^{2}_{+}
in (
4.8
), and the third
-\chi(\llbracket E\rrbracket,\llbracket E\rrbracket)
comes from the Atiyah–Singer index theorem as in Kronheimer
[
69
, equation (3), p. 64]
. If
g
is not generic then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{E}^{\rm st}
is a
derived manifold
of virtual dimension (
4.10
), in the sense of
[
54
,
55
,
56
,
57
]
.

As in
[
23
]
, by
Uhlenbeck compactification
the moduli spaces
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{E}^{\rm st}\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{E}^{\rm ss}
have completions
{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm st}\subseteq{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm ss}
such that
{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm ss}
is compact, and if
{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm st}={\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm ss}
then
{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm ss}
should have a virtual class
[{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm ss}]_{\rm virt}
, of dimension (
4.10
). Points of
{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm ss}\setminus{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{E}^{\rm ss}
are singular instantons with ‘bubbles’ at finitely many points in
X
.

Remark 4.13
.

The usual definition
[
23
]
of instantons (generally for
\mathop{\rm SU}(2)
connections) has
F^{\nabla_{E}}_{+}=0
rather than (
4.8
). In fact
\omega
in (
4.8
) is determined by
[\omega]=\pi_{H^{2}_{+}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})}(2\pi c_{1}(E))
in
H^{2}_{+}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
, so
\omega=0
if
E
is an
\mathop{\rm SU}(m)
-bundle.

We now restrict to the case
b^{2}_{+}(X)=1
. We outline the analogue of the data in Assumption
4.1
(a)–(j). We can take
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
to be the category of pairs
(E,\nabla_{E})
of a unitary bundle
E\rightarrow X
with a unitary connection
\nabla_{E}
. Then:

(a)

The obvious choices for
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
are the topological stacks

{\mathbin{\cal B}}=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\hskip 7.97224ptiso. classes $[E]$ of}\\
\text{unitary bundles $E\rightarrow X$}\end{subarray}}{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E},\qquad{\mathbin{\cal B}}^{\rm pl}=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\hskip 7.97224ptiso. classes $[E]$ of}\\
\text{unitary bundles $E\rightarrow X$}\end{subarray}}{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{E}^{\rm pl}.

These are studied in
[
61
]
, and their topological realizations have homotopy equivalences

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\mathbin{\cal B}}^{\rm top}\simeq{\mathop{\rm Map}\nolimits}_{C^{0}}\bigg(X,\coprod_{m\geqslant 0}B{\mathbin{\rm U}}(m)\bigg),\\
&({\mathbin{\cal B}}^{\rm pl})^{\rm top}\simeq{\mathop{\rm Map}\nolimits}_{C^{0}}\bigg(X,\coprod_{m\geqslant 0}B{\mathbin{\rm U}}(m)\bigg)/B{\mathbin{\rm U}}(1).\end{split}

(4.12)

However, there is a problem:
it is not clear that the completions
{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm st}
,
{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm ss}
of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{E}^{\rm st}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{E}^{\rm ss}
map naturally to

{\mathbin{\cal B}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal B}}^{\rm pl}
, and the authors expect that they do not. So we should not define invariants
[{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm ss}]_{\rm inv}
in
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal B}}^{\rm pl}\big)
.

Instead, we define topological spaces

{\mathbin{\cal M}}={\mathop{\rm Map}\nolimits}_{C^{0}}(X,B{\mathbin{\rm U}}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}),\qquad{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}={\mathop{\rm Map}\nolimits}_{C^{0}}(X,B{\mathbin{\rm U}}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/B{\mathbin{\rm U}}(1),

(4.13)

which are a kind of completion of (
4.12
) (in fact
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
is the
H-space completion
of
{\mathbin{\cal B}}^{\rm top}
, and is studied in
[
17
,
61
]
). We propose that invariants
[{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm ss}]_{\rm inv}
should lie in
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
. See Remark
4.14
below on this.

(b), (c)

We take
K({\mathbin{\cal A}})=K^{0}(X)
, and
\chi
as in (
4.11
).

(e)

Orientations on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
are explained in Joyce–Tanaka–Upmeier
[
61
, Section 4.2.3]
and constructed in
[
61
, Theorem 4.6]
, following previous work of Donaldson.

(g), (h)

The vertex algebra structure on
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
will be constructed in
[
58
]
. It is the lattice vertex algebra on the super-lattice
K^{0}(X)\oplus K^{1}(X)
with intersection form
\chi
in (
4.11
), so in a similar way to (
4.7
) we have

\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\cong R\big[K^{0}(X)\big]\otimes_{R}\mathop{\rm Sym}\nolimits^{*}\bigl(K^{0}(X)\otimes_{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}t^{2}R\big[t^{2}\big]\bigr)\otimes_{R}\bigwedge\vphantom{\bigl(}^{*}\bigl(K^{1}(X)\otimes_{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}tR\big[t^{2}\big]\bigr).

This gives the Lie algebra
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)=\hat{H}_{2}({\mathbin{\cal M}})/D\big(\hat{H}_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\big)
.

(i)

By
stability condition
we mean either the Riemannian metric
g
, or the orthogonal splitting
H^{2}_{\rm dR}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})=H^{2}_{+}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\oplus H^{2}_{-}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
induced by
g
, depending on your point of view. Here
H^{2}_{+}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})=\langle[\omega]\rangle_{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
as
b^{2}_{+}(X)=1
, where
\omega\in{\mathbin{\cal H}}^{2}_{+}
is a harmonic self-dual 2-form on
X
.

For comparison, Gieseker and
\mu
-stability conditions on
\operatorname{coh}(X)
for a projective surface
X
with
b^{2}_{+}(X)=1
correspond to Kähler classes
[\omega]
in
H^{2}_{\rm dR}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
, where
{\mathbin{\cal H}}^{2}_{+}=\langle\omega\rangle
. For the wall-crossing formula (
4.2
), the coefficients
U(-)
should be defined as for
\mu
-stability for projective surfaces.

(j)

If
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
, by
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
we mean the disjoint union over isomorphism classes of unitary bundles
E\rightarrow X
with
\llbracket E\rrbracket=\alpha
of the moduli spaces
{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm st}
,
{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}_{E}^{\rm ss}
above, considered as mapping to
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
as claimed in (a). Although the moduli spaces themselves depend on
g
, when
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
the virtual class
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm virt}\in H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
depends only on the splitting
H^{2}_{\rm dR}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})=H^{2}_{+}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\oplus H^{2}_{-}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
, which is why we have these two choices for stability conditions in (i). Note too that as
b^{2}_{+}(X)=1
, equation (
4.10
) gives
\mathop{\rm vdim}\nolimits{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)=2-\chi(\alpha,\alpha)
, as required.

Remark 4.14
.

Here is some justification for the choice of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
in (
4.13
). Let
X
be a projective surface. Then we can form moduli stacks
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{vect}}\subset{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{t-f}}\subset{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{perf}}
of vector bundles, and torsion-free sheaves, and perfect complexes on
X
, and projective linear versions
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{vect}}^{\rm pl}\subset{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{t-f}}^{\rm pl}\subset{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{perf}}^{\rm pl}
with
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{vect}}^{\rm pl}={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{vect}}/[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
, and so on. We have mapping stack presentations

{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{vect}}\cong{\mathop{\rm Map}\nolimits}\bigg(X,\coprod_{m\geqslant 0}[*/\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits(m,{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})]\bigg),\qquad{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{perf}}\cong{\mathop{\rm Map}\nolimits}\big(X,\mathop{\rm Perf}_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\big).

The analogues of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{E}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{E}
in Definition
4.12
are moduli spaces of (semi)stable vector bundles,
and are substacks of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{vect}}^{\rm pl}
. But the analogues of the Uhlenbeck compactifications
{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}^{\rm st}_{E}
,
{\mathbin{\smash{\,\,\overline{\!\!\mathcal{M}\!}\,}}}^{\rm ss}_{E}
are moduli spaces of (semi)stable
torsion-free sheaves
(thought of as singular vector bundles), so they are substacks of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{t-f}}^{\rm pl}
, and hence of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{perf}}
, but
not
of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{vect}}^{\rm pl}
. We have topological realizations

\bigg(\coprod\limits_{m\geqslant 0}[*/\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits(m,{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})]\bigg)^{\rm top}\simeq\coprod\limits_{m\geqslant 0\!\!}B{\mathbin{\rm U}}(m),\qquad\mathop{\rm Perf}_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{\rm top}\simeq B{\mathbin{\rm U}}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}},\qquad[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]^{\rm top}\simeq B{\mathbin{\rm U}}(1).

Hence
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{vect}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{vect}}^{\rm pl}
are analogous to
{\mathbin{\cal B}}^{\rm top}
,
\big({\mathbin{\cal B}}^{\rm pl}\big)^{\rm top}
in (
4.12
), and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{perf}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\text{perf}}^{\rm pl}
are analogous to
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
in (
4.13
).

Conjecture 4.15
.

With the set up above, Conjecture
4.2
holds for Donaldson theory of 4-manifolds
X
with
b^{2}_{+}(X)=1
, not necessarily simply-connected.

4.6
Other gauge-theoretic enumerative invariant theories

We comment briefly on other gauge-theoretic invariants in the literature.

Casson invariants of 3-manifolds

Casson invariants count flat connections on compact 3-manifolds, as in Akbulut–McCarthy
[
2
]
, Taubes
[
98
]
and Boden–Herald
[
9
]
. They do not fit into the set-up of Section
4.1
, because the virtual dimension is wrong, as for Donaldson–Thomas invariants of Calabi–Yau 3-folds in Section
4.3.6
(a), and for other reasons.

\boldsymbol{G_{2}}
-instantons on
\boldsymbol{G_{2}}
-manifolds

Let
X
be a compact 7-manifold and
(\varphi,g)
a torsion-free
G_{2}
-structure on
X
, as in Joyce
[
47
]
. The
G_{2}
-structure induces a splitting
\Lambda^{2}T^{*}X=\Lambda^{2}_{7}\oplus\Lambda^{2}_{14}
into subbundles of ranks 7, 14. A connection
\nabla_{E}
on a vector or principal bundle
E\rightarrow X
is a
G_{2}
-
instanton
if
\pi_{\Lambda^{2}_{7}}\big(F^{\nabla_{E}}\big)=0
. Donaldson and Segal
[
20
]
propose defining enumerative invariants of
(X,\varphi,g)
by counting
G_{2}
-instantons. Again, these do not fit into the set-up of Section
4.1
, because the virtual dimension is wrong.

\boldsymbol{\mathop{\rm Spin}(7)}
-instantons on
\boldsymbol{\mathop{\rm Spin}(7)}
-manifolds

Let
X
be a compact, simply-connected 8-manifold and
(\Omega,g)
a torsion-free
\mathop{\rm Spin}(7)
-structure on
X
, as in Joyce
[
47
]
. The
\mathop{\rm Spin}(7)
-structure induces a splitting
\Lambda^{2}T^{*}X=\Lambda^{2}_{7}\oplus\Lambda^{2}_{21}
into subbundles of ranks 7, 21. A connection
\nabla_{E}
on a vector or principal bundle
E\rightarrow X
is a
\mathop{\rm Spin}(7)
-
instanton
if
\pi_{\Lambda^{2}_{7}}\big(F^{\nabla_{E}}\big)=0
. Donaldson and Thomas
[
24
]
propose defining enumerative invariants of
(X,\Omega,g)
by counting
\mathop{\rm Spin}(7)
-instantons.

There is a strong analogy between counting
\mathop{\rm Spin}(7)
-instantons and Donaldson theory of 4-manifolds. Under this analogy we compare
\Lambda^{2}_{7}\leftrightsquigarrow\Lambda^{2}_{+}
and
\Lambda^{2}_{21}\leftrightsquigarrow\Lambda^{2}_{-}
. There is a splitting
H^{2}_{\rm dR}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})=H^{2}_{7}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\oplus H^{2}_{21}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
, with
b^{2}_{7}=\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits H^{2}_{7}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
the analogue of
b^{2}_{+}
. As in Section
4.5
, we divide into three cases:

(i)

b^{2}_{7}=0
, which happens if
g
has holonomy group
\mathop{\rm Hol}(g)=\mathop{\rm Spin}(7)
. Then the analogy with Donaldson theory suggests that enumerative invariants counting
\mathop{\rm Spin}(7)
instantons cannot be defined.

(ii)

b^{2}_{7}=1
, which happens if
\mathop{\rm Hol}(g)=\mathop{\rm SU}(4)
, and
X
is a Calabi–Yau 4-fold. Then the set-up of Section
4.1
may work. Note however that this may just be a gauge-theoretic version of the DT4 invariants of Calabi–Yau 4-folds discussed in Section
4.4
, as proposed in Cao–Leung
[
19
]
.

(iii)

b^{2}_{7}>1
, which happens if
b^{2}_{7}=2
and
\mathop{\rm Hol}(g)=\mathop{\rm Sp}(2)
or
b^{2}_{7}=3
and
\mathop{\rm Hol}(g)=\mathop{\rm Sp}(1)\times\mathop{\rm Sp}(1)
, and
g
is hyperkähler. Then we might hope that enumerative invariants can be defined, which are independent of
g
, but which do not fit into the framework of Section
4.1
, though see Question
4.19
.

4.7
Questions for future work

Here are some questions that seem to the authors to be interesting.

Question 4.16
.

In a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear enumerative invariant theory of the kind discussed in Section
4.1
, can we write the family of invariants
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
for all
\alpha
in
C({\mathbin{\cal A}})
in terms of a small amount of data by a universal formula?

Examples of the kind of formula we have in mind are those writing the generating series of Donaldson invariants in terms of a finite collection of Kronheimer–Mrowka basic classes, or Seiberg–Witten invariants, as in Kronheimer–Mrowka
[
70
]
, Fintushel–Stern
[
27
]
, Witten
[
108
]
, Moore–Witten
[
80
]
, Mariño–Moore
[
74
]
, and Göttsche–Nakajima–Yoshioka
[
39
]
. Using the results of Sections
5
–
6
, we can investigate this question for
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, and the authors hope to write about this in a future paper in the series.

Question 4.17
.

Can we extend the set up of Section
4.1
to replace
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
,
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
by
E_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
,
\allowbreak E_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
for
E_{*}(-)
a complex-oriented generalized homology theory over
R
, such as K-homology?

The vertex algebra and Lie algebra parts of Assumption
4.1
will be extended to
E_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
,
E_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
in
[
58
]
. It turns out that
E_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
is a ‘vertex
F
-algebra’, where
F(x,y)
is the formal group law associated to the complex-oriented cohomology theory
E^{*}(-)
. There is an interesting literature on K-theoretic versions of enumerative invariants, which often form generating functions with attractive properties – see for example Göttsche–Nakajima–Yoshioka
[
38
]
, Okounkov
[
88
]
, Göttsche–Kool
[
36
]
, Thomas
[
101
]
, Laarakker
[
71
]
, Arbesfeld
[
4
]
, and Cao–Kool–Monavari
[
18
]
– and also on cobordism invariants, as in Göttsche–Kool
[
35
]
and Shen
[
94
]
. In Question
4.16
over
E_{*}(-)
, should the universal formula depend on the formal group law
F
? A good starting point would be to study invariants
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\in E_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
for
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, as in Sections
5
–
6
.

Question 4.18
.

Can we use the theory of vertex algebras to understand more about the structure of enumerative invariants, given the appearance of vertex algebras in Assumption
4.1
?
For example, to explain modular properties of generating functions of enumerative invariants? Remark
4.6
may help.

Connections between vertex algebras and Donaldson theory, Vafa–Witten theory, or Seiberg–Witten theory of 4-manifolds are suggested by the work of Nakajima
[
82
,
83
]
and Feigin–Gukov
[
26
]
.

As a possible place to start, observe that in Conjecture
4.2
, for
\alpha\in K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
and with
R={\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
or
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
, we can consider the graded
R
-vector space

V^{\alpha}_{*}:=[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\cap H^{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big)\subset H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm pl}\big).

This is finite-dimensional over
R
, and may be considered an approximation to the homology
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau))
, since if
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
is a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme and the restriction map
H^{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}_{\alpha}\big)\rightarrow H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau))
is surjective (this is called
Kirwan surjectivity
, and can be proved in some situations), then
V^{\alpha}_{*}=H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau))
. For example, if
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
is a moduli space of rank 1 torsion-free sheaves on a simply-connected projective surface
X
then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
may be identified with a Hilbert scheme
{\rm Hilb}{}^{(n)}(X)
, and
V^{\alpha}_{*}\cong H_{*}\big({\rm Hilb}{}^{(n)}(X)\big)
.

We regard
V^{\alpha}_{*}
as a ‘categorification’ of
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
. It would be interesting to use vertex algebra ideas to produce representations of interesting algebras on
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in S}V_{*}^{\alpha}
for subsets
S\subset K({\mathbin{\cal A}})
, just as Grojnowski
[
41
]
and Nakajima
[
84
]
find representations of Heisenberg algebras on
\bigoplus_{n\geqslant 0}H_{*}\big({\rm Hilb}^{(n)}(X)\big)
.

Question 4.19
.

Donaldson theory of compact, oriented
4
-manifolds
X
with
b^{2}_{+}(X)=1
fits directly into our theory, as in Section
4.5
. Can we produce a variant of our theory which describes the case
b^{2}_{+}(X)>1
?

This should also apply to Mochizuki-style
[
79
]
counting of coherent sheaves on smooth projective surfaces
X
with
p_{g}>0
, and other situations. There should no longer be wall-crossing phenomena under change of stability condition, but counting strictly
\tau
-semistables, as in Conjecture
4.2
(i)
, and general structures in the invariants, as in Question
4.16
, may still apply.

Question
4.19
is answered in the sequel
[
59
, Section 7.6]
for invariants counting coherent sheaves on smooth projective surfaces
X
with
p_{g}>0
.

Question 4.20
.

Conjecture
4.2
gives invariants
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-homology rather than
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-homology when
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm st}(\tau)\neq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)
. The wall-crossing formulae
(
4.1
)
,
(
4.2
)
,
(
4.4
)
also involve coefficients
\tilde{U}(-)
,
U(-)
in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
rather than
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

Is there a universal way to produce invariants
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}^{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-homology, with an invertible
(
modulo torsion
)
transformation law to the
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
similar to
(
3.11
)
–
(
3.14
)
, satisfying a different wall-crossing formula under change of stability condition, with coefficients in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
?

As an example of what we have in mind, note that Joyce–Song
[
60
]
define ‘generalized Donaldson–Thomas invariants’
\bar{DT}{}^{\alpha}(\tau)\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
, the analogue of our
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\alpha}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
. In
[
60
, Section 6.2]
they also define ‘BPS invariants’
\hat{DT}{}^{\alpha}(\tau)
by

\bar{DT}{}^{\alpha}(\tau)=\sum_{m\geqslant 1,\,m\mid\alpha}\frac{1}{m^{2}}\,\hat{DT}{}^{\alpha/m}(\tau),

and they conjecture
[
60
, Conjecture 6.12]
that
\hat{DT}{}^{\alpha}(\tau)\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
when
\tau
is ‘generic’. The authors expect the answer to Question
4.20
will involve linear operations mapping
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}_{\alpha}\big)\rightarrow\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}_{m\alpha}\big)
for
m=2,3,\dots
.

Question 4.21
.

Do our conjectures have an interpretation in string theory?

5
An example: representations of quivers

We now prove Conjecture
4.2
when
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
for
Q
a quiver without oriented cycles. The proofs of Theorems
5.8
and
5.11
are postponed to Section
6
.

5.1
Quivers, their moduli stacks, and vertex algebras

Here are the basic definitions in quiver theory, as in Benson
[
7
, Section 4.1]
.

Definition 5.1
.

A
quiver

Q
is a finite
directed graph. That is,
Q
is a quadruple
(Q_{0},Q_{1},h,t)
, where

Q_{0}
is a finite set of
vertices
,
Q_{1}
is a finite set of

edges
, and
h,t\colon Q_{1}\rightarrow Q_{0}
are maps giving the
head

and
tail
of each edge.

A closed loop of directed edges
\overset{v_{0}}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e_{1}}}}\,\overset{v_{1}}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e_{2}}}}\,\cdots\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e_{n}}}}\,\overset{v_{n}=v_{0}}{\bullet}
is an
oriented cycle
in
Q
. Later we will restrict to quivers with
no oriented cycles
.

A
representation

(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})=((V_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}},(\rho_{e})_{e\in Q_{1}})
of
Q
gives
finite-dimensional
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector spaces
V_{v}
for
v\in Q_{0}
, and linear maps
\rho_{e}\colon V_{t(e)}\rightarrow V_{h(e)}
for
e\in Q_{1}
. A
morphism of representations

\boldsymbol{\phi}=(\phi_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}}\colon(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})\rightarrow(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
gives linear maps
\phi_{v}\colon V_{v}\rightarrow W_{v}
for
v\in Q_{0}
with
\phi_{h(e)}\circ\rho_{e}=\sigma_{e}\circ\phi_{t(e)}
for
e\in Q_{1}
. Write
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
for the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear abelian category of representations of
Q
.

Write
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}}
for the abelian group of maps
\boldsymbol{d}\colon Q_{0}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
, and
{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\subset{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}}
for the subset of maps
\boldsymbol{d}\colon Q_{0}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}
. The
dimension vector

{\mathbin{\bf dim}\kern 1.00006pt}(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}
of a representation
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
is
{\mathbin{\bf dim}\kern 1.00006pt}(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})\colon v\mapsto\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}V_{v}
. This induces a surjective morphism
{\mathbin{\bf dim}\kern 1.00006pt}\colon K_{0}(\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q})\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}}
.

We describe the moduli stacks
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
for
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},h,t)
be a quiver. Write
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
for the moduli stack of objects
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
in
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. Then there is a natural decomposition

{\mathbin{\cal M}}=\coprod_{\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}},

where
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}
is the moduli stack of
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
with
{\mathbin{\bf dim}\kern 1.00006pt}(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})=\boldsymbol{d}
. For any such
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
, by considering isomorphisms
V_{v}\cong{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{\boldsymbol{d}(v)}
for
v\in Q_{0}
we see we may write
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}
explicitly as a quotient stack

\displaystyle{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}=[R_{\boldsymbol{d}}/\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}],\qquad\text{where}\quad R_{\boldsymbol{d}}=\prod_{e\in Q_{1}}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits\big({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{\boldsymbol{d}(t(e))},{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{\boldsymbol{d}(h(e))}\big)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}=\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits(\boldsymbol{d}(v),{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}),\quad\text{with group action}

\displaystyle(A_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}}\colon\ ((B_{e})_{e\in Q_{1}})\longmapsto\big(A_{h(e)}\circ B_{e}\circ A_{t(e)}^{-1}\big)_{e\in Q_{1}}.

Write
{\cal V}_{v}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
for
v\in Q_{0}
for the tautological vector bundle with

{\cal V}_{v}|_{[((V_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}},(\rho_{e})_{e\in Q_{1}})]}=V_{v},

and write
{\cal V}_{v,\boldsymbol{d}}={\cal V}_{v}|_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}}
for
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}
, so that
\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits{\cal V}_{v,\boldsymbol{d}}=\boldsymbol{d}(v)
. As
R_{\boldsymbol{d}}
is contractible, we have
{\mathbin{\mathbb{A}}}^{1}
-homotopy equivalences

{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}\simeq[*/\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}]=\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}[*/\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits(\boldsymbol{d}(v),{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})].

Thus the topological realization of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}
is

{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm top}\simeq\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}B\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits(\boldsymbol{d}(v),{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}).

Let
R
be a commutative
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-algebra, such as
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
,
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
or
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
. As
\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits(r,{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}})\simeq{\mathbin{\rm U}}(r)
, the computation of
H^{*}(B{\mathbin{\rm U}}(r))
by Milnor and Stasheff
[
78
, Theorem 14.5]
implies that the cohomology of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}
over
R
is

H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}})=H^{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm top},R\big)\cong R\big[c_{v,{\boldsymbol{d}}}^{i}\colon v\in Q_{0},\,i=1,2,\dots,\boldsymbol{d}(v)\big],

(5.1)

where
c_{v,{\boldsymbol{d}}}^{i}
is a formal variable of degree
2i
, with
c_{v,{\boldsymbol{d}}}^{i}=c_{i}({\cal V}_{v,\boldsymbol{d}})
. The homology
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}})
is the
R
-linear dual of (
5.1
), and
H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})=\bigoplus_{\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}}H_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}})
.

Similarly, the projective linear moduli stack
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
from Definition
2.7
is

{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}=\coprod_{\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl},\qquad\text{where}\quad{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}=[R_{\boldsymbol{d}}/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}],

(5.2)

for
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}=\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}
with
{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}=\bigl\{(\lambda\,{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{\boldsymbol{d}(v)})_{v\in Q_{0}}\colon 0\neq\lambda\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\bigr\}\subseteq\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}
.

We can describe the Ext groups
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{i}(D,E)
, the Euler form
\chi_{Q}
, and the Ext complex
\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet}
explicitly for
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},h,t)
be a quiver. It is well known that
\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{i}(D,E)=0
for all
D,E\in\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
and
i>1
, and

\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(D,E)-\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{1}(D,E)=\chi_{Q}({\mathbin{\bf dim}\kern 1.00006pt}D,{\mathbin{\bf dim}\kern 1.00006pt}E),

where
\chi_{Q}\colon{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
is the Euler form of

\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, given by

\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{e})=\sum_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)\boldsymbol{e}(v)-\sum_{e\in Q_{1}}\boldsymbol{d}(t(e))\boldsymbol{e}(h(e)).

(5.3)

The Ext complex
\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
may be written explicitly as the two-term complex of vector bundles in degrees 0, 1:

\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet}=\Bigl[\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 33.49384pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-33.49384pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathop{\bigoplus_{v\in Q_{0}}{\cal V}_{v}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal V}_{v}}\limits_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.83015pt\raise 5.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\lambda}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 83.49384pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 83.49384pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathop{\bigoplus_{e\in Q_{1}}{\cal V}_{t(e)}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal V}_{h(e)}}\limits_{1}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\Bigr],

(5.4)

where ‘
\boxtimes
’ is external tensor product, and the morphism
\lambda
depends on the point in
R_{\boldsymbol{d}}
in
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}=[R_{\boldsymbol{d}}/\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}]
.

We then make
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
into a graded vertex algebra, and
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
into a graded Lie algebra, as in Sections
2.3
–
2.4
with the data in Assumption
2.4
chosen as in Section
4.3.1
, so in particular we take

\displaystyle K(\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q})={\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}},\qquad

\displaystyle\chi(\alpha,\beta)=\chi_{Q}(\alpha,\beta)+\chi_{Q}(\beta,\alpha),

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\alpha,\beta}=(-1)^{\chi_{Q}(\alpha,\beta)},\qquad

\displaystyle\Theta^{\bullet}=(\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet})^{\vee}\oplus\sigma_{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{*}(\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet}).

(5.5)

5.2
(Weak) stability conditions on quiver categories

Slope stability conditions
on quiver categories are an important class.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
Q
be a quiver, and in the situation of Section
3.3
with
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, take
K({\mathbin{\cal A}})={\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}}
, so that
C({\mathbin{\cal A}})={\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
. Fix
\mu_{v}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
for all
v\in Q_{0}
. Define
\mu\colon C({\mathbin{\cal A}})\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
by

\mu(\boldsymbol{d})=\frac{\sum_{v\in Q_{0}}\mu_{v}\boldsymbol{d}(v)}{\sum_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)}.

Then
(\mu,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}},\leqslant\penalty)
is a stability condition on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
in the sense of Definition
3.5
, called
slope stability
, which we often write as
\mu
. We call
\mu
a
slope function
.

For an object
E
of
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
to be
\mu
-stable, or
\mu
-semistable, is an open condition on the point
[E]
in
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
or
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
. Write
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
for the open
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-substacks of
\mu
-(semi)stable objects. They are quotient stacks

{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)=[R^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}],\qquad{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)=[R^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}],

for
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}
-invariant open subschemes
R^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\subseteq R^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\subseteq R_{\boldsymbol{d}}
.

Here is a class of slope functions for which the moduli spaces
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
,
\allowbreak{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
are easy to understand.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
Q
be a quiver, and
\mu
a slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
defined using
\mu_{v}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
for
v\in Q_{0}
. We call
\mu

increasing
if for all edges
\overset{v}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e}}}\,\overset{w}{\bullet}
in
Q
we have
\mu_{v}<\mu_{w}
. Such
\mu
exist if and only if
Q
has
no oriented cycles
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
Q
be a quiver with no oriented cycles, and
\mu
be an increasing slope function on
Q
. Then for each
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
, either:

(a)

\boldsymbol{d}=\delta_{v}
for some
v\in Q_{0}
, that is,
\boldsymbol{d}(v)=1
and
\boldsymbol{d}(w)=0
for
w\neq v
. Then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
is a single point
*
.

(b)

\boldsymbol{d}\neq\delta_{v}
for any
v\in Q_{0}
, and for some
t\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
we have
\boldsymbol{d}(v)=0
for all
v\in Q_{0}
with
\mu_{v}\neq t
. Then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)=\varnothing
and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\cong[*/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}]
. Also
2-\chi(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})<0
in this case.

(c)

Neither
(a)
nor
(b)
hold. Then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)=\varnothing
.

Proof.

For (a), if
\boldsymbol{d}=\delta_{v}
then as
Q
has no oriented cycles there are no edges
\overset{v}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e}}}\,\overset{v}{\bullet}
in
Q
, and in (
5.2
) we have
R_{\boldsymbol{d}}=0
and
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}=\{1\}
, so
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}=*
. But
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
as any
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
in class
\boldsymbol{d}
in
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
has no non-trivial subobjects, and so is automatically
\mu
-stable.

For (b), suppose
\boldsymbol{d}\neq\delta_{v}
for any
v
, and for
t\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
we have
\boldsymbol{d}(v)=0
for all
v\in Q_{0}
with
\mu_{v}\neq t
. Then if
v_{1},v_{2}\in Q_{0}
with
\boldsymbol{d}(v_{1}),\boldsymbol{d}(v_{2})>0
we have
\mu_{v_{1}}=\mu_{v_{2}}=t
, so there are no edges
\overset{v_{1}}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e}}}\,\overset{v_{2}}{\bullet}
in
Q
as
\mu
is increasing. Thus in (
5.2
) we have
R_{\boldsymbol{d}}=0
, so
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\cong[*/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}]
.

Let
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
lie in class
\boldsymbol{d}
in
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. Then
\boldsymbol{\rho}=\boldsymbol{0}
as
R_{\boldsymbol{d}}=0
. If
0\neq(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{0})\subseteq(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{0})
is any subobject then
\mu(\llbracket\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{0}\rrbracket)=\mu(\llbracket\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{0}\rrbracket)=t
as
\boldsymbol{d}(v)=0
unless
\mu_{v}=t
. Hence
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
is
\mu
-semistable and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\cong[*/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}]
. Also as
\boldsymbol{d}\neq\delta_{v}
we have
\sum_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)>1
. So there exists a proper subobject
0\neq(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{0})\subsetneq(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{0})
, and
\mu(\llbracket\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{0}\rrbracket)=\mu(\llbracket\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{0}\rrbracket)
shows that
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
is not
\mu
-stable, giving
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)=\varnothing
. As above, if
v_{1},v_{2}\in Q_{0}
with
\boldsymbol{d}(v_{1}),\boldsymbol{d}(v_{2})>0
there are no edges
\overset{v_{1}}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e}}}\,\overset{v_{2}}{\bullet}
in
Q
. Thus from (
5.3
) and (
5.5
) we see that
\chi(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})=2\sum_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)^{2}
, so
\chi(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})\geqslant 4
as
\boldsymbol{d}\neq\delta_{v}
, and
2-\chi(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})<0
.

For (c), as neither (a), (b) hold there exist
v_{1},v_{2}\in Q_{0}
with
\boldsymbol{d}(v_{1}),\boldsymbol{d}(v_{2})>0
and
\mu_{v_{1}}\neq\mu_{v_{2}}
. Set
t={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}(\mu_{v_{1}}+\mu_{v_{2}})
. Define a subobject
(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\subset(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
in
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
by
W_{v}=V_{v}
if
\mu_{v}\geqslant t
and
W_{v}=0\subseteq V_{v}
if
\mu_{v}<t
, and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\rho}|_{\boldsymbol{W}}
. If
\overset{v}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e}}}\,\overset{w}{\bullet}
is an edge in
Q
then
\mu
increasing implies that either (i)
W_{v}=V_{v}
,
W_{w}=V_{w}
, or (ii)
W_{v}=W_{w}=0
, or (iii)
W_{v}=0
,
W_{w}=V_{w}
, and in each case
\rho_{e}\colon V_{v}\rightarrow V_{w}
maps
W_{v}\rightarrow W_{w}
, so
(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
is well defined. Both
(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
and
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})/(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
are nonzero, as one contains
V_{v_{1}}\neq 0
and the other
V_{v_{2}}\neq 0
. Also
\mu\bigl([(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]\bigr)\geqslant t
and
\mu\bigl([(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})/(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]\bigr)<t
, so
\mu\bigl([(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]\bigr)>\mu\bigl([(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})/(\boldsymbol{W},\boldsymbol{\sigma})]\bigr)
. Hence
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
is
\mu
-unstable by Definition
3.5
, for all
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
with
{\mathbin{\bf dim}\kern 1.00006pt}(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})=\boldsymbol{d}
. Thus
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)=\varnothing
.
∎

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
Q
be a quiver with no oriented cycles, and
\mu
a slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, and
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
with
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
. Then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
is a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme.

Proof.

Suppose first that
\mu_{v}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
for all
v\in Q_{0}
. Then using geometric invariant theory (GIT) from Mumford–Fogarty–Kirwan
[
81
]
, King
[
64
, Theorem 4.1]
shows that
R^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
,
R^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
are the open subschemes of GIT (semi)stable points for a certain linearization
\theta
of the action of
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}
on
R_{\boldsymbol{d}}
determined by the
\mu_{v}
. Thus
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)=[R^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}]
is not merely an Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack, but a smooth quasi-projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme. Also there exists a GIT quotient
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)=R^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)/\!/_{\theta}\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}
, which is a coarse moduli scheme for
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
. As
Q
has no oriented cycles, there is a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}
-subgroup of
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}
acting on the vector space
R_{\boldsymbol{d}}
with only positive weights, so
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
is a projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme. If
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
, so
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
is a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme.

The condition that
\mu_{v}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
is unnecessary. As the notions of
\mu
-(semi)stability are unchanged by multiplying all
\mu_{v}
by a positive number, the result holds for
\mu_{v}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
. To allow
\mu_{v}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
, note that the space
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{Q_{0}}
of values
(\mu_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}}
is divided into chambers by finitely many real hyperplanes of the form
\mu(\boldsymbol{e})=\mu(\boldsymbol{d}-\boldsymbol{e})
for
0<\boldsymbol{e}<\boldsymbol{d}
, such that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
depend only on the codimension
k
stratum
S
in the induced stratification of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{Q_{0}}
containing
(\mu_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}}
. As the hyperplanes
\mu(\boldsymbol{e})=\mu(\boldsymbol{d}-\boldsymbol{e})
are rational,
S
contains a rational point
(\tilde{\mu}_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}}
, and then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\tilde{\mu})
and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\tilde{\mu})
. The proposition follows. ∎

5.3
Defining invariants

The next theorem, Conjecture
4.2
(i), (ii) for
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, is proved in Section
6
.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
Q
be a quiver with no oriented cycles, and use the notation of Section
5.1
, so in particular we have a Lie algebra over the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-algebra
R

\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)=\bigoplus_{\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}}\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)=\bigoplus_{\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}}H_{2-2\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big),

for
\chi_{Q}
as in
(
5.3
)
. Then for all weak stability conditions
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
in the sense of Section
3.3
, and for all
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
, there exist unique classes
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\in\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
with the properties:

(i)

Suppose
\mu
is a slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, and
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
with
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
, so that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
is a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme by Proposition
5.7
. Then
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)=1-\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})
, so it has a fundamental class
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\big]_{\rm fund}
in
H_{2-2\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\big)
, and
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}
is the pushforward
\iota_{*}\bigl([{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)]_{\rm fund}\bigr)
under the inclusion
\iota\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\hookrightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
.

This includes the case when
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)=\varnothing
, with
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}=0
.

(ii)

Let
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
and
\big(\tilde{\tau},\tilde{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
be two weak stability conditions on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. Then as for
(
4.1
)
–
(
4.2
)
, for all
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
we have

\displaystyle[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\tau})]_{\rm inv}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}\colon\\
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{d}_{n}=\boldsymbol{d}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\begin{aligned} \tilde{U}(\boldsymbol{d}_{1},&\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\bigl[\bigl[\cdots\bigl[\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv},\\
&\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{2}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}\bigr],\dots\bigr],\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}\bigr],\end{aligned}

(5.6)

\displaystyle[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\tau})]_{\rm inv}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}\colon\\
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{d}_{n}=\boldsymbol{d}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\begin{aligned} U(\boldsymbol{d}_{1},&\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n};\tau,\tilde{\tau})\cdot\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}*\\
&\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{2}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}*\cdots*\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv},\end{aligned}

(5.7)

which are equivalent equations,
(
5.6
)
in the Lie algebra
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
and
(
5.7
)
in its universal enveloping algebra
U\big(\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\big)
.

(iii)

Let
\mu
be an increasing slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. Then

[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}=\begin{cases}1\in H_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)\cong R,&\boldsymbol{d}=\delta_{v},\ v\in Q_{0},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(5.8)

This follows from
(i)
and Proposition
5.6
, noting that in Proposition
5.6
(b)
,
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}
lies in
H_{<0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)=0
as
2-\chi(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})<0
.

The sequel
[
59
, Section 6]
gives an alternative proof of Theorem
5.8
.

5.4
Morphisms of quivers

Here is a new notion of morphisms of quivers, which is designed to be compatible with the morphisms of vertex and Lie algebras in Section
2.5
.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},h,t)
and
Q^{\prime}=(Q_{0}^{\prime},Q_{1}^{\prime},h^{\prime},t^{\prime})
be quivers. A
morphism

\lambda\colon Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
is a pair
\lambda=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
, where
\lambda_{0}\colon Q_{0}\rightarrow Q_{0}^{\prime}
is a map, and
\lambda_{1}\subseteq Q_{1}\times Q_{1}^{\prime}
a subset satisfying:

(i)

If
(e,e^{\prime})\in\lambda_{1}
then
\lambda_{0}\circ h(e)=h^{\prime}(e^{\prime})
and
\lambda_{0}\circ t(e)=t^{\prime}(e^{\prime})
.

(ii)

If
v,w\in Q_{0}
and
e^{\prime}\in Q_{1}^{\prime}
with
\lambda_{0}(v)=h^{\prime}(e^{\prime})
and
\lambda_{0}(w)=t^{\prime}(e^{\prime})
, there exists unique
e\in Q_{1}
such that
(e,e^{\prime})\in\lambda_{1}
and
h(e)=v
,
t(e)=w
.

(iii)

The projection
\pi_{Q_{1}}\colon\lambda_{1}\rightarrow Q_{1}
mapping
(e,e^{\prime})\mapsto e
is injective.

If
Q^{\prime\prime}=(Q_{0}^{\prime\prime},Q_{1}^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime\prime})
is another quiver and
\mu\colon Q^{\prime}\rightarrow Q^{\prime\prime}
is a morphism, the
composition

\mu\circ\lambda=((\mu\circ\lambda)_{0},(\mu\circ\lambda)_{1})
is given by
(\mu\circ\lambda)_{0}=\mu_{0}\circ\lambda_{0}
and

(\mu\circ\lambda)_{1}=\bigl\{(e,e^{\prime\prime})\in Q_{1}\times Q_{1}^{\prime\prime}\colon\text{$\exists e^{\prime}\in Q_{1}^{\prime}$ with $(e,e^{\prime})\in\lambda_{1}$ and $(e^{\prime},e^{\prime\prime})\in\mu_{1}$}\bigr\}.

It is easy to show this is a morphism, and makes quivers into a category.

For
\lambda
as above, define a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear exact functor
\Sigma_{\lambda}\colon\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}\rightarrow\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
by

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\lambda}\colon\ ((V_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}},(\rho_{e})_{e\in Q_{1}})\mapsto((V^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})_{v^{\prime}\in Q_{0}^{\prime}},(\rho^{\prime}_{e^{\prime}})_{e^{\prime}\in Q_{1}^{\prime}})\ \text{on objects},

\displaystyle\text{where}\ V^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}=\bigoplus_{v\in Q_{0}\colon\lambda_{0}(v)=v^{\prime}}V_{v}\quad\text{and}\quad\rho^{\prime}_{e^{\prime}}=\sum_{e\in Q_{1}\colon(e,e^{\prime})\in\lambda_{1}}\rho_{e}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\lambda}\colon\ (\phi_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}}\mapsto(\phi^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})_{v^{\prime}\in Q_{0}^{\prime}}\ \text{on morphisms, where}\ \phi^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}=\sum_{v\in Q_{0}\colon\lambda_{0}(v)=v^{\prime}}\phi_{v}.

If
\mu\colon Q^{\prime}\rightarrow Q^{\prime\prime}
is another morphism then
\Sigma_{\mu\circ\lambda}=\Sigma_{\mu}\circ\Sigma_{\lambda}
.

The induced action
(\Sigma_{\lambda})_{*}\colon K_{0}(\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q})\rightarrow K_{0}(\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime})
descends to

\lambda_{*}\colon\ {\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}^{\prime}},\qquad\lambda_{*}\colon\ \boldsymbol{d}\mapsto\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime},\qquad\text{where}\quad\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}(v^{\prime})=\sum_{v\in Q_{0}\colon\lambda_{0}(v)=v^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{d}(v).

Then
\lambda_{*}
maps
{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}^{\prime}}
and
{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}^{\prime}}\setminus\{0\}
.

If
(\tau^{\prime},T^{\prime},\leqslant\penalty)
is a (weak) stability condition on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
, as in Section
3.3
, it is easy to check that
(\tau^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{*},T^{\prime},\leqslant\penalty)
is a (weak) stability condition on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. If
\mu^{\prime}
is a slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
defined using constants
\mu^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
for
v^{\prime}\in Q_{0}^{\prime}
then
\mu=\mu^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{*}
is the slope function defined using
\mu_{v}=\mu^{\prime}_{\lambda_{0}(v)}
for
v\in Q_{0}
.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
\lambda\colon Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
be a morphism of quivers, as in Definition
5.9
. Write
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime}
for the moduli stacks of objects in
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
,
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
, and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}
for the projective linear moduli stacks, so that Section
5.1
and Sections
2.3
–
2.4
define graded vertex algebras
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
,
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime})
and graded Lie algebras
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
,
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}\big)
. We will use the constructions of Section
2.5
to define morphisms
\Omega:\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\rightarrow\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime})
and
\Omega^{\rm pl}\colon\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\rightarrow\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}\big)
.

In Definition
2.11
(a)–(c), let
T\colon{\mathbin{\cal A}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}
be
\Sigma_{\lambda}\colon\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}\rightarrow\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
, and write
\sigma_{\lambda}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime}
and
\sigma_{\lambda}^{\rm pl}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}
for the induced stack morphisms. Define
\xi\colon{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
and vector bundles
F\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}
,
G\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
by

\displaystyle\xi(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{e})=\sum_{v\neq w\in Q_{0}\colon\lambda_{0}(v)=\lambda_{0}(w)}\boldsymbol{d}(v)\boldsymbol{d}(w)+\sum_{e\in Q_{1}\colon\not\exists(e,e^{\prime})\in\lambda_{1}}\boldsymbol{d}(t(e))\boldsymbol{e}(h(e)),

(5.9)

\displaystyle F=\bigoplus_{v\neq w\in Q_{0}\colon\lambda_{0}(v)=\lambda_{0}(w)}{\cal V}_{v}\boxtimes{\cal V}_{w}^{*}\oplus\bigoplus_{e\in Q_{1}\colon\not\exists(e,e^{\prime})\in\lambda_{1}}{\cal V}_{t(e)}\boxtimes{\cal V}_{h(e)}^{*},

(5.10)

\displaystyle G=\bigoplus_{v\neq w\in Q_{0}\colon\lambda_{0}(v)=\lambda_{0}(w)}{\cal V}_{v}^{*}\otimes{\cal V}_{w}\oplus\bigoplus_{e\in Q_{1}\colon\not\exists(e,e^{\prime})\in\lambda_{1}}{\cal V}_{t(e)}^{*}\otimes{\cal V}_{h(e)}.

(5.11)

We now claim that the conditions Definition
2.11
(i)–(v) hold. From (
5.3
) and Definition
5.9
(especially (i)–(iii)) we can show that

\chi_{Q^{\prime}}(\lambda_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}),\lambda_{*}(\boldsymbol{e}))=\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{e})+\xi(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{e}),

and then Definition
2.11
(i), (ii) follow from the second and third equations of (
5.5
). For (iii), equations (
2.17
)–(
2.20
) follow from obvious compatibilities between
{\cal V}_{v}
and
\Phi
,
\Psi
. For (iv), in
K_{0}(\mathop{\rm Perf}({\mathbin{\cal M}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}))
we have

\displaystyle(\sigma_{\lambda}\times\sigma_{\lambda})^{*}\big(\big[(\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\prime\bullet})^{\vee}\big]\big)=\sum_{v^{\prime}\in Q_{0}^{\prime}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\times\sigma_{\lambda})^{*}([{\cal V}_{v^{\prime}}\boxtimes{\cal V}_{v^{\prime}}^{*}])-\sum_{e^{\prime}\in Q_{1}^{\prime}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\times\sigma_{\lambda})^{*}([{\cal V}_{t^{\prime}(e^{\prime})}\boxtimes{\cal V}_{h^{\prime}(e^{\prime})}^{*}])

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{v^{\prime}\in Q_{0}^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v,w\in Q_{0}\colon\\
\lambda_{0}(v)=\lambda_{0}(w)=v^{\prime}\end{subarray}}[{\cal V}_{v}\boxtimes{\cal V}_{w}^{*}]-\sum_{e^{\prime}\in Q_{1}^{\prime}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v,w\in Q_{0}\colon\\
\lambda_{0}(v)=t^{\prime}(e^{\prime}),\,\lambda_{0}(w)=h^{\prime}(e^{\prime})\end{subarray}}[{\cal V}_{v}\boxtimes{\cal V}_{w}^{*}]

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\bigg(\sum_{v\in Q_{0}}[{\cal V}_{v}\boxtimes{\cal V}_{v}^{*}]+\sum_{v\neq w\in Q_{0}\colon\lambda_{0}(v)=\lambda_{0}(w)}[{\cal V}_{v}\boxtimes{\cal V}_{w}^{*}]\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}-\bigg(\sum_{e\in Q_{1}}[{\cal V}_{t(e)}\boxtimes{\cal V}_{h(e)}^{*}]-\sum_{e\in Q_{1}\colon\not\exists(e,e^{\prime})\in\lambda_{1}}[{\cal V}_{t(e)}\boxtimes{\cal V}_{h(e)}^{*}]\bigg)=\big[(\mathop{{\mathcal{E}}{\rm xt}}\nolimits^{\bullet})^{\vee}\big]+[F],

using (
5.4
) in the first step, and Definition
5.9
in the second, and Definition
5.9
(i)–(iii) to rewrite the sum
\sum_{e^{\prime}\in Q_{1}^{\prime}}\sum_{v,w}
in the third, and (
5.4
) and (
5.10
) in the fourth. Definition
2.11
(iv) then follows from the fourth equation of (
5.5
). Part (v) follows as the vector bundles
{\cal V}_{v}^{*}\otimes{\cal V}_{w}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
descend to
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
, as they have weight 0 for the
[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
-action
\Psi
on
{\mathbin{\cal M}}
.

The additional condition in Definition
2.14
that if
E\neq 0
then
\Sigma_{\lambda}(E)\neq 0
in
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
also holds. Thus Section
2.5
defines morphisms
\Omega
,
\Omega^{\rm pl}
in (
2.21
)–(
2.22
).

The next theorem relates enumerative invariants of quivers
Q
,
Q^{\prime}
linked by a morphism
\lambda\colon Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
. It will be proved in Section
6.2
.

Theorem 5.11
.

In the situation of Definitions
5.9
and
5.10
, let
(\tau^{\prime},T^{\prime},\leqslant\penalty)
be a weak stability condition on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
, so that
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty):=(\tau^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{*},T^{\prime},\leqslant\penalty)
is a weak stability condition on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. Suppose that
Q,Q^{\prime}
have no oriented cycles. Then for all
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
with
\lambda_{*}(\boldsymbol{d})=\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}^{\prime}}\setminus\{0\}
, the invariants of Theorem
5.8
for
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q},\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
satisfy

\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)!\cdot\Omega^{\rm pl}\bigl([{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\bigr)=\prod_{v^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}(v^{\prime})!\cdot[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\tau^{\prime})]_{\rm inv}.

(5.12)

If
\lambda_{0}\colon Q_{0}\!\rightarrow\!Q_{0}^{\prime}
is injective this simplifies to
\Omega^{\rm pl}([{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv})\!=\![{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\tau^{\prime})]_{\rm inv}
.

We will use Theorem
5.11
in Sections
6.4
–
6.5
to prove Theorem
5.8
(i).

5.5
Pair invariants for quivers

Conjecture
4.2
(iii) and Definition
4.3
described the method of pair invariants. We explain how to use this for quivers, proving a version of Conjecture
4.2
(iii).

Definition 5.12
.

Let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},h,t)
be a quiver with no oriented cycles. Choose
n_{v}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}
for
v\in Q_{0}
. Define a new quiver
\tilde{Q}=\big(\tilde{Q}_{0},\tilde{Q}_{1},\tilde{h},\tilde{t}\big)
to be
Q
together with one extra vertex
\infty
, so that
\tilde{Q}_{0}=Q_{0}\amalg\{\infty\}
, and with
n_{v}
extra edges
\overset{\infty}{\bullet}\longrightarrow\overset{v}{\bullet}
for each
v\in Q_{0}
, so that
\tilde{Q}_{1}=Q_{1}\amalg\coprod_{v\in Q_{0}}\{v\}\times\{1,\dots,n_{v}\}
. Then
\tilde{Q}
also has no oriented cycles. There is an obvious inclusion
i\colon\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}\hookrightarrow\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\tilde{Q}}
identifying
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
with the full subcategory of
((V_{v})_{v\in\tilde{Q}_{0}},(\rho_{e})_{e\in\tilde{Q}_{1}})
in
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\tilde{Q}}
with
V_{\infty}=0
. In fact
i=\Sigma_{\lambda}
in Definition
5.9
for
\lambda\colon Q\rightarrow\tilde{Q}
given by
\lambda_{0}\colon v\mapsto v
and
\lambda_{1}=\{(e,e)\colon e\in Q_{1}\}
.

We can now apply Definition
4.3
with
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
and
{\mathbin{\cal B}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\tilde{Q}}
. For Definition
4.3
(i)–(vi), in (i) we take
i\colon\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}\hookrightarrow\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\tilde{Q}}
as above, and in (ii) we define an object
I=\big((W_{v})_{v\in\tilde{Q}_{0}},(\sigma_{e})_{e\in\tilde{Q}_{1}}\big)
in
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\tilde{Q}}
to have
W_{\infty}={\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
, and
W_{v}=0
for all
v\in Q_{0}
, and
\sigma_{e}=0
for all
e\in\tilde{Q}_{1}
. For (iii) we use
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\tilde{Q}_{0}}={\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}\amalg\{\infty\}}={\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}}\oplus{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\{\infty\}}={\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{Q_{0}}\oplus{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
. Parts (iv)–(vi) are obvious. The rest of Definition
4.3
then works in a straightforward way. Consider the map

\big[{-},1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 5.7361pt\widetilde{\kern-5.7361pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.27773pt}\kern 3.27773pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm pl})}\big]\colon\ H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,0)}^{\rm pl}\big)\longrightarrow H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,1)}^{\rm pl}\big).

(5.13)

Using the explicit formula for
[\,,\,]
in
[
58
]
we can prove that (
5.13
) is injective for all
\alpha\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus 0
if and only if
n_{v}>0
for all
v\in Q_{0}
. One way to do this is to consider the line bundle

{\cal V}_{\infty}^{-\sum_{v\in Q_{0}}\alpha(v)}\otimes\bigotimes_{v\in Q_{0}}\mathop{\rm det}\nolimits{\cal V}_{v}\longrightarrow{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,1)}.

This has weight 0 for the
[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
-action on
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,1)}
, and so is the pullback of a line bundle
L\rightarrow{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,1)}^{\rm pl}
. The map (
5.13
) increases homological degree by
2d=2\big(\sum_{v\in Q_{0}}n_{v}\alpha(v)-1\big)
, where
d\geqslant 0
as
\alpha\neq 0
and
n_{v}>0
for all
v\in Q_{0}
. We can show that if
\lambda\in H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,0)}^{\rm pl}\big)
then
\Pi_{*}\bigl(\big[\lambda,1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 5.7361pt\widetilde{\kern-5.7361pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.27773pt}\kern 3.27773pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm pl})}\big]\cap c_{1}(L)^{d}\bigr)
is a nonzero multiple of
\lambda
, where
\Pi\colon{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,1)}^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\alpha,0)}^{\rm pl}
is the forgetful morphism omitting
V_{\infty}
and its edge maps, so
\big[\lambda,1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 5.7361pt\widetilde{\kern-5.7361pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.27773pt}\kern 3.27773pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm pl})}\big]\neq 0
if
\lambda\neq 0
.

However, there is a problem: Theorem
5.8
(i) only gives an explicit geometric definition of
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
when
