{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm st}(\tau)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)

and
\tau
is a slope function
. As the weak stability conditions
(\tau_{\pm},T_{\pm},\leqslant\penalty)
on
{\mathbin{\cal B}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\tilde{Q}}
in Definition
4.3
are clearly not slope functions, Definition
4.3
as written does
not
give an inductive definition of
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
in terms of geometrically defined classes.

We now explain a way to get round this, by a variation of Definition
4.3
which uses only slope functions. Instead of a general weak stability condition
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, we choose a slope function
\mu
on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, defined by constants
(\mu_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}}
. The analogue of the weak stability conditions
(\tau_{\pm},T_{\pm},\leqslant\penalty)
on
{\mathbin{\cal B}}=\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\tilde{Q}}
in Definition
4.3
are slope functions
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\pm}
on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\tilde{Q}}
which depend on a choice of
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
, defined by constants
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\pm,v}=\mu_{v}
for
v\in Q_{0}\subset\tilde{Q}_{0}
, and
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\pm,\infty}=\mu(\boldsymbol{d})\pm\epsilon
for
\infty\in\tilde{Q}_{0}
, where
\epsilon>0
is small.

The important properties of
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\pm}
we need are:

(a)

If
\boldsymbol{e}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
then
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\pm}(\boldsymbol{e},0)=\mu(\boldsymbol{e})
.

(b)

If
\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
with
\boldsymbol{d}=\boldsymbol{e}+\boldsymbol{f}
and
\mu(\boldsymbol{e})<\mu(\boldsymbol{f})
then
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}(\boldsymbol{e},0)<\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}(\boldsymbol{f},1)
,
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}(\boldsymbol{e},1)<\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}(\boldsymbol{f},0)
,
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{-}(\boldsymbol{e},0)<\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{-}(\boldsymbol{f},1)
,
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{-}(\boldsymbol{e},1)<\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{-}(\boldsymbol{f},0)
.

(c)

If
\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
with
\boldsymbol{d}=\boldsymbol{e}+\boldsymbol{f}
and
\mu(\boldsymbol{e})=\mu(\boldsymbol{f})
then
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}(\boldsymbol{e},0)<\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}(\boldsymbol{f},1)
and
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{-}(\boldsymbol{e},1)<\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{-}(\boldsymbol{f},0)
.

(d)

\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}(0,1)>\mu(\boldsymbol{d})
and
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{-}(0,1)<\mu(\boldsymbol{d})
.

Here
\epsilon>0
needs to be small to ensure
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}(\boldsymbol{e},1)<\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}(\boldsymbol{f},0)
and
\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{-}(\boldsymbol{e},0)<\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{-}(\boldsymbol{f},1)
in (b). Then
\mu
,
\mu_{+}^{\boldsymbol{d}}
,
\mu_{-}^{\boldsymbol{d}}
satisfy all the same inequalities for
\tau
,
\tau_{+}
,
\tau_{-}
used to prove (
4.4
) with
\alpha=\boldsymbol{d}
. So combining (
4.4
) with Theorem
5.8
(i) and Definition
4.3
(a), (b) proves that

\displaystyle\tilde{\iota}_{*}\bigl(\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\boldsymbol{d},1)}^{\rm ss}\big(\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}\big)\big]_{\rm fund}\bigr)=

(5.14)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}\colon\\
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{d}_{n}=\boldsymbol{d},\\
\mu(\boldsymbol{d}_{i})=\mu(\boldsymbol{d}),\,i=1,\dots,n\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\cdot\bigl[\bigl[\cdots\bigl[[1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 5.7361pt\widetilde{\kern-5.7361pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.27773pt}\kern 3.27773pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm pl})},i_{*}^{\rm pl}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}\big)\bigr],\dots\bigr],i_{*}^{\rm pl}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}\big)\bigr].

Here
\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\boldsymbol{d},1)}^{\rm ss}\big(\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}\big)\big]_{\rm fund}
is the fundamental class of the smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\boldsymbol{d},1)}^{\rm st}\!\big(\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}\big)\allowbreak={\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\boldsymbol{d},1)}^{\rm ss}\big(\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}\big)
, and is geometrically defined. As for (
4.5
), we can rewrite (
5.14
) as

\tilde{\iota}_{*}\bigl(\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\boldsymbol{d},1)}^{\rm ss}\big(\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}\big)\big]_{\rm fund}\bigr)=\bigl[i_{*}^{\rm pl}\bigl(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}\bigr),1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 5.7361pt\widetilde{\kern-5.7361pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.27773pt}\kern 3.27773pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm pl})}\bigr]+\text{lower order terms},

(5.15)

where the operation
\big[{-},1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 5.7361pt\widetilde{\kern-5.7361pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.27773pt}\kern 3.27773pt}}}_{(0,1)}^{\rm pl})}\big]
is injective if
n_{v}>0
for all
v
in
Q_{0}
, and
i_{*}^{\rm pl}
is an isomorphism. Then we can use (
5.15
) to determine
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}
uniquely in terms of
\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{(\boldsymbol{d},1)}^{\rm ss}\big(\tilde{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{+}\big)\big]_{\rm fund}
and
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}
for
|\boldsymbol{e}|<|\boldsymbol{d}|
, by induction on increasing
|\boldsymbol{d}|
.

6
Proofs of Theorems
5.8
and
5.11

We now prove Theorems
5.8
and
5.11
. Firstly, in Section
6.1
, we prove that there are unique classes
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}
satisfying Theorem
5.8
(ii), (iii), but without yet showing that they satisfy Theorem
5.8
(i). Then Section
6.2
shows that Theorem
5.11
holds for these classes
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}
. Sections
6.3
–
6.6
prove that Theorem
5.8
(i) also holds, using Theorem
5.11
as a tool.

6.1
Proof of Theorem
5.8
(ii), (iii)

Proposition 6.1
.

In the situation of Theorem
5.8
, there exist unique classes
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}
in
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
satisfying Theorem
5.8
(ii)
,
(iii)
.

Proof.

Fix an increasing slope function
\mu
on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, which is possible as in Definition
5.5
since
Q
has no oriented cycles. Then Theorem
5.8
(iii) determines the classes
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}
. Let
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
be any weak stability condition on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. Define classes
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}\in\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
for all
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
by the equivalent equations (
5.6
)–(
5.7
) with
\mu,\tau
in place of
\tau
,
\tilde{\tau}
. Note that when
\tau=\mu
, equation (
3.16
) implies that this recovers the classes
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}
in (
5.8
), so Theorem
5.8
(iii) holds for this fixed increasing slope function
\mu
.

We claim that these classes
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}
satisfy Theorem
5.8
(ii). To see this, let
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
,
\big(\tilde{\tau},\tilde{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
be weak stability conditions on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. Then

\displaystyle\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{e}_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}\colon\\
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{e}_{n}=\boldsymbol{d}\end{subarray}}\begin{aligned} U(\boldsymbol{e}_{1},&\dots,\boldsymbol{e}_{n};\mu,\tau)\cdot[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}*\\
&[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{2}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}*\cdots*[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv},\end{aligned}

(6.1)

\displaystyle\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\tau})\big]_{\rm inv}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{e}_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}:\\
\boldsymbol{e}_{1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{e}_{n}=\boldsymbol{d}\end{subarray}}\begin{aligned} U(\boldsymbol{e}_{1},&\dots,\boldsymbol{e}_{n};\mu,\tilde{\tau})\cdot[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}*\\
&[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{2}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}*\cdots*[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}.\end{aligned}

(6.2)

To verify (
5.7
) for
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)
,
\big(\tilde{\tau},\tilde{T},{\leqslant\penalty}\big)
, substitute (
6.2
) into the left-hand side of (
5.7
), and substitute (
6.1
) with
\boldsymbol{d}_{i}
in place of
\boldsymbol{d}
into each term
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{i}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}
on the right-hand side. Multiplying out, we see that the coefficients of
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{1}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}*\cdots*[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}_{n}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}
on each side are equal by equation (
3.17
) of Theorem
3.13
with
\mu
,
\tau
,
\tilde{\tau}
,
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
in place of
\tau
,
\hat{\tau}
,
\tilde{\tau}
,
\alpha_{i}
. Thus (
5.7
) holds, so (
5.6
) also holds as it is equivalent by Theorem
3.15
. This proves Theorem
5.8
(ii).

Next, suppose for a contradiction that
\tilde{\mu}
is another increasing slope function, and (
5.8
) fails for
\tilde{\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
. Let
\mu
,
\tilde{\mu}
be defined using constants
(\mu_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}}
,
\allowbreak(\tilde{\mu}_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}}
in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{Q_{0}}
. Observe that the condition on
(\mu_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}}
for
\mu
to be increasing in Definition
5.5
defines an open convex subset
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{Q_{0}}_{>}
of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{Q_{0}}
. Choose a generic smooth path
\big(\mu_{v}^{t}\big)_{v\in Q_{0}}
for
t\in[0,1]
in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{Q_{0}}_{>}
with
\mu_{v}^{0}=\mu_{v}
and
\mu_{v}^{1}=\tilde{\mu}_{v}
for
v\in Q_{0}
. This defines a generic smooth path
\mu^{t}
of increasing slope functions on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
with
\mu^{0}=\mu
and
\mu^{1}=\tilde{\mu}
. Define

t_{0}=\inf\bigl\{t\in[0,1]\colon\text{{\rm(\ref{wc5eq9})} fails for $\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}\big(\mu^{t}\big)\big]_{\rm inv}$ for some $\boldsymbol{d}$ with $0<\boldsymbol{d}\leqslant\penalty\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}$}\bigr\}.

Divide into cases:

(a)

(
5.8
) holds for
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}\big(\mu^{t_{0}}\big)\big]_{\rm inv}
whenever
0<\boldsymbol{d}\leqslant\penalty\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}
. Then let
\epsilon>0
be small and
0<\boldsymbol{d}\leqslant\penalty\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}
such that (
5.8
) fails for
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}\big(\mu^{t_{0}+\epsilon}\big)\big]_{\rm inv}
, and set
\mu^{\prime}=\mu^{t_{0}}
and
\mu^{\prime\prime}=\mu^{t_{0}+\epsilon}
.

(b)

(
5.8
) does not hold for
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}\big(\mu^{t_{0}}\big)\big]_{\rm inv}
, some
0<\boldsymbol{d}\leqslant\penalty\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}
. Then let
\epsilon>0
be small and set
\mu^{\prime}=\mu^{t_{0}-\epsilon}
and
\mu^{\prime\prime}=\mu^{t_{0}}
.

In both cases,
\mu^{\prime}
,
\mu^{\prime\prime}
are increasing such that (
5.8
) holds for
\mu^{\prime}
and all
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}\leqslant\penalty\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}
, but fails for
\mu^{\prime\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{d}
. Furthermore, as
\epsilon
is small, in case (a)
\mu^{\prime}
dominates
\mu^{\prime\prime}
in the sense of Definition
3.5
, and in case (b)
\mu^{\prime\prime}
dominates
\mu^{\prime}
. This holds because any slope function
\mu_{1}
on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
dominates all slope functions
\mu_{2}
in a sufficiently small neighbourhood of
\mu_{1}
in the space of slope functions
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{Q_{0}}
.

Consider (
5.7
) with
\mu^{\prime}
,
\mu^{\prime\prime}
in place of
\tau
,
\tilde{\tau}
. To get a nonzero term on the right-hand side, we must have
\boldsymbol{d}_{i}=\delta_{v_{i}}
for
v_{i}\in Q_{0}
and
i=1,\dots,n
, as (
5.8
) holds for
\mu^{\prime}
. Also, if
U(\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n};\mu^{\prime},\mu^{\prime\prime})\neq 0
then (
3.18
) gives
\mu^{\prime}(\delta_{v_{1}})=\cdots=\mu^{\prime}(\delta_{v_{n}})
in case (a), as
\mu^{\prime}
dominates
\mu^{\prime\prime}
, and
\mu^{\prime\prime}(\delta_{v_{1}})=\cdots=\mu^{\prime\prime}(\delta_{v_{n}})
in case (b). As
\mu^{\prime}
,
\mu^{\prime\prime}
are increasing, Definition
5.5
implies that there are no edges in
Q
joining
v_{i}
,
v_{j}
for any
i,j=1,\dots,n
. The definition of the Lie bracket on
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
and (
5.8
) for
\mu^{\prime}
then implies that

\bigl[\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\delta_{v_{i}}}^{\rm ss}(\mu^{\prime})\big]_{\rm inv},\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\delta_{v_{j}}}^{\rm ss}(\mu^{\prime})\big]_{\rm inv}\bigr]=0.

Thus rewriting (
5.7
) in the form (
5.6
), we see that every term on the right-hand side is zero, so
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu^{\prime\prime})\big]_{\rm inv}=0
, a contradiction, as (
5.8
) fails for
\mu^{\prime\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{d}
. Therefore Theorem
5.8
(iii) holds. This completes the proof.
∎

6.2
Proof of Theorem
5.11

Given Sections
6.3
–
6.6
, the next proposition is equivalent to Theorem
5.11
.

Proposition 6.2
.

In the situation of Theorem
5.11
, the classes
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}
in
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
defined in Proposition
6.1
satisfy
(
5.12
)
.

Proof.

We divide into three cases, depending on the morphism
\lambda\colon Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
:

(a)

We have
Q_{0}=Q_{0}^{\prime}
and
\lambda_{0}={\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{Q_{0}}
.

(b)

The projection
\pi_{Q_{1}}\colon\lambda_{1}\rightarrow Q_{1}
in Definition
5.9
(iii) is a bijection.

(c)

The general case.

Now any
\lambda=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})\colon Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
may be written
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime\prime}\circ\lambda^{\prime}
, for
\lambda^{\prime}\colon Q\rightarrow\tilde{Q}
as in (a) and
\lambda^{\prime\prime}\colon\tilde{Q}\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
as in (b), where
\tilde{Q}
is
Q
with edges
e
in
Q_{1}\setminus\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\pi_{Q_{1}}
deleted, and
\lambda^{\prime}=({\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{Q_{0}},\{(e,e)\colon e\in\tilde{Q}_{1}\})
,
\lambda^{\prime\prime}=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
. Clearly (
5.12
) for
\lambda^{\prime}
,
\lambda^{\prime\prime}
imply (
5.12
) for
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime\prime}\circ\lambda^{\prime}
. Thus (a), (b) imply (c).

For both (a), (b), we will give different constructions of slope functions
\mu^{\prime}
and
\mu=\mu^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{*}
on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
,
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
with both
\mu
,
\mu^{\prime}
increasing, and prove (
5.12
) holds for
\mu
,
\mu^{\prime}
. Then we complete the proofs of (a), (b) together.

For (a), note that the map
\mu^{\prime}\mapsto\mu=\mu^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{*}
is a bijection from slope functions on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
to slope functions on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
. Choose
\mu^{\prime}
such that
\mu=\mu^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{*}
is an increasing slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. Then
\mu^{\prime}
is also increasing, since edges of
Q^{\prime}
are also edges of
Q
. Hence (
5.8
) holds for
\mu
on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
and for
\mu^{\prime}
on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
. To verify (
5.12
) with
\mu
,
\mu^{\prime}
in place of
\tau
,
\tau^{\prime}
, note that if
\boldsymbol{d}=\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}=\delta_{v}
then
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}=\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\tau^{\prime})\big]_{\rm inv}=1
and
\Omega^{\rm pl}={\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}
on
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
as
\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits G^{\rm pl}_{\boldsymbol{d}}=\xi(\delta_{v},\delta_{v})=0
, and if
\boldsymbol{d}\neq\delta_{v}
then both sides of (
5.12
) are zero by (
5.8
). So (
5.12
) holds for
\mu
,
\mu^{\prime}
.

For (b), let
\mu^{\prime}
be an increasing slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
with constants
(\mu^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})_{v^{\prime}\in Q_{0}^{\prime}}
, and set
\mu=\mu^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{*}
. Then
\mu
is a slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, with constants
(\mu_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}}
for
\mu_{v}=\mu^{\prime}_{\lambda_{0}(v)}
. If
\overset{v}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e}}}\,\overset{w}{\bullet}
is an edge in
Q
there exists unique
e^{\prime}\in Q_{1}^{\prime}
with
(e,e^{\prime})\in\lambda_{1}
, and then
\overset{\lambda_{0}(v)}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e^{\prime}}}}\,\overset{\lambda_{0}(w)}{\bullet}
is an edge in
Q^{\prime}
. Hence as
\mu^{\prime}
is increasing we have
\mu_{v}=\mu^{\prime}_{\lambda_{0}(v)}<\mu^{\prime}_{\lambda_{0}(w)}=\mu_{w}
, so
\mu
is also increasing.

If
\boldsymbol{d}=\delta_{v}
for some
v\in Q_{0}
, so that
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}=\delta_{\lambda_{0}(v)}
, then
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}=\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\tau^{\prime})\big]_{\rm inv}=1
by (
5.8
) and we can show (
5.12
) holds in a similar way to (a). If
\boldsymbol{d}\neq\delta_{v}
for any
v\in Q_{0}
then
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}\neq\delta_{v^{\prime}}
for any
v^{\prime}\in Q_{0}^{\prime}
, so
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)\big]_{\rm inv}=\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\tau^{\prime})\big]_{\rm inv}=0
by (
5.8
) as
\mu
,
\mu^{\prime}
are increasing, and (
5.12
) holds trivially.

Note that in both cases (a), (b), in (
5.12
) for
\mu
,
\mu^{\prime}
either both sides are zero, or
\boldsymbol{d}
,
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}
are
binary
in the sense of Definition
6.3
below, so the mysterious factors
\prod_{v}\boldsymbol{d}(v)!
,
\prod_{v^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}(v^{\prime})!
in (
5.12
) are 1.

Now in both cases (a), (b), let
(\tau^{\prime},T^{\prime},\leqslant\penalty)
be any weak stability condition on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, set
(\tau,T,\leqslant\penalty)=(\tau^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{*},T^{\prime},\leqslant\penalty)
, and let
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
with
\lambda_{*}(\boldsymbol{d})=\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}^{\prime}}\setminus\{0\}
. Then

\displaystyle\Omega^{\rm pl}\bigl([{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\tau)]_{\rm inv}\bigr)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}\colon\\
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{d}_{n}=\boldsymbol{d},\\
\text{set $\boldsymbol{d}_{i}^{\prime}=\lambda_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}_{i})$}\end{subarray}}\begin{aligned} U(\boldsymbol{d}_{1},&\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n};\mu,\tau)\cdot\frac{\prod_{i=1,\dots,n,\,v^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{i}(v^{\prime})!}{\prod_{i=1,\dots,n,\,v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}_{i}(v)!}\\
&[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})]_{\rm inv}*\cdots*[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{n}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})]_{\rm inv}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}^{\prime}}\setminus\{0\}\colon\\
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{n}=\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\begin{aligned} U(\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{1},&\dots,\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{n};\mu^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\cdot[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})]_{\rm inv}*\cdots*[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{n}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})]_{\rm inv}\\[-3.0pt]
&{}\cdot\raisebox{-6.0pt}{$\biggl[$}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\colon\\
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{d}_{n}=\boldsymbol{d},\,\lambda_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}_{i})=\boldsymbol{d}_{i}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\raisebox{-6.0pt}{$\displaystyle\frac{\prod_{i=1,\dots,n,\,v^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{i}(v^{\prime})!}{\prod_{i=1,\dots,n,\,v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}_{i}(v)!}\biggr]$}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1,\,\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}^{\prime}}\setminus\{0\}\colon\\
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{n}=\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\begin{aligned} U(\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{1},&\dots,\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{n};\mu^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})\cdot[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})]_{\rm inv}*\cdots*[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{n}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})]_{\rm inv}\\
&{}\cdot\Biggl[\frac{\prod_{v^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}(v^{\prime})!}{\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)!}\Biggr].\end{aligned}

(6.3)

Here the first step holds by applying the Lie algebra morphism
\Omega^{\rm pl}
to (
5.7
) for
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
with
\mu
,
\tau
in place of
\tau
,
\tilde{\tau}
and using (
5.12
) for
\mu
,
\mu^{\prime}
. In the second we rewrite the sums and use
U(\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{n};\mu^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})=U(\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n};\mu,\tau)
as
\mu=\mu^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{*}
,
\tau=\tau^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{*}
and
\boldsymbol{d}_{i}^{\prime}=\lambda_{*}(\boldsymbol{d}_{i})
.

The third step holds by a combinatorial identity showing the brackets
[\cdots]
are equal. It may be written as the product over
v^{\prime}\in Q_{0}^{\prime}
of the simpler identity

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{v^{\prime}},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n}^{v^{\prime}}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{\lambda_{0}^{-1}(v^{\prime})}\colon\\
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{v^{\prime}}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{d}_{n}^{v^{\prime}}=\boldsymbol{d}^{v^{\prime}},\,\sum_{v\in\lambda_{0}^{-1}(v^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{d}_{i}^{v^{\prime}}(v)=\boldsymbol{d}_{i}^{\prime}(v^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{i=1,\dots,n}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{i}(v^{\prime})!}{\prod_{i=1,\dots,n,\,v\in\lambda_{0}^{-1}(v^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{d}_{i}^{v^{\prime}}(v)!}=\frac{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}(v^{\prime})!}{\prod_{v\in\lambda_{0}^{-1}(v^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{d}^{v^{\prime}}(v)!},

writing
\boldsymbol{d}^{v^{\prime}}
,
\boldsymbol{d}_{i}^{v^{\prime}}
for the restrictions of
\boldsymbol{d}
,
\boldsymbol{d}_{i}
to
\lambda_{0}^{-1}(v^{\prime})\subseteq Q_{0}
. Equivalently,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{v^{\prime}},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n}^{v^{\prime}}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{\lambda_{0}^{-1}(v^{\prime})}\colon\\
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{v^{\prime}}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{d}_{n}^{v^{\prime}}=\boldsymbol{d}^{v^{\prime}},\,\sum_{v\in\lambda_{0}^{-1}(v^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{d}_{i}^{v^{\prime}}(v)=\boldsymbol{d}_{i}^{\prime}(v^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{v\in\lambda_{0}^{-1}(v^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{d}^{v^{\prime}}(v)!}{\prod_{i=1,\dots,n,\,v\in\lambda_{0}^{-1}(v^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{d}_{i}^{v^{\prime}}(v)!}=\frac{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}(v^{\prime})!}{\prod_{i=1,\dots,n}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{i}(v^{\prime})!},

which follows from considering the number of ways of dividing a set of size
\sum_{v\in\lambda_{0}^{-1}(v^{\prime})}\boldsymbol{d}^{v^{\prime}}(v)=\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}(v^{\prime})
into
n
subsets of size
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{i}(v^{\prime})
for
i=1,\dots,n
. Equation (
6.3
) implies (
5.12
) for
\tau
,
\tau^{\prime}
. This proves (a), (b), and (c) follows as above. ∎

6.3
Proof of Theorem
5.8
(i) for
\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{d}}
binary,
\boldsymbol{Q}
a tree

We will break the proof of Theorem
5.8
(i) into four cases in Sections
6.3
–
6.6
, depending on the following definition.

Definition 6.3
.

Work in the situation of Theorem
5.8
. We call a dimension vector
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}

binary
if
\boldsymbol{d}(v)\in\{0,1\}
for all
v\in Q_{0}
. The
support

\mathop{\rm supp}\boldsymbol{d}
of
\boldsymbol{d}
is the subquiver
Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q
containing all vertices
v\in Q_{0}
with
\boldsymbol{d}(v)>0
, and all edges linking two such vertices. We say that
\mathop{\rm supp}\boldsymbol{d}

is a tree
if
Q^{\prime}
is connected and simply-connected.

Let
\mu
be a slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
, and
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
. We call the pair
\mu
,
\boldsymbol{d}

generic
if whenever
\boldsymbol{d}=\boldsymbol{e}+\boldsymbol{f}
for
\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
we have
\mu(\boldsymbol{e})\neq\mu(\boldsymbol{f})
. This implies that there are no strictly
\mu
-semistable objects in class
\boldsymbol{d}
, so
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm st}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)
. Note that
\mu
,
\boldsymbol{d}
cannot be generic if
\boldsymbol{d}=n\boldsymbol{e}
for
n>1
.

The proof of the next proposition uses the Donaldson–Thomas theory of quivers in Joyce–Song
[
60
, Section 7]
, which satisfy a wall-crossing formula like (
5.6
).

Proposition 6.4
.

The classes
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}
in
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
defined in Proposition
6.1
satisfy Theorem
5.8
(i)
when
\boldsymbol{d}
is binary and
\mathop{\rm supp}\boldsymbol{d}
is a tree.

Proof.

Suppose
\boldsymbol{d}
is binary,
\mathop{\rm supp}\boldsymbol{d}
is a tree, and
\mu
is a slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
with
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
. Now
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
is a smooth Artin
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-stack with
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}=1-\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})
, for
\chi_{Q}
as in (
5.3
). As
\boldsymbol{d}
is binary and
\mathop{\rm supp}\boldsymbol{d}
is a tree, we see that
\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})=1
, giving
\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits_{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}=0
, so as
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
is open in
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
, it is smooth of dimension 0. Also
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)=[R^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}]
with
R^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\subseteq R_{\boldsymbol{d}}\cong{\mathbin{\mathbb{A}}}^{n}
open, so
R^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
and hence
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
are connected. This implies that

\text{${\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)$ is either a point $*$ or empty}.

(6.4)

Now let
\tilde{\mu}
be an increasing slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. Equations (
3.23
) and (
5.6
) with
\tilde{\mu}
,
\mu
in place of
\tau
,
\tilde{\tau}
and (
5.8
) for
\tilde{\mu}
yield

\displaystyle\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}\colon n=|\boldsymbol{d}|,\\
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{d}_{n}=\boldsymbol{d},\,\boldsymbol{d}_{i}=\delta_{v_{i}},\;v_{i}\in Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\begin{aligned} \tilde{U}(\boldsymbol{d}_{1},&\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n};\tilde{\mu},\mu)\cdot\bigl[\bigl[\cdots\bigl[\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}(\tilde{\mu}),\\
&\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{d}_{2}}(\tilde{\mu})\bigr],\dots\bigr],\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{d}_{n}}(\tilde{\mu})\bigr],\end{aligned}

(6.5)

\displaystyle[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}\colon n=|\boldsymbol{d}|,\\
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{d}_{n}=\boldsymbol{d},\;\boldsymbol{d}_{i}=\delta_{v_{i}},\,v_{i}\in Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\begin{aligned} \tilde{U}(\boldsymbol{d}_{1},&\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n};\tilde{\mu},\mu)\cdot\bigl[\bigl[\cdots\bigl[1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}^{\rm pl})},\\
&1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{2}}^{\rm pl})}\bigr],\dots\bigr],1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{n}}^{\rm pl})}\bigr].\end{aligned}

(6.6)

Here we use Proposition
5.6
and (
5.8
) to deduce that the only nonzero terms on the right-hand sides of (
3.23
) and (
5.6
) are when
\boldsymbol{d}_{i}=\delta_{v_{i}}
for
i=1,\dots,n
, and then
n=|\boldsymbol{d}|:=\sum_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)
. Equations (
6.5
) and (
6.6
) are in the Lie algebras
\mathop{\rm SF_{al}^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
and
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
.

Now Joyce and Song
[
60
, Section 7]
define invariants
\bar{DT}{}_{Q}^{\boldsymbol{e}}(\mu)
for quivers
Q
(this is the special case when the superpotential is
W=0
). In
[
60
, Section 7.3]
they define an explicit Lie algebra
L(Q)
over
R
(they take
R={\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
and write
\tilde{L}(Q)
), with basis given by symbols
\lambda^{\boldsymbol{e}}
for
\boldsymbol{e}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}
, and Lie bracket

\big[\lambda^{\boldsymbol{e}},\lambda^{\boldsymbol{f}}\big]=(-1)^{\bar{\chi}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})}\bar{\chi}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})\lambda^{\boldsymbol{e}+\boldsymbol{f}},

where
\bar{\chi}
is the
anti-symmetric Euler form

\bar{\chi}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f}):=\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})-\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{e})
. They define a Lie algebra morphism
\Psi\colon\mathop{\rm SF_{al}^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})\rightarrow L(Q)
(this is difficult), for
\mathop{\rm SF_{al}^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})
as in Section
3.2
, and they define
\bar{DT}{}_{Q}^{\boldsymbol{e}}(\mu)\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}\subseteq R
by
\Psi(\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{e}}(\mu))=-\bar{DT}{}_{Q}^{\boldsymbol{e}}(\mu)\lambda^{\boldsymbol{e}}
, for
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{e}}(\mu)
as in Section
3.4
.

Define Lie algebra ideals

\displaystyle L(Q)_{\text{non-bin}}=\bigoplus_{\boldsymbol{e}\ \text{non-binary}}R\cdot\lambda^{\boldsymbol{e}}\subseteq L(Q),

\displaystyle\check{H}_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl})_{\text{non-bin}}=\bigoplus_{\boldsymbol{e}\ \text{non-binary}}\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}_{\boldsymbol{e}}\big)\subseteq\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}_{Q}\big),

noting that if
\boldsymbol{e}
or
\boldsymbol{f}
is non-binary then so is
\boldsymbol{e}+\boldsymbol{f}
. Define Lie subalgebras

\displaystyle L(Q)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{conn}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\boldsymbol{e}$ connected support},\\
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})\subseteq\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})\end{subarray}}R\cdot\lambda^{\boldsymbol{e}}\subseteq L(Q),

\displaystyle\check{H}_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl})^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{conn}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\boldsymbol{e}$ connected support},\\
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})\subseteq\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})\end{subarray}}\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}_{\boldsymbol{e}}\big)\subseteq\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big).

These are Lie subalgebras as if
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})
,
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{f})
are disjoint and not connected by any edges then the Lie brackets between
\lambda^{\boldsymbol{e}}
,
\lambda^{\boldsymbol{f}}
and
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}_{\boldsymbol{e}}\big)
,
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}_{\boldsymbol{f}}\big)
are zero. The quotients

\displaystyle L(Q)_{\text{bin}}^{\boldsymbol{d}}=L(Q)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{conn}}/(L(Q)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{conn}}\cap L(Q)_{\text{non-bin}}),

\displaystyle\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{bin}}=\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{conn}}/\big(\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{conn}}\cap\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)_{\text{non-bin}}\big)

are Lie algebras.

Define an
R
-linear map
\Upsilon\colon L(Q)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{bin}}\rightarrow\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{bin}}
by
\lambda^{\boldsymbol{e}}\mapsto-1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\rm pl})}
. We will show
\Upsilon
is a Lie algebra morphism, as
\Upsilon\big(\big[\lambda^{\boldsymbol{e}},\lambda^{\boldsymbol{f}}\big]\big)=\big[\Upsilon(\lambda^{\boldsymbol{e}}),\Upsilon\big(\lambda^{\boldsymbol{f}}\big)\big]
, that is

\begin{split}&\big[1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\rm pl})},1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\rm pl})}\big]=-(-1)^{\bar{\chi}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})}\bar{\chi}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})\cdot 1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}+\boldsymbol{f}}^{\rm pl})}\\
&\qquad\mod\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{conn}}\cap\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)_{\text{non-bin}},\end{split}

(6.7)

for
\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
binary with
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})
,
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{f})
connected and in
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})
.

Using the definition of the Lie bracket on
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)
via Proposition
2.3
and
\check{H}_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)=\hat{H}_{*+2}({\mathbin{\cal M}})/D\big(\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\big)
, and the definition (
2.11
) of the vertex algebra structure on
\hat{H}_{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}})
, we find that

\begin{split}&\big[1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\rm pl})},1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\rm pl})}\big]\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\geqslant 0\colon\\
i\geqslant\chi(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})+1\end{subarray}}\begin{aligned} &\frac{(-1)^{\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})}}{(i-\chi(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})-1)!}H_{*}(\Phi)\circ\big(D^{i-\chi(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})-1}\otimes{\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}\big)\\
&\bigl((1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}})}\boxtimes 1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{f}})})\cap c_{i}(\Theta^{\bullet})\bigr)\end{aligned}\\
&\quad{}+D\big(\hat{H}_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}})\big),\quad\mod\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)_{\text{conn}}\cap\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)_{\text{non-bin}}.\end{split}

(6.8)

Equation (
6.8
) implies that

\displaystyle\big[1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\rm pl})},1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\rm pl})}\big]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0,&\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})\cap\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{f})\neq\varnothing,\\
0,&\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})\cap\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{f})=\varnothing,\;\>\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})=\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{e})=0,\\
1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}+\boldsymbol{f}}^{\rm pl})},&\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})\cap\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{f})=\varnothing,\ \chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})=0,\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{e})=-1,\\
-1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}+\boldsymbol{f}}^{\rm pl})},&\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})\cap\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{f})=\varnothing,\ \chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})=-1,\ \chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{e})=0,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\quad\mod\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{conn}}\cap\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)_{\text{non-bin}},

(6.9)

where the four cases realize all possibilities. To see this, note that if
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})\cap\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{f})\neq\varnothing
then
\boldsymbol{e}+\boldsymbol{f}
is non-binary, so
\big[1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}}^{\rm pl})},1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{f}}^{\rm pl})}\big]\in\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)_{\text{non-bin}}
, giving the first case. If
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})\cap\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{f})=\varnothing
then as
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})
,
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{f})
lie in
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})
, which is a tree, there is at most one edge joining
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})
,
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{f})
, so the possibilities are
\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})=\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{e})=0
(if no edges), or
\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})=0
,
\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{e})=-1
(if one edge
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{f})\rightarrow\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})
, by (
5.3
)), or
\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})=-1
,
\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{e})=0
(if one edge
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{e})\rightarrow\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{f})
).

To prove (
6.9
), the first case is immediate. In (
6.8
), we have
\big(1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{e}})}\boxtimes 1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{f}})}\big)\cap c_{i}(\Theta^{\bullet})=0
unless
i=0
, for dimension reasons. As
\chi(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})=\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})+\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{e})
, the condition
i\geqslant\chi(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})+1
in (
6.8
) means there are no nonzero terms in (
6.8
) in the second case of (
6.9
). For the third and fourth cases, there is one nonzero term in (
6.8
), with
i=0
,
\chi(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})=-1
, and
(-1)^{\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})}
is 1 in the third case and
-1
in the fourth. Equation (
6.9
) follows, and as
\bar{\chi}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})=\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{f})-\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{f},\boldsymbol{e})
this implies (
6.7
). Hence
\Upsilon
is a Lie algebra morphism, as claimed.

Applying the Lie algebra morphism
\Psi\colon\mathop{\rm SF_{al}^{ind}}\nolimits({\mathbin{\cal M}})\rightarrow L(Q)
to (
6.5
) gives an equation in
L(Q)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{conn}}\subseteq L(Q)
. Composing with the Lie algebra projection
L(Q)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{conn}}\rightarrow L(Q)_{\text{bin}}^{\boldsymbol{d}}
, and then applying the Lie algebra morphism
\Upsilon\colon L(Q)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{bin}}\rightarrow\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{bin}}
, gives an identity in
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{bin}}
:

\Upsilon\circ\Psi(\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}\colon n=|\boldsymbol{d}|,\\
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}+\cdots+\boldsymbol{d}_{n}=\boldsymbol{d},\,\boldsymbol{d}_{i}=\delta_{v_{i}},\,v_{i}\in Q_{0}\end{subarray}}\begin{aligned} \tilde{U}(\boldsymbol{d}_{1},&\dots,\boldsymbol{d}_{n};\tilde{\mu},\mu)\cdot\bigl[\bigl[\cdots\bigl[\Upsilon\!\circ\!\Psi(\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}(\tilde{\mu})),\\
&\Upsilon\circ\Psi(\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{d}_{2}}(\tilde{\mu}))\bigr],\dots\bigr],\Upsilon\!\circ\!\Psi(\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{d}_{n}}(\tilde{\mu}))\bigr].\end{aligned}

(6.10)

Now (
6.6
) is an identity in
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{conn}}
, so we can apply the Lie algebra morphism
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{conn}}\allowbreak\rightarrow\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{bin}}
, and compare it with (
6.10
). Using
\boldsymbol{d}_{i}=\delta_{v_{i}}
, Proposition
5.6
, and the definitions of
\Psi
,
\Upsilon
, we find that

\Upsilon\circ\Psi\big(\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{d}_{i}}(\tilde{\mu})\big)=\Upsilon\big({-}\lambda^{\boldsymbol{d}_{i}}\big)=1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{i}}^{\rm pl})}.

(6.11)

Note that this uses the complicated definition of
\Psi
in
[
60
, Section 7.3]
, which we have not explained. But in this case,
\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{d}_{i}}(\tilde{\mu})
is the stack function
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{i}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
, where
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{i}}\cong[*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}]
as
\boldsymbol{d}_{i}=\delta_{v_{i}}
, and showing
\Psi\big(\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{d}_{i}}(\tilde{\mu})\big)=-\lambda^{\boldsymbol{d}_{i}}
is straightforward. Therefore the right-hand sides of (
6.10
) and the projection of (
6.6
) agree, so

\Upsilon\circ\Psi\big(\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\big)=[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}\mod\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)^{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\text{conn}}\cap\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)_{\text{non-bin}}.

(6.12)

But (
6.4
) and the proof of (
6.11
) imply that

\Upsilon\circ\Psi\big(\bar{\epsilon}{}^{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\big)=\begin{cases}1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{i}}^{\rm pl})},&{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\cong*,\\
0,&{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)=\varnothing.\end{cases}

(6.13)

Combining (
6.12
)–(
6.13
) proves Theorem
5.8
(i) for
\mu
,
\boldsymbol{d}
.
∎

6.4
Proof of Theorem
5.8
(i) for
\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{d}}
binary

Consider the following situation:

Definition 6.5
.

Let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},h,t)
,
Q^{\prime}=(Q_{0}^{\prime},Q_{1}^{\prime},h^{\prime},t^{\prime})
be quivers with
Q_{0}^{\prime}=Q_{0}
,
Q_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq Q_{1}
,
h^{\prime}=h|_{Q_{1}^{\prime}}
,
t^{\prime}=t|_{Q_{1}^{\prime}}
, that is,
Q^{\prime}
is a subquiver of
Q
obtained by deleting the edges
Q_{1}\setminus Q_{1}^{\prime}
. Then
\lambda=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})\colon Q\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
given by
\lambda_{0}={\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}_{Q_{0}}
,
\lambda_{1}=\{(e^{\prime},e^{\prime})\colon e^{\prime}\in Q_{1}^{\prime}\}
is a morphism in the sense of Section
5.4
, so Definition
5.10
gives a Lie algebra morphism
\Omega^{\rm pl}\colon\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}\big)\rightarrow\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}\big)
, where
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}
are the ‘projective linear’ moduli stacks for
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
,
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
.

Let
\mu^{\prime}
be a slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
, and
\mu=\mu^{\prime}\circ\lambda_{*}
the associated slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. Let
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
, and
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}=\lambda_{*}(\boldsymbol{d})\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}^{\prime}}\setminus\{0\}
(in fact
\boldsymbol{d}=\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}
). As the factors
\prod_{v}\boldsymbol{d}(v)!
,
\prod_{v^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}(v^{\prime})!
in (
5.12
) are equal, Proposition
6.2
implies that the classes
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}
defined in Proposition
6.1
satisfy

\Omega^{\rm pl}\bigl(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}\bigr)=\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})\big]_{\rm inv}.

(6.14)

Proposition 6.6
.

In Definition
6.5
, suppose also that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
. Then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm st}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})
, so
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})
are smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-schemes by Proposition
5.7
, and

\Omega^{\rm pl}\bigl(\iota_{*}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm fund}\big)\bigr)=\iota^{\prime}_{*}\bigl(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})\big]_{\rm fund}\bigr),

(6.15)

where
\iota\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\hookrightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
,
\iota^{\prime}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})\hookrightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}
are the inclusions.

Proof.

We have
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}=[R_{\boldsymbol{d}}/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}]
,
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}=[R^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}]
, where
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}=\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}
, and
R_{\boldsymbol{d}}=R^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}\oplus R_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\prime\prime}
, where
R^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}
is the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space of edge maps
\rho_{e}
coming from
e\in Q_{1}^{\prime}\subset Q_{1}
, and
R_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\prime\prime}
the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-vector space of edge maps
\rho_{e}
coming from
e\in Q_{1}\setminus Q_{1}^{\prime}
. By (
5.11
), the vector bundle
G_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
in Definitions
2.11
and
5.10
is that associated to
R_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\prime\prime}
with its
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}
-action. Also
\sigma^{\rm pl}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}
is induced by the projection
R_{\boldsymbol{d}}\rightarrow R^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}
and the identity
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}\rightarrow\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}
.

There is a natural morphism
\iota^{\rm pl}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
induced by the inclusion
R^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow R_{\boldsymbol{d}}
and the identity
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}
, which embeds
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}
as a closed substack of
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
. It has
\sigma^{\rm pl}\circ\iota^{\rm pl}={\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}
. There is a natural transverse section
s
of the vector bundle
G^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
mapping an object
((V_{v})_{v\in Q_{0}},(\rho_{e})_{e\in Q_{1}})
to
(\rho_{e})_{e\in Q_{1}\setminus Q_{1}^{\prime}}
in
G^{\rm pl}
. The zero locus
s^{-1}(0)
in
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
is the substack
\iota^{\rm pl}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}\big)
.

Now
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\cap s^{-1}(0)=\iota^{\rm pl}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm st}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})\big)
and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\cap s^{-1}(0)=\iota^{\rm pl}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})\big)
. Hence
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
implies that

\iota^{\rm pl}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm st}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})\big)=\iota^{\rm pl}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})\big)
, and thus that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm st}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})
, as
\iota^{\rm pl}
is an embedding.

By Definition
2.14
,
\Omega^{\rm pl}
on
H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
is the composition

\textstyle{H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cap c_{\rm top}(G^{\rm pl})}
\textstyle{H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{*}^{\rm pl}}
\textstyle{H_{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm pl}}\big).}

(6.16)

This acts on
\iota_{*}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm fund}\big)
by

\textstyle{\iota_{*}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm fund}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle\cap c_{\rm top}(G^{\rm pl})
\textstyle{{\begin{subarray}{c}\iota_{*}([{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\!\cap\!s^{-1}(0)]_{\rm fund})\\
=\iota_{*}([\iota^{\rm pl}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime}))]_{\rm fund})\\
=\iota^{\rm pl}_{*}(\iota^{\prime}_{*}([{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})]_{\rm fund}))\end{subarray}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{*}^{\rm pl}}
\textstyle{\iota^{\prime}_{*}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})\big]_{\rm fund}\big).}

Here the first step holds as
\cap s^{-1}(0)
and
\cap c_{\rm top}\big(G^{\rm pl}\big)
have the same effect in homology, since
s
is transverse, and
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\cap s^{-1}(0)=\iota^{\rm pl}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})\big)
, and the second step holds as
\sigma^{\rm pl}\circ\iota^{\rm pl}={\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}
. Equation (
6.15
) follows. ∎

Corollary 6.7
.

In Proposition
6.6
, suppose also that
\boldsymbol{d}
is binary. Then if either
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime})
is a tree in
Q^{\prime}
, or
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime})
is disconnected, then

\Omega^{\rm pl}\bigl(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}\bigr)=\Omega^{\rm pl}\bigl(\iota_{*}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm fund}\big)\bigr).

(6.17)

Proof.

If
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime})
is a tree then

\Omega^{\rm pl}\bigl(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}\bigr)=\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})\big]_{\rm inv}=\iota^{\prime}_{*}\bigl(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})\big]_{\rm fund}\bigr)=\Omega^{\rm pl}\bigl(\iota_{*}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm fund}\big)\bigr),

using (
6.14
) in the first step, Proposition
6.4
in the second, and (
6.15
) in the third, proving (
6.17
).

If
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime})
is disconnected then
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm st}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})=\varnothing
, as every object in class
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}
in
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
is the direct sum of nonzero objects from each component of
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime})
, and so cannot be
\mu^{\prime}
-stable. Thus
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})=\varnothing
as
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm st}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})
by Proposition
6.6
, so
[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})]_{\rm fund}=0
. Let
\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}
be an increasing slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}^{\prime}
. Then
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})\big]_{\rm inv}
is given by (
6.6
) with
\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}
,
\mu^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}
in place of
\tilde{\mu}
,
\mu
,
\boldsymbol{d}
. For each term on the right-hand side of (
6.6
) from
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{1}=\delta_{v_{1}^{\prime}},\dots,\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{n}=\delta_{v_{n}^{\prime}}
, there exists unique
1\leqslant\penalty k<n
such that
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
lie in one component of
\mathop{\rm supp}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}
, and
v_{k+1}
in a different component. Then the nested Lie bracket in this term

\bigl[\bigl[\cdots\bigl[1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}^{\rm pl})},1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{2}}^{\rm pl})}\bigr],\dots 1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{k}}^{\rm pl})}\bigr],1_{H_{0}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{k+1}}^{\rm pl})}\bigr]=0,

as the outer Lie bracket is of the form
[A,B]
, where
A
,
B
are supported on different connected components of
\boldsymbol{d}
, and their Lie bracket is zero. Hence
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}(\mu^{\prime})\big]_{\rm inv}=\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\prime{\rm ss}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}}(\mu^{\prime})\big]_{\rm fund}=0
, so (
6.17
) follows from (
6.14
)–(
6.15
).
∎

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},h,t)
be a quiver. For each
e\in Q_{1}
, let
Q^{\prime}_{e}
be
Q
with edge
e
deleted, and
\lambda_{e}\colon Q\rightarrow Q_{e}^{\prime}
,
\Omega_{e}^{\rm pl}\colon\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)\rightarrow\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d},e}^{\prime{\rm pl}}\big)
be the morphisms in Definition
6.5
, where
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d},e}^{\prime{\rm pl}}
is the moduli stack of
(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})
in
\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q^{\prime}_{e}
with
{\mathbin{\bf dim}\kern 1.00006pt}(\boldsymbol{V},\boldsymbol{\rho})=\boldsymbol{d}
. Suppose
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
is a binary dimension vector with connected support and
\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})\leqslant\penalty 0
. Then the following is injective:

\bigoplus_{\text{$e$ edge in $\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})$}}\Omega_{e}^{\rm pl}\colon\ \check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\text{$e$ edge in $\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})$}}\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d},e}^{\prime{\rm pl}}\big).

(6.18)

Proof.

As
\boldsymbol{d}
is binary, we have

\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}=\prod_{v\in Q_{0}\colon\boldsymbol{d}(v)=1}{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}\cong{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}^{|\boldsymbol{d}|}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}\cong{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}^{|\boldsymbol{d}|-1}.

Since
H^{*}([*/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}])\cong R[c]
for
c
a formal variable of degree 2, we see that

H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}})=H^{*}([R_{\boldsymbol{d}}/\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}])\cong H^{*}([*/\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}])\cong R[c_{v}\colon v\in Q_{0},\,\boldsymbol{d}(v)=1].

The projection
\Pi^{\rm pl}\colon{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
has pullback
\big(\Pi^{\rm pl}\big)^{*}\colon H^{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)\rightarrow H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}})
, which is injective. This realizes
H^{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
as the subalgebra

H^{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)\cong\langle c_{w}-c_{v}\colon v\neq w,\,v,w\in Q_{0},\,\boldsymbol{d}(v)=\boldsymbol{d}(w)=1\rangle\subset R[c_{v}\colon v\in Q_{0},\,\boldsymbol{d}(v)=1]

generated by differences
c_{w}-c_{v}
. Since
\boldsymbol{d}
has connected support, any such
c_{w}-c_{v}
is a finite sum of
\pm(c_{w^{\prime}}-c_{v^{\prime}})
, for
\overset{v^{\prime}}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e}}}\,\overset{w^{\prime}}{\bullet}
an edge in
\mathop{\rm supp}(d)
. Hence

H^{*}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)\cong\big\langle c_{w}-c_{v}\colon\text{$\overset{v}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e}}}\,\overset{w}{\bullet}$ an edge in $\mathop{\rm supp}(d)$}\big\rangle\subset R[c_{v}\colon v\in Q_{0},\,\boldsymbol{d}(v)=1].

(6.19)

Suppose
\eta\!\in\!\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)=H_{2-2\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
lies in the kernel of (
6.18
). Let
\zeta\!\in\!H^{2-2\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
. By (
6.19
), as
\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})\leqslant\penalty 0
we may write

\zeta=\sum_{\text{$\overset{v}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e}}}\,\overset{w}{\bullet}$ edge in $\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})$}}\zeta_{e}\cup(c_{w}-c_{v}),

(6.20)

with
\zeta_{e}\in H^{-2\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
. Then

\displaystyle\zeta\cdot\eta

\displaystyle=\sum_{\text{$\overset{v}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e}}}\,\overset{w}{\bullet}$ edge in $\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})$}}\bigl(\zeta_{e}\cup(c_{w}-c_{v})\bigr)\cdot\eta

\displaystyle=\sum_{\text{$\overset{v}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e}}}\,\overset{w}{\bullet}$ edge in $\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})$}}\big(\big(\sigma^{\rm pl}_{e}\big)^{*}\big)^{-1}(\zeta_{e})\cdot\Omega^{\rm pl}_{e}(\eta)=0,

using (
6.20
) in the first step, and the definition of
\Omega^{\rm pl}_{e}
in Definition
2.14
with
c_{\rm top}\big(G_{e}^{\rm pl}\big)=c_{w}-c_{v}
in the second, and
\eta
in the kernel of (
6.18
) in the third. Hence
\zeta\cdot\eta=0
for all
\zeta\in H^{2-2\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
, so
\eta=0
, and (
6.18
) is injective.
∎

Proposition 6.9
.

The classes
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}
in
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
defined in Proposition
6.1
satisfy Theorem
5.8
(i)
when
\boldsymbol{d}
is binary.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{d}
be a binary dimension vector, and
\mu
a slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
such that
{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm st}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)
. We must prove that

\iota_{*}\bigl(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)\big]_{\rm fund}\bigr)=\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}\qquad\text{in}\quad H_{2-2\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}_{\boldsymbol{d}}\big).

(6.21)

If
\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})>1
this is automatic, as both sides lie in
H_{<0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}_{\boldsymbol{d}}\big)=0
. If
\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})=1
, then
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})
is a quiver with
n
vertices and
n-1
edges by (
5.3
), so either
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})
is a tree, when (
6.21
) holds by Proposition
6.4
, or
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})
is disconnected, when both sides of (
6.21
) are zero by the proof of Corollary
6.7
.

Suppose by induction on
k=0,1,\dots
that (
6.21
) holds when
\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})\geqslant 1-k
. The first step
k=0
holds from above. For the inductive step, suppose the inductive hypothesis holds for some
k\geqslant 0
, and that
\boldsymbol{d}
has
\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})=1-(k+1)\leqslant\penalty 0
. Using the notation of Proposition
6.8
, we have

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\text{$e$ edge in $\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})$}}\Omega_{e}^{\rm pl}\bigl(\iota_{*}([{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm ss}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\mu)]_{\rm fund})\bigr)=\bigoplus_{\text{$e$ edge in $\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})$}}\iota_{e}^{*}\bigl([{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{e}}^{\rm ss}(\mu^{\prime}_{e})]_{\rm fund}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\bigoplus_{\text{$e$ edge in $\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})$}}[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{e}}^{\rm ss}(\mu^{\prime}_{e})]_{\rm inv}=\bigoplus_{\text{$e$ edge in $\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})$}}\Omega_{e}^{\rm pl}\bigl([{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)]_{\rm inv}\bigr),

where the first step uses Proposition
6.6
, the second the inductive hypothesis for
Q_{e}^{\prime}
, noting that
\chi_{Q^{\prime}_{e}}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})=1-k
as
Q_{e}^{\prime}
has one fewer edge in
\mathop{\rm supp}(\boldsymbol{d})
, and the third Proposition
6.2
for
\lambda_{e}\colon Q\rightarrow Q_{e}^{\prime}
. As (
6.18
) is injective by Proposition
6.8
, this implies (
6.21
), and the proposition follows by induction.
∎

6.5
Proof of Theorem
5.8
(i) when
\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{d}}
are generic

Consider the following situation:

Definition 6.10
.

Let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},h,t)
be a quiver, and
\boldsymbol{d}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{Q_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
. Define a quiver
\tilde{Q}=\big(\tilde{Q}_{0},\tilde{Q}_{1},\tilde{h},\tilde{t}\big)
as follows: for each vertex
v\in Q_{0}
there are
\boldsymbol{d}(v)
vertices labelled by pairs
(v,i)
where
v\in Q_{0}
and
i=1,\dots,\boldsymbol{d}(v)
. For each edge
\overset{v}{\bullet}\,{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{e}}}\,\overset{w}{\bullet}
in
Q
there is an edge from
(v,i)
to
(w,j)
in
\tilde{Q}
for all
i=1,\dots,\boldsymbol{d}(v)
and
j=1,\dots,\boldsymbol{d}(w)
. Explicitly we set

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{0}=\bigl\{(v,i)\colon v\in Q_{0},\,i=1,\dots,\boldsymbol{d}(v)\bigr\},\qquad

\displaystyle\tilde{h}\colon\ (e,i,j)\mapsto(h(e),i),

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{1}=\bigl\{(e,i,j)\in Q_{1}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{2}\colon 1\leqslant\penalty i\leqslant\penalty\boldsymbol{d}(h(e)),\,1\!\leqslant\penalty j\leqslant\penalty\boldsymbol{d}(t(e))\bigr\},\qquad

\displaystyle\tilde{t}\colon(e,i,j)\mapsto(t(e),j).

This is illustrated in the next diagram, with
\boldsymbol{d}=(2,3)
:

\textstyle{Q}
\textstyle{\tilde{Q}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{v}
\textstyle{2}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{w}
\textstyle{3}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{(w,2)}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{(w,1)}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{(v,2)}
\textstyle{(v,1)}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{(w,3)}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}

Define
\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{N}}}^{\tilde{Q}_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
by
\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}(v,i)=1
for all
(v,i)\in\tilde{Q}_{0}
.

Define a morphism
\lambda\colon\tilde{Q}\rightarrow Q
in the sense of Section
5.4
by
\lambda_{0}\colon(v,i)\mapsto v
and
\lambda_{1}=\bigl\{((e,i,j),e)\colon(e,i,j)\in\tilde{Q}_{1}\bigr\}
. Then Definition
5.9
(i)–(iii) hold, so we have a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear exact functor
\Sigma_{\lambda}\colon\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\tilde{Q}}\rightarrow\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
inducing morphisms of moduli stacks
\sigma_{\lambda}\colon{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}
and
\sigma_{\lambda}^{\rm pl}\colon{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}^{\rm pl}
for
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\tilde{Q}}
,
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
. We have
\lambda_{*}\big(\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}\big)=\boldsymbol{d}
, so
\sigma_{\lambda}
,
\sigma_{\lambda}^{\rm pl}
map
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}
and
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
.

Explicitly, as in Definition
5.2
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}=[R_{\boldsymbol{d}}/\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}],\qquad\text{where}\quad R_{\boldsymbol{d}}=\prod_{e\in Q_{1}}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits\big({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{\boldsymbol{d}(t(e))},{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{\boldsymbol{d}(h(e))}\big),

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}=\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits(\boldsymbol{d}(v),{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}),\qquad{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}=[R_{\boldsymbol{d}}/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}],\qquad\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}=\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m},

\displaystyle{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}=\big[\tilde{R}_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}/\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}\big],\qquad\text{where}\quad\tilde{R}_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}=\prod_{e\in Q_{1},\,i=1,\dots,\boldsymbol{d}(t(e)),\,j=1,\dots,\boldsymbol{d}(h(e))}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits({\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}},{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}),

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}=\prod_{v\in Q_{0},\;i=1,\dots,\boldsymbol{d}(v)}{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m},\qquad{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}^{\rm pl}=\big[\tilde{R}_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}\big],\qquad\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}=\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}/{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}.

The morphisms
\sigma_{\lambda}\colon{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}
and
\sigma_{\lambda}^{\rm pl}\colon{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
are induced by the obvious map
\lambda_{*}\colon\tilde{R}_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}\rightarrow R_{\boldsymbol{d}}
, which is an
isomorphism
in this case, and by morphisms
\lambda_{*}\colon\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}\rightarrow\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}
and
\lambda_{*}^{\rm pl}\colon\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}\rightarrow\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}
, which are
inclusions of maximal tori
in this case. That is, we have
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}=[V/G]
and
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}=[V/H]
where
H\subseteq G
is the maximal torus.

Next let
\mu
be a slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}Q}
such that
\mu
,
\boldsymbol{d}
are generic in the sense of Definition
6.3
, and let
\tilde{\mu}=\mu\circ\lambda_{*}
be the associated slope function on
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\tilde{Q}}
. Then
\tilde{\mu}
,
\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}
are also generic for
\mathop{\text{\rm mod-}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\tilde{Q}}
, so
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm st}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)
and
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm st}(\tilde{\mu})={\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})
. Proposition
6.2
gives

\Omega^{\rm pl}\bigl(\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})\big]_{\rm inv}\bigr)=\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)!\cdot\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv},

(6.22)

and as
\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}
is binary with
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm st}(\tilde{\mu})={\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})
, Proposition
6.9
gives

\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})\big]_{\rm inv}=\tilde{\iota}_{*}\bigl(\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})\big]_{\rm fund}\bigr).

(6.23)

The reason we suppose
\mu
,
\boldsymbol{d}
generic in this section is that otherwise
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm st}(\mu)={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)
does not imply that
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm st}(\tilde{\mu})={\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})
, and if
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm st}(\tilde{\mu})\neq{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})
then
\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})\big]_{\rm fund}
in (
6.23
) is not defined.

We will use the following result of Martin
[
75
, Theorem B]
. It can also be written in algebraic geometry in terms of smooth GIT quotients.

Theorem 6.11
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a symplectic
2n
-manifold, with a Hamiltonian action of a compact Lie group
G
with moment map
\mu_{G}\colon X\rightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
, where
{\mathfrak{g}}
is the Lie algebra of
G
. Let
T\subset G
be a maximal torus with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
, so
\mu_{G}
induces a moment map
\mu_{T}\colon X\rightarrow\mathfrak{t^{*}}
. Suppose
\mu_{G}^{-1}(0)
and
\mu_{T}^{-1}(0)
are compact, with free
G
- and
T
-actions, so the quotients
X/\!/G=\mu_{G}^{-1}(0)/G
and
X/\!/T=\mu_{T}^{-1}(0)/T
are compact symplectic manifolds. Also
Y=\mu_{G}^{-1}(0)/T
is a compact manifold, with projections
\pi\colon Y\rightarrow X/\!/G
,
i\colon Y\rightarrow X/\!/T
.

Write
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
for the
T
-invariant splitting, and
E\rightarrow X/\!/T
for the complex vector bundle
E=\big(\mu_{T}^{-1}(0)\times\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\big)/T
associated to the complex representation of
T
on
\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
. Then for all classes
\eta\in H^{2n-2\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits G}(X/\!/G)
,
\zeta\in H^{2n-2\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits G}(X/\!/T)
with
\pi^{*}(\eta)=i^{*}(\zeta)
in
H^{2n-2\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits G}(Y)
, we have

\int_{X/\!/G}\eta=\frac{1}{|W|}\int_{X/\!/T}\zeta\cup c_{\rm top}(E),

where
W
is the Weyl group of
G
.

Proposition 6.12
.

The classes
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}
in
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
defined in Proposition
6.1
satisfy Theorem
5.8
(i)
if
\mu,\boldsymbol{d}
are generic.

Proof.

Use the notation of Definition
6.10
. We apply Theorem
6.11
with
X=R_{\boldsymbol{d}}
, and
G=\bigl(\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}{\mathbin{\rm U}}(\boldsymbol{d}(v))\bigr)/{\mathbin{\rm U}}(1)
, which is the maximal compact subgroup of
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}
, and acts on
R_{\boldsymbol{d}}
via the
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}
-action preserving a Euclidean Kähler form
\omega
on
R_{\boldsymbol{d}}
. By the relationship between GIT quotients and symplectic quotients in Kirwan
[
65
]
, there is a moment map
\mu_{G}\colon X\rightarrow{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
such that
\mu_{G}^{-1}(0)/G
is the GIT quotient
R_{d}/\!/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}={\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)
, so as
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)
is a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme we see that
\mu_{G}^{-1}(0)
is compact with a free
G
-action.

We take the maximal torus
T\subset G
to be
T=\bigl(\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}{\mathbin{\rm U}}(1)^{\boldsymbol{d}(v)}\bigr)/{\mathbin{\rm U}}(1)\cong{\mathbin{\rm U}}(1)^{|\boldsymbol{d}|-1}
. This is the maximal compact subgroup of
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}\cong{\mathbin{\mathbb{G}}}_{m}^{|\boldsymbol{d}|-1}
under the inclusion
\lambda_{*}^{\rm pl}\colon\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}\rightarrow\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\boldsymbol{d}}
as an algebraic maximal torus. Then the isomorphism
\lambda_{*}\colon\tilde{R}_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}\rightarrow R_{\boldsymbol{d}}
and the fact that
\tilde{\mu}=\mu\circ\lambda_{*}
implies that
\mu_{T}^{-1}(0)/T
is the GIT quotient
\tilde{R}_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}/\!/\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{\smash{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}}={\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})
, so as
{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})
is a smooth projective
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-scheme we see that
\mu_{T}^{-1}(0)
is compact with a free
T
-action.

Let
\theta\in H^{2n-2\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits G}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
, and set
\eta=\iota^{*}(\theta)
in
H^{2n-2\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits G}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big)
and
\zeta=\tilde{\iota}^{*}\circ\big(\sigma_{\lambda}^{\rm pl}\big)^{*}(\theta)
in
H^{2n-2\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits G}\big({\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})\big)
. Since
\eta,\zeta
are pullbacks of the same class
\theta\in H^{*}\big(\big[X/G^{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}\big]\big)
, we see that
\pi^{*}(\eta)=i^{*}(\zeta)
in
H^{2n-2\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits G}(Y)
. Hence as the Weyl group of
G
is
\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}S_{\boldsymbol{d}(v)}
, Theorem
6.11
gives

\int_{{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)}\iota^{*}(\theta)=\frac{1}{\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)!}\int_{{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 5.7361pt\widetilde{\kern-5.7361pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.27773pt}\kern 3.27773pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})}\tilde{\iota}^{*}\circ\big(\sigma_{\lambda}^{\rm pl}\big)^{*}(\theta)\cup c_{\rm top}(E).

We may rewrite this as

\theta\cdot\bigg(\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)!\cdot\iota_{*}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm fund}\big)\bigg)=\theta\cdot\bigl(\big(\sigma_{\lambda}^{\rm pl}\big)_{*}\bigl(\tilde{\iota}_{*}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})\big]_{\rm fund}\big)\cap c_{\rm top}(E)\bigr)\bigr).

As this holds for all
\theta\in H^{2n-2\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits G}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
, where
2n-2\mathop{\rm dim}\nolimits G=2-\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})
is the dimension of
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm fund}
, we see that in
H_{2-\chi_{Q}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d})}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
we have

\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)!\cdot\iota_{*}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm fund}\big)=\big(\sigma_{\lambda}^{\rm pl}\big)_{*}\circ\tilde{\iota}_{*}\bigl(\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})\big]_{\rm fund}\cap c_{\rm top}(E)\bigr).

(6.24)

Now one can show from the definitions that the vector bundle
E
over
X/\!/T={\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})
in Theorem
6.11
is isomorphic to
\tilde{\iota}^{*}\big(G^{\rm pl}\big)
, where
G^{\rm pl}\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}
is defined as in Definition
2.11
(c), (v) from the vector bundle
F\rightarrow{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}\times{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}
in (
5.10
). Thus as in (
6.16
), the right-hand side of (
6.24
) is
\Omega^{\rm pl}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})\big]_{\rm fund}\big)
, giving

\prod_{v\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{d}(v)!\cdot\iota_{*}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm fund}\big)=\Omega^{\rm pl}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\smash{\kern 6.99997pt\widetilde{\kern-6.99997pt\mathcal{M}\kern-3.99994pt}\kern 3.99994pt}}}_{\boldsymbol{\tilde{d}}}^{\rm ss}(\tilde{\mu})\big]_{\rm fund}\big).

(6.25)

Comparing (
6.22
), (
6.23
) and (
6.25
) gives
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}=\iota_{*}\big(\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm fund}\big)
, as we want.
∎

6.6
Proof of Theorem
5.8
(i) in the general case

The next proposition completes the proof of Theorem
5.8
.

Proposition 6.13
.

The classes
\big[{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm ss}(\mu)\big]_{\rm inv}
in
\check{H}_{0}\big({\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\boldsymbol{d}}^{\rm pl}\big)
defined in Proposition
6.1
satisfy Theorem
5.8
(i)
for all
\boldsymbol{d}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
k=0,1\dots
, with inductive hypothesis that the
